A retained ordinary or specialization body may register a referenced specialization before body replay only after its template resolves uniquely, its retained declaration and body source match, each argument remaps to a unique fresh type with the same shape, and its fingerprint is re-derived under the current provider, constraint and build context. Registration uses the normal bounded instance worklist; the callee body is independently checked or replayed. Missing, damaged or ambiguous recipes, synthesized-lambda references and catalog references absent from the fresh resolved catalog remain conservative misses; resolved aggregate argument descriptors include transitive provider identity, and catalog ordinals do not define nominal identity; a self-recursive specialization remaps to its own fresh instance. Whole-graph effect and mutation propagation always reruns from the combined fresh and replayed trees. `--no-cache` performs no specialization reads or writes.
- [SEMANTIC-CACHE-3] Cached source spans and node/statement identities must belong to unchanged mapped intervals and their function's tables; invalid relocation falls back instead of trusting stale references. Retained ordinary bodies carry path, interval and exact-byte digest proofs for referenced original files, so nonlocal default-expression locations can relocate when those files remain unchanged. Parser spans extending at most three bytes beyond the declaration region relocate only after those trailing bytes compare equal. Missing or ambiguous paths, changed external bytes and unproven spans remain misses; declaration, dependency, type-shape and effect guards still apply.

- [SEMANTIC-CACHE-4] Reusing a caller body never reuses transitive effect verdicts; a callee's new effect rejects an unchanged effect-bounded caller.
- [SEMANTIC-CACHE-5] Failed cache publication releases only its pending snapshot; the previous successful function queries remain reusable after OutOfMemory.
- [SEMANTIC-CACHE-6] Project queries include module paths, import/export text, signatures and type catalogs, preserving fresh/cached diagnostics and execution through source relocation and changed module interfaces.
- [CALLBACK-1] Noncapturing function values have exact parameter/result signatures and effect ceilings, defaulting to unbounded when omitted, including parameter-dependent signatures in generic helpers. An implementation callable is assignable to a destination callable when its parameter count and types and its result type are exactly equal and its declared effect ceiling is a subset of the destination's; the reverse direction rejects. A call through a stored callback uses the destination ceiling. Creation stores an immediate function identity; invocation validates the target and preserves ordinary ownership/result obligations. Callback parameters and results are ordinary copied values; captured environments are rejected.

- [CALLBACK-EFFECTS-1] A callback type with no `effects(...)` ceiling is unbounded, and an unannotated free function is assignable to any callback slot; an explicit narrow ceiling still rejects a function value whose declared ceiling is wider, and a call through a stored callback uses the destination ceiling.
- [CALLBACK-SLOT-1] An unannotated function used as a value takes the effect ceiling of the callback slot it fills: a parameter known at the call, and equally a generic callee's callback parameter once its type arguments are inferred from the checked arguments (`sort.sort_by_key(xs, key)` with `key` unannotated). The function's inferred transitive body effects must fit that ceiling: a function value whose target has no clause of its own and whose body reaches an effect the slot forbids — `managed_allocation` included, so an allocating helper no longer fills an `effects()` slot a `noalloc` caller invokes — is `E_EFFECT_BOUND` at the value, naming the target, the slot type, the effect and its witness path, so no effect can reach a bounded caller through a callback. `mutation` is the mutation analysis's, an unbounded slot accepts any body, and a sort key that builds a `str` still fits because the key slot admits allocation (LAMBDA-KEY-INFER-1).

- [DEAD-RECORD-WRITE-1] Plain record values copied into an explicit `mut` local are independent writable snapshots, including copies read from collections. Ordinary parameters and loop bindings are readonly and reject field writes with `E_IMMUTABLE`; declared `mut` parameters update caller storage. A deliberately mutable local copy remains legal even when its final value is unused.

- [SEMANTIC-CALLBACK-1] Function-name values may infer callback schemas while checking bodies. Supported snapshots reconstruct these types from current declarations and require exact transitive shapes before replay; unsupported contexts remain fresh. Editing a callback implementation rechecks that implementation while an unchanged caller may reuse its tree. Fresh and changed-source callback execution retain identical signatures and outcomes.

- [STANDARD-TIME-RANDOM-1] Standard durations and Unix nanosecond instants use checked signed arithmetic that reports overflow, reversal and unrepresentable spans as Result failures instead of wrapping or trapping; seeded SplitMix64 has stable word sequences, independent snapshots when a generator is copied, caller-state advancement through `mut` parameters, and inclusive integer sampling that covers the whole signed range without modulo bias.
- [STANDARD-TIME-RANDOM-2] Standard clock and entropy calls require explicit capabilities and keep the unavailable, denied and failed provider categories apart in their reported messages; a later failed read leaves earlier results intact and host allocation failure aborts normally.
- [STANDARD-TIME-RANDOM-3] Standard provider wrappers preserve source effect ceilings, so a caller declaring a narrower ceiling than clock or random plus managed allocation is rejected while checking; one entropy-seeded generator performs exactly one provider read and replays identically from its state word.

- [TYPE-ALIAS-SCHEMA-1] Exported transparent aliases retain their checked canonical target in public schemas and native interfaces, including scalar, array, chained and concrete generic targets.





## Standard library over managed types

- [STANDARD-MATH-1] Standard math classifies, rounds and roots binary64 values with IEEE behaviour preserved at zeros, infinities and not-a-number; sqrt reduces by exact powers of four and refines by Newton iteration, pow takes an integer exponent whose magnitude saturates by parity, and abs, gcd, lcm and isqrt report the values the signed range cannot hold as Result failures.
- [STANDARD-MATH-TRIG-1] Standard `math.sin` and `math.cos` accept one binary64 angle in radians and use native sine/cosine with range reduction across finite inputs. Sine preserves signed zero and subnormal inputs, cosine of either zero is one, and nonfinite inputs produce not-a-number under the ordinary float observation rules. The implementation uses compiler-owned intrinsics: raw internal spellings and forged stdlib paths are rejected with E_INTERNAL_NAME. Existing unary opcode values stay fixed; the appended trigonometric operations never enter the checked integer-conversion helper.
- [STANDARD-STRINGS-1] Standard strings add the classification, search, trimming, padding, case mapping and numeric conversion that builtin str lacks. `to_int` delegates to `int(text)` and `to_float` delegates to `float(text)`, so both wrappers use the pinned numeric grammar and parser; the float wrapper accepts surrounding whitespace, digit underscores, infinities and NaNs that its former guest parser rejected. `format_int` covers bases 2 to 36 including the most negative integer, and `format_float` rounds halves away from zero and rejects magnitudes it cannot render.
- [STANDARD-FORMAT-FLOAT-EXACT-1] `strings.format_float` rounds the exact binary value of its argument: when the scaled product `|value| * 10**decimals` rounds onto an exact half (for example `2.675 * 100.0 == 267.5`), the exact product error decides, so `format_float(2.675, 2)` is `"2.67"` as in C and Python. Only a value whose scaled product is exactly a half rounds away from zero (`format_float(0.125, 2)` is `"0.13"`), and a result that rounds to zero carries no sign (`format_float(-0.001, 2)` is `"0.00"`).
- [STANDARD-STRINGS-2] `strings.repeat(text, times)` doubles its copies instead of appending one copy at a time: the answer so far is appended to itself while its copy count stays within `times`, and one prefix of it tops up the rest, so building an answer of `n` octets copies at most about `3n` octets and takes a logarithmic number of concatenations, where the old loop copied the growing answer once per copy and was quadratic in `times`. An empty `text` or a zero count answers the empty text at once; a negative count is still `Error.Invalid`. Every answer is the same text.
- [STANDARD-SORT-1] Standard ordering is a stable merge over a copy that leaves its input unchanged, for integers, binary64 values, Unicode code point order on text, and any element type through an integer key callback. The concrete integer, float and text wrappers delegate to the same one-work-buffer engine as `sorted`, and the keyed form to the keyed sort (STANDARD-SORT-KEY-1). Text comparison reads stored Unicode scalars directly without allocating a byte encoding. Binary search, extrema and duplicate removal report absence through Option and keep first-occurrence order.
- [STANDARD-PATH-1] Standard path operations are pure text algebra with no filesystem authority: joining honours an absolute right side, basename and dirname agree with the trailing-separator convention, splitext ignores a leading dot, and normalize removes duplicate separators, single dots and resolvable parents while keeping unresolvable ones.
- [STANDARD-CODECS-1] Standard base64 emits canonical padding for both RFC 4648 alphabets and decodes only canonical input, rejecting a length that is not a multiple of four, a symbol outside the alphabet, padding before the final group and a final group with nonzero unused bits. Hexadecimal transcoding accepts either letter case and rejects odd lengths.
- [STANDARD-JSON-1] Standard JSON parses strict RFC 8259 text into a recursive union, rejecting comments, trailing commas, leading zeros, bare fractions and exponents, unescaped control characters, lone surrogates, duplicate object keys, trailing text and nesting past a fixed limit, and naming the byte offset in every message. Objects keep parsed key order, a number without fraction or exponent that fits the signed range stays exact, and rendering round-trips a parsed document without added whitespace.
- [STDLIB-ERROR-UNION-1] A standard module whose operations can fail exports an error union and returns `Result[T, Module.Error]` from every exported fallible operation instead of `Result[T, str]`; each case carries a `str` payload reachable through an exported `message(error)` helper, so callers match on category instead of the human text and adding a case is a breaking interface change. `json` and `strings` are converted; `math`, `time`, `random`, `base64` and `hex` still return `Result[T, str]`.
- [STANDARD-POOL-1] A standard pool hands out copyable generational handles into a reusable slot store. Removing a slot advances its generation so every earlier handle stops matching; a stale or out-of-range handle reports absence and no handle traps, released slots are reused before the store grows, and copies of a pool value refer to one shared store. A handle names a slot and a generation rather than a pool, so it must be used with the pool that issued it.
- [STANDARD-SORT-KEY-1] `sort.sort_by_key(values, key)` is a thin forwarder to `sorted(values, key=key)` (LIST-SORT-KEY-1), so it returns a new stable list and leaves its input unchanged. Each key is computed exactly once, in input order — earlier versions computed them in the order a recursive merge first asked for each one, so a key function that traps on several elements now raises the trap of the first such element in input order, as Python does. The extra memory is the result, one key word per element and the native sort's scratch, for lists of any length.

- [FUNCTION-IDENTITY-1] `same_function(left, right)` accepts compatible callable values and compares their process-local function identity without invoking them or contributing effects. Non-callables and incompatible signatures are rejected. Identity remains valid through stored, returned and indirect function values in native debug and release builds; it supplies no serialized address or persistent implementation digest and does not enable ordinary callable equality or hashing.
- [FUNCTION-DIGEST-1] `function_digest(callback)` returns a compiler-issued persistent identity for the actual callable and its reachable implementation, structural source schema and relevant compiler/runtime/toolchain/target/build context. Same-signature transitive edits invalidate identities; rooted unrelated implementations may retain them, and reverts recover the prior identity. Unknown indirect targets widen dependency coverage; unsupported external context or self-referential digest computation returns an empty identity and cannot authorize persistent reuse. Tests reaching identity queries execute afresh until their receipt keys bind complete finalized identity authority, including live batch membership; unrelated tests retain ordinary receipt reuse.
- [NEW-CORE-DURABLE-STORAGE-1] Durable filesystem operations require explicit native filesystem authority, reject unsupported stand-ins, preserve scoped/read-only grants, and resolve paths without following symlinks. Bounded reads require regular files and a complete size check. Atomic publication writes a same-directory exclusive temporary file, checks complete writes, synchronizes file contents, renames and synchronizes the parent; errors after rename report uncertain publication. An owned directory lock is exclusive across processes and releases on close or process exit. These operations provide the prerequisites for recoverable application journals without claiming an experimentally established power-loss guarantee. `list_directory` takes a 0..4096 entry bound, checks complete directory enumeration including read errors and refuses oversized results. Directory leases reject overlapping grants that resolve to distinct physical directories so storage fallback cannot escape ownership.
- [STANDARD-MEMO-1] The embedded Selka `memo` module retains completed allocation-only callback results for exact tagged integer, text and byte arguments. Each entry binds its actual callable, so replacing the cache's callback cannot reuse another computation's value. `create` accepts 0 through 65536 entries and reports invalid capacity through `Error.InvalidCapacity` and `message`; zero disables retention. Misses fill bounded slots and then replace the oldest insertion; hits preserve order, computation completes before insertion, and traps propagate. `Lookup` reports the result and reuse status, `count` and `capacity` expose the entry bound, and `clear` releases this cache's entries. Keys, returned managed results and copied caches keep independent value snapshots; equality-and-storable result constraints exclude callbacks, capabilities and owned resources. The limit bounds entry count rather than arbitrary payload bytes. This process-local library performs no persistent reuse or durable recovery.
- [STANDARD-HASHES-1] The embedded Selka `hashes.sha256(bytes)` computes standard SHA-256 lowercase hexadecimal digests in 64-byte input blocks with bounded working arrays and no whole-input padding copy, including empty inputs and padding across block boundaries.
- [STANDARD-CACHE-RECORD-1] The embedded Selka `cache_record` encodes string arrays as canonical JSON inside an exact versioned SHA-256 envelope. Decode rejects truncation, trailing bytes, noncanonical spellings, nonstring members and checksum mismatch. The checksum supplies integrity, not authentication; storage callers bound input reads before decoding.
- [STANDARD-PERSISTENT-MEMO-1] Selka `persistent_memo.call` persists completed byte snapshots for exact tagged `memo.Key` inputs and compiler-issued callable identities in an existing dedicated filesystem directory. It validates record identity, schema, key, canonical representation and checksum before reuse. Missing or malformed records are misses; provider failures remain errors. Zero entries forces execution without storage; unavailable callable authority and oversized results execute without receipts. Options bound entries to 0..256 and each entry's raw key plus result to 0..1048576 bytes. Directory ownership serializes publication and deterministic eviction; lowered limits trim retained entries, orphan temporary files are collected under ownership, and atomic publication errors never report success.
- [STANDARD-DURABLE-1] Selka `durable.run` accepts captured byte input, ordered byte chunks, initial byte state, a simple output filename and a pure chunk/state callback returning byte output and state. Bounds limit chunks to 1024 and input, cumulative chunks, cumulative output and each state to a selected 1..8388608 bytes. A compiler-qualified descriptor binds implementation, source schemas, captured inputs, ordered chunks, initial state, output and limits. Under an exclusive directory lease, each artifact is durably published before its digest enters the durably replaced journal. Recovery validates all committed artifacts and state links before skipping their computations; missing, corrupt or incompatible committed evidence fails closed. Uncommitted artifacts and private temporary files can be collected without deleting retained progress. Final output is atomically published from verified committed data; interruption or uncertain publication can be retried, and a published journal never substitutes for checking final bytes. Native qualification establishes process-crash recovery, not experimentally proven power-loss durability.


## Application library parity

- [STANDARD-INTEGER-EXTREMA-1] Standard math integer min, max and clamp compare exact values at every supported integer width without arithmetic overflow, including both ends of each signed and unsigned range.

## Ordinary JSON schema helpers




## Bytecode execution




- [NATIVE-TIMING-1] `run --timings` reports native execution nanoseconds on stderr, covering the guest process and the native build it needed but excluding source compilation. Timing preserves guest results, quotas and runtime diagnostics; duplicate options and unsupported commands reject it.


- [PHASE-TIMING-2] Opt-in project timing may additionally separate source/header load, syntax replay and parse, declarations/interfaces, source merge, dependency-key preparation, ordinary-body replay, symbolic templates, concrete-specialization replay, fresh body checking, effect/origin passes, warning derivation and semantic retention/durable-store I/O. These records are bounded, disabled by default, never affect cache keys, acceptance, diagnostics or work-report counts, and use `inclusive=false` for disjoint phases while only `total` is inclusive.

- [ERGONOMIC-INTEGRATION-1] Late exports, inferred generic calls and unit procedures compose across module boundaries, preserving cross-module effect and result obligations.
- [GENERIC-INFERENCE-1] Generic function calls may omit their entire type-argument list when checked call arguments and the expected result type uniquely determine every type and const length parameter. Matching follows direct variables, fixed arrays, nominal applications, and callback signatures; repeated occurrences must agree. Abstract callers retain distinct symbolic identities and declared requirements. Argument expressions are checked and evaluated once in their original order; ordinary type, effect, ownership, and structural checks remain in force. Constraints alone, defaults, and partial explicit lists do not infer missing arguments; ambiguity and conflicting inference report E_GENERIC_INFERENCE. Unmaterialized phantom type arguments require explicit specialization.
## Unit procedures

- [UNIT-1] The builtin `unit` is a distinct no-value type. Unit functions accept `return;` and implicit fallthrough; `main` still returns `i64`.
- [UNIT-2] Ordinary standalone expressions must have type `unit`; all other values require `_ =` or result handling, including generic bodies. VM calls preserve unit identity and ownership cleanup.
- [UNIT-3] Unit is described explicitly in public schemas and is rejected by data/wire boundaries and as an `extern def` parameter (a `-> unit` result is C `void`).
- [UNIT-4] Unit fallthrough and early return release owned parameters through the ordinary cleanup paths.
- [UNIT-5] Fallible values with unit success payloads retain all result obligations; a successful `try` yielding unit may appear as a standalone statement.
- [UNIT-6] Public unit-returning functions remain callable within language modules. Their schema uses `kind: unit`; copied VM/host/native ABI invocation rejects unit-containing signatures explicitly instead of interpreting unit as a scalar or capability.

## Managed tracing heap

- [GC-ROOTS-1] Managed collections and aggregate snapshots retain independent values across later mutations. Retained values, including intermediate call arguments and aggregate snapshots, root managed objects across allocation and stress collection.
- [GC-BOUNDARY-1] Managed list operations enforce element types, and an out-of-range read or write raises a located list-bounds trap.
- [GC-SOURCE-1] Managed local copies can be mutated through a `mut` helper without explicit allocator authority. Updating their source requires an explicit store-back; the original remains unchanged until that store.
- [GC-SOURCE-3] Managed results held as intermediate call arguments or inside ordinary record snapshots survive collection triggered by later arguments and callee allocations.
- [GC-SOURCE-4] Out-of-bounds managed reads and writes produce located R_LIST_BOUNDS traps and release execution roots and backing.
- [GC-SOURCE-6] Explicit `effects()` and `effects(mutation)` ceilings reject transitive managed allocation and list growth. Written `noalloc` instead warns on ownership-expanded Core allocation sites, including copy-on-write at a fixed-slot mutation ([FRONTEND-CORE-NOALLOC-1]).
- [GC-SOURCE-7] Managed element declarations reject owned resources and unchecked results, including such values nested in ordinary aggregates.
- [GC-GENERIC-3] Directly recursive value declarations without a finite construction remain rejected. Recursive records and unions with finite cases use the immutable boxes defined by RECURSIVE-VALUES-1.
- [GC-GENERIC-4] Source formatting preserves generic list declarations and contextual uses of list as a variant name.

## Managed standard library

- [GC-GENERIC-5] Managed list elements cannot contain allocator or provider capabilities, including nested records, arrays, and instantiated generic elements; declarations reject with E_LIST_CAPABILITY.
- [GC-CAPABILITY-1] Managed element declarations reject capability values and capabilities nested in aggregates, preserving the source authority boundary.


## Managed arenas


- [SURFACE-STMT-1] A statement ends at its newline, at the dedent that closes its suite, or at EOF; two statements written on one line are separated by `;`.
- [SURFACE-STMT-2] Postfix `?` propagates result errors and unwraps success with postfix precedence.
- [SURFACE-STMT-3] `None` and `()` both spell the unit type and unit value; a bare `return` before a newline or a dedent returns unit.
- [SURFACE-STMT-4] Calls and function parameters permit trailing commas; a delimiter continues an expression across newlines.
- [SURFACE-STMT-5] Parse-only declaration listing and module formatting preserve newline-terminated module, import, export, and type directives.
- [SURFACE-STMT-6] Project discovery recognizes newline-terminated directives and declarations while allowing multiline function signatures.

## Command-line authority

- [CAPABILITY-1] `Filesystem`, `Network`, `Clock`, `Entropy` and `Environment` are the canonical capability types. The removed `*_cap` spellings report `E_REMOVED_SPELLING` with the corresponding type; declared or imported types of the canonical name still win. Authority stays explicit: an entry point declares what it needs by parameter type, helpers receive grants as ordinary arguments, and no numeric cast or copy mints one.
- [CAPABILITY-2] `mylang run` and `run-project` grant every capability by default: filesystem reading and writing with no directory restriction, the clock, entropy and the whole process environment. `--restrict` switches the command to deny-by-default, and only then do `--allow-read=DIR` and `--allow-write=DIR` (each repeatable, up to eight directories), `--allow-clock`, `--allow-random`, `--allow-env` and `--allow-all` narrow anything; without `--restrict` they are accepted and narrow nothing. `--allow-random=SEED` pins the entropy seed in either mode, because it selects a stream rather than withholding one; an unseeded stream is seeded from the wall clock. `--allow-all` grants the clock, entropy, every environment name and read/write access to the working directory, and cannot be combined with another grant. A repeated flag, an empty-valued flag, and any of these options on a command that does not execute a program are usage errors.
- [CAPABILITY-3] Under `--restrict`, an entry point that names a capability the command line did not grant is rejected after checking and lowering but before the first guest instruction, with a message that says `--restrict` is active, names the flag that would grant the capability, and offers to drop `--restrict`; the status is 2. Without `--restrict` that refusal cannot happen. Grants for capabilities the entry point does not request provision providers that are never read.
- [CAPABILITY-4] The default filesystem grant is one direct root over the process working directory: a relative guest path resolves against it and an absolute path names itself, each operation applies the whole path at once and follows symlinks, and write authority is always present, so the default can never be narrower than a read grant. Under `--restrict`, directory grants are an ordered search path of scoped roots instead: a guest path must be relative with no empty, `.`, `..`, NUL or backslash component, it is tried against each granted root in order and the first success wins, traversal opens each component with symlink following disabled and final opens reject symlinks. Modes that change the tree — write, exclusive create, truncate, create directory and remove — consider only roots granted with `--allow-write`, which also grants reading that root, and a read-only grant rejects them before opening anything. Neither mode is a sandbox: the providers run inside the compiler process, the host filesystem tree is trusted, directory handles retain authority across renames, and directory scoping does not distinguish hard links to shared inodes.
- [CAPABILITY-5] The clock reports Unix-epoch nanoseconds from the host wall clock, with no monotonicity guarantee. Entropy is a reproducible pseudorandom stream, seeded by `--allow-random=SEED` when given and otherwise from the wall clock; it is not a cryptographic entropy source, and a program needing one must be embedded with its own provider. The environment exposes the host process environment, restricted to the `--allow-env=NAME,...` allowlist when one is given, reporting absent names as absent and denying names outside the list.
- [CAPABILITY-6] The default filesystem grant carries the process's own authority, so like C's `fopen` it opens whatever the process can open other than a directory: a FIFO, a character device such as `/dev/null`, or the `/dev/fd/N` pipe a shell's process substitution `<(...)` names, and a read waits for the FIFO's writer and reads to end of file; truncation still applies only to a regular file. Under `--restrict`, a granted directory serves regular files only, exactly as before: a FIFO or device inside it is refused with the ordinary denial, opened without waiting for a writer, and absolute paths such as `/dev/fd/N` remain outside every grant.

## Ordinary output

- [IO-1] `stream.print(values...)` writes each argument's Python rendering to the receiver stream's destination, separated by single spaces and followed by one newline. `int` and the fixed-width integers render in decimal, `bool` as `True`/`False`, `None` as `None`, `float` with a `.0` suffix when it has no fractional digits and `nan`/`inf`/`-inf` for the nonfinite values, `str` raw at the top level and `repr`-quoted inside a container, and `bytes` as a Python `b'...'` literal with `\n`, `\r`, `\t`, `\'`, `\\` and `\xNN` escapes. Lists render as `[a, b]`, insertion-ordered dictionaries as `{k: v}` and fixed arrays as `[a, b]`. Nesting deeper than 24 levels renders as `...`, so a cyclic managed graph still terminates. `stream.print()` writes only the newline.
- [IO-2] Records render as `Name(field=value, ...)` in declaration order, enum cases as `Name.case`, union cases as `Name.case(payload)` or `Name.case`, checked results as `Ok(value)`/`Err(value)` and optionals as `Some(value)`/`None`. Module qualification is stripped from the printed name. A record with a moved field prints `<moved>` for that field rather than reading it.
- [IO-3] `stream.print` accepts the keyword arguments `sep=` and `end=`, each an ordinary `str` expression that replaces the separator or the trailing newline. Positional arguments must precede both keywords, neither may be repeated, and any other keyword is rejected with `E_IO_ARGUMENT`. Printed values evaluate in source order, then `sep=`, then `end=`. Both keywords materialize the prelude `str` type on demand, so a program whose only text is a separator still checks, and a `stream.print` call without keywords needs no text schema at all.
- [IO-4] `stream.print` and `stream.write` contribute the `output` effect. `noio` withholds `filesystem`, `network` and `output` together, so a direct or transitive `stream.print` under `noio` is rejected with `E_EFFECT_BOUND` and a call witness; `effects(output)` permits it and `noalloc` does not constrain it. Bytecode validation independently recomputes the effect from the emitted instruction.
- [IO-5] `stream.print` is checked only on a receiver whose type is `Stream`; a bare `print`/`eprint` reports `E_OUTPUT_CAPABILITY` with the fix to add `out: Stream` and pass one from main, while a local binding or declared function named `print` remains an ordinary name. Capabilities, files, owned resources and callback values have no rendering and are rejected with `E_IO_ARGUMENT`. A host that supplies no output writer discards program output rather than failing, and a writer that refuses bytes traps with `R_OUTPUT`.
- [IO-7] `with` is one statement form with two resource kinds. `with arena() as name:` opens a managed region; any other expression must produce an open file handle and must name its binding. `open(fs, path, mode)` takes a `Filesystem` capability, a relative `str` path and a literal mode — `"r"`, `"w"`, `"a"` with an optional `"b"` or `"t"` — and returns `Result[File, str]` for binary modes or `Result[TextFile, str]` for text modes. Directly as the head of a `with` statement the call yields the handle itself and a failure traps with a located `R_FILE`, the way Python's `open` raises; `with open(...)? as f:` propagates the result instead, and every other position yields the ordinary result. A binary handle's `read`, `readline` and `readlines` produce `bytes`; a text handle's produce `str`, decoded as UTF-8. `f.read()` reads to end of file, `f.read(n)` reads at most `n` bytes, `f.readline()` keeps its terminator, and `f.readlines()` returns the lines with their terminators kept.
- [IO-8] `f.write(data)` accepts the handle's own element type, writes every byte by looping over the provider's partial progress, and returns the count. `f.flush()` exists for familiarity and is a no-op because writes are unbuffered. Mode `"w"` creates the file or truncates an existing one, `"a"` appends at the current end, and `"r"` neither creates nor truncates. Whole-file reads and writes are capped at 64 MiB.
- [IO-9] `read_file`, `read_text`, `write_file`, `write_text`, `listdir`, `mkdir` and `remove` each take the capability first and return `Result[T, str]`, whose error text names the operation, the path and a readable reason. `exists(fs, path)` returns `bool` rather than a result. A helper opens, uses and closes its resource within the call, so a failure leaves no handle behind.
- [IO-10] A directory granted without write authority rejects every writing mode — `w`, `a`, create, truncate, `mkdir` and `remove` — before opening anything, and the returned error says the mode needs a write grant.
- [IO-11] A file `with` scope closes its handle exactly once at every exit: falling off the end, `return`, `?` propagation, `break`, `continue`, an explicit `f.close()`, and a trap or host allocation failure unwinding through it. A method on a closed or stale handle traps with `R_FILE`; so does any provider failure during a method, since `f.read()` yields bytes rather than a result. The trap message carries the same readable reason as the `Result` error text.
- [IO-12] File operations contribute the `filesystem` effect, so `noio` rejects them with `E_EFFECT_BOUND`. `with` on a value that is not an arena or an open file is rejected with `E_WITH_RESOURCE`, which tells a `Result` operand to propagate with `open(...)?` or match first. A non-literal mode, an unsupported mode and an unknown handle method are rejected with `E_FILE_ARGUMENT`. Generic function bodies reject file operations. The QBE native backend runs the whole file surface too, over a native scoped-directory provider with the same request grammar, `Result` error text and located `R_FILE` traps; an open handle is one owned resource word that the ordinary local drop closes at every scope exit. A native executable grants every capability by default and `--restrict` switches it to deny-by-default with the same `--allow-*` flags, which `mylang build --run` forwards after `--`.
- [IO-13] A `class` value renders its own fields wherever it is nested — a union or option payload, a record or class field, a tuple or fixed-array element, a list or dictionary element — exactly as it renders at the top level. Every generated renderer receives the address of its value's slot, and a class slot holds the body pointer, so the class renderer loads that pointer before reading any field; no renderer reads a field at the slot itself.
- [IO-14] `stream.print` renders every finite `float` in its whole positional spelling, however long: the renderer's frame holds the longest finite double (the smallest subnormal's `0.` and 323 zeros, or the largest double's 309 integer digits), so `1.2345e300`, `-1.7976931348623157e308` and `5e-324` print digit for digit as `str(x)` spells them, where a value longer than 64 characters used to leave the line unprintable and trap `InternalError`.
- [IO-15] `print` and `write` are methods of any `Stream`-typed receiver, not only of a bare name: a `Stream` held in a class or record field prints and writes through the field expression (`sink.stream.write(data)`, `pair.first.stream.print(...)`) exactly as a bound local does, with the same `output` effect, return value and lowering. Such a receiver takes positional arguments only (`sep=`/`end=` still need the stream bound to a name), and any other method on it is `E_COLLECTION_METHOD` with a message naming `print` and `write` instead of the collection methods.
- [CLI-CACHE-DIR-1] `--cache-dir=DIR` (or, for the frontend caches, `MYLANG_FRONTEND_CACHE=DIR`) keeps every persistent cache a command writes under `DIR`, including the ones a command's own compiler children open: `mylang test` gives its `list-interfaces --contracts` child the same `--cache-dir` as its facts, check and build children, and the checked-body store and the `--explain-invalidation` baseline follow `MYLANG_FRONTEND_CACHE` exactly as the syntax cache does. With no directory named the caches keep their per-project `.mylang-cache` defaults. `--no-cache` leaves native objects in the default object cache, as before.
- [RUN-FLAGS-1] `mylang run` and `run-project` take every execution budget in both spellings, `--flag N` and `--flag=N`, for `--max-steps`, `--max-frames`, `--timeout` and `--max-memory`, and check the value exactly as the `=` spelling is checked (a missing, zero or non-numeric value is a usage failure). A rejected `run` or `run-project` command line, including `mylang run` with no file, prints the usage of those two commands, which lists each budget in both spellings, not the `mylang test` usage the general text opens with; a bare `mylang` still prints the general usage and exits 2.
- [RUN-FRAME-BUDGET-1] A `--max-frames` budget reserves 32 KiB of native stack per frame plus 8 MiB inside the guest's address space, which is its `--max-memory` ceiling plus 256 MiB and never more than the limit the process inherited (the compiler's own 8 GiB ceiling, `MYLANG_HOST_MEMORY_LIMIT`, or `ulimit -v`). `mylang run`, `run-project` and `test` refuse a budget that cannot fit before any build or guest starts, with exit 2 and `mylang <command>: a call-depth budget of N frames (--max-frames) reserves X of native stack (32 KiB per frame), but <guest> may map only Y: ...`, naming either the `--max-memory` that would hold it and the largest `--max-frames` that fits, or, when the inherited process limit is the cause, that limit and `MYLANG_HOST_MEMORY_LIMIT=0`. The runtime's own stack mapping reports the same facts instead of a bare `OutOfMemory` for a budget that slips past the check (for example a built program run with `MYLANG_OPTIONS`). The former "hard cap near 269,622 frames" was this inherited 8 GiB limit, not a size overflow. `R_FRAME_LIMIT` advises `--max-frames=N` together with a `--max-memory` that covers N x 32 KiB.
- [PROJECT-LIMITS-1] `mylang.json` may carry a `limits` object with a `run` and a `test` entry, each any of `max_steps` and `max_frames` (positive integers up to 4294967295) and `max_memory` (a byte count or a K/M/G size; zero removes the ceiling). `mylang run`, `run-project` and `test` read the configuration nearest the entry file and use these as defaults for the matching flag: a flag on the command line always wins, `MYLANG_MEMORY_LIMIT` outranks the file's `max_memory`, and the built-in defaults apply to whatever the file leaves out. The values go through the same checks as the flags, so a configured `max_frames` the memory cannot back is refused exactly as the flag is. A directory run leaves each program to its own configuration, and a missing or invalid configuration changes nothing here because it is reported where configuration errors already are. An unknown or duplicate key, an entry that is not an object, and a zero, non-integer or out-of-range budget are configuration failures.
- [CLI-INPUT-REFUSED-1] Every command that takes a source entry (`check`, `check-module`, `check-project`, `run`, `run-project`, `build`, `test`, `fmt`, `fmt-module`, `fix`, `lint`, `api`, `invoke`, `list-*`, `emit-*`, `dump-*` and the rest) probes it before any cache opens or any work starts. A missing path, a path the process may not read and a directory where a file is required are refused with `mylang <command>: cannot read '<path>': <reason>` on stderr (or the `diagnostic-v1` message form under `--diagnostics=json`), where the reason is `no such file or directory`, `permission denied` or `is a directory, not a file`, and exit 2 like every usage failure; `test` and `fix` accept a directory. `invoke` answers with its `native-invocation-v1` `setup-failure` whose `failure.diagnostics` carries the reason. A refused input creates no cache directory, and no command prints a Zig error-return trace for a host failure.
- [CAPABILITY-FIELD-METHOD-1] A capability held in a record field takes method syntax through the field expression exactly as a bound local does: `ctx.fs.read_text(path)`, `ctx.fs.open(path, "rb")` (also as a `with` head, where a direct `open` traps `R_FILE`), `ctx.fs.dir("data")`, `ctx.fs.read_only()`, `ctx.env.get`, `ctx.env.only`, `ctx.env.names` and `ctx.net.host(name)` check and lower to the nodes the local spelling builds, so effects, permissions, literal-argument rules, arity errors and `noio` bounds are unchanged. The same holds for any receiver expression, such as `make_fs().dir("x")` or `pair.first.fs`. `Clock`, `Entropy` and `Host` have no methods, and an unknown method on a capability receiver is `E_COLLECTION_METHOD` whose message names that receiver's own methods rather than the collection methods.
- [REQUIRED-PROJECT-IMPORT-POLICY-1] A `mylang.json` discovered by walking up from the entry source file to the filesystem root makes its `import_policy` required for ordinary `check`, `check-project`, `build`, `run`, `run-project`, project variants and native test preparation, evaluated before any cached success and never selected from the shell working directory. Missing or malformed configuration is a host failure (status 2); a policy violation is status 3 and language or import-cycle errors stay status 1. Policy and configuration identity participates in verdict caches and receipts so a stricter policy invalidates an earlier pass, import-cycle checking cannot be disabled, and projects without a configuration keep their prior behavior.
- [POLICY-IDENTITY-OWNED-1] The configured-policy identity that the successful-verdict caches key on is copied into memory each cache owns when it is published, and freed when the cache is dropped, so a warm `check-project` or `check-session` request under a `mylang.json` replays its stored verdict exactly as an unconfigured project does instead of keying on stale stack bytes. Every request first clears the identity, so a long-lived session whose project stops configuring one never keys on the previous request's configuration.
- [SELFHOST-BOOTSTRAP-1] Ordinary source installation and component-only builds use a native Zig bootstrap coordinator without Python. It verifies the pinned seed and immutable artifacts, hashes files with bounded streaming buffers, preserves a component's selected manifest when that component fails, and can rebuild/install a component with an already qualified seed when PATH contains no interpreter or external tools. Python is permitted only in test/development drivers.
- [SELFHOST-CACHE-PLAN-1] Core test receipt reachability uses the independently compiled Selka cache-plan component over an immutable complete call/function-reference graph. Each batch excludes all supplied roots and returns reached nonroot IDs sorted by digest then ID, preserving distinct equal-digest nodes. The host retains the original ordered root contributions, including repeats, and the existing u64 nonroot-count/digest encoding. Versioned frames, correlation identities, graph indices, output order and finite input/output/traversal bounds are validated; malformed, missing, corrupt, changed, failed or timed-out components fail explicitly. `MYLANG_CACHE_PLAN_MODE=reference` selects the host oracle, `shadow` compares exact batch answers, and `component` is the default without automatic fallback.
- [NEW-CORE-TEST-FACTS-1] Canonical pre-pass function digests and complete call/function-reference edges may be restored from independently addressable packed metadata only after validated raw discovery, current catalog, compiler, source and locator proofs agree. Stable logical identities distinguish equal-body declarations; ambiguous or unsupported metadata requests complete lowering. Missing or damaged records recompute facts, and missing deferred bodies request complete-lowering retry. Warm fact hits do not materialize unrelated raw instruction arrays; oversized edge lists use checked chunks.
- [NEW-CORE-TEST-GRAPH-1] Persisted test-key candidates belong to a complete graph generation with stable logical node identities, canonical bodies, outgoing sets, exact test envelopes and ordered roots. Immutable metadata chunks publish before their generation manifest; missing, damaged or inconsistent chunks discard prior authority. Changed nodes and edges are compared after logical remapping, and retained reverse/SCC indexes require the exact current ordered topology. Only the bound planner authorizes reusing an exact derived key. Shadow mode compares every eligible derived key with independent eager lowering and canonical traversal; global checking and final source/tool revalidation remain required. Current-invocation work appears once per provider group and is absent on whole-report replay.
- [SELFHOST-CACHE-PLAN-2] Incremental cache planning uses a bounded version-2 exchange while preserving version-1 batch semantics. Selka validates or rebuilds reverse indexes and recursive groups, propagates changed nodes to current callers, and reuses only unaffected candidate tests. Recursive groups share demand-driven closure work; topology changes rebuild groups, including cycle splitting. Reused tests omit closure vectors, sorting and key hashing; changed tests preserve ordered/repeated roots and the legacy sorted nonroot digest multiplicity. An independent Zig planner qualifies exact decisions, indexes and nonroot IDs in shadow mode. Malformed input, exhausted budgets and failed components are explicit errors, never empty affected sets.
- [SELFHOST-CACHE-CONTRACT-1] The Wave 1 reuse contract admits only pure computation, disposable storage, successful results, cache publication and pure recomputation. Durable retention/checkpoints, run/step occurrence identities, measurement, exploration and effect/retry policies are explicit reserved distinctions and are refused until their guarantees are implemented; a compiler cache receipt never claims application crash recovery.
- [SELFHOST-CACHE-IDENTITY-1] Every test replay path binds the verified planner mode/schema/implementation identity before reuse, including directory tool tickets, child expectations, whole-file reports and per-test receipts. Changing only the planner invalidates that evidence; missing or corrupt deployment refuses even an otherwise warm run. The deployment is revalidated before accepting replay and publishing receipts, and component execution uses a private sealed memory snapshot verified against the bound digest and retained until child completion.
- [SELFHOST-CACHE-REPORT-1] Test JSON results carry additive per-test cache explanations identifying the action, reason, receipt key when available and bound planner identity. Whole-report replay replaces the earlier execution explanation with the current reuse reason, and directory aggregation preserves the explanations without treating skipped or unsupported work as previously passed.
- [SELFHOST-COMPONENT-1] Informational test reachability in `tests-for` (checked and syntax), `context`, `lint` and checked `list-interfaces` uses the separately compiled Selka test-index component. Its versioned bounded binary protocol preserves discovery order, cycles and repeated direct edges; malformed, stale, oversized, missing or digest-mismatched artifacts and failed or timed-out executions produce an explicit command failure, never empty answers or automatic reference fallback. `MYLANG_TEST_INDEX_MODE=reference` selects the native bootstrap oracle and `shadow` checks exact parity. Installed components are resolved beside the host, or through `MYLANG_COMPONENT_DIR`; an independently pinned compiler builds them, and component-only edits rebuild the component without changing the host executable.
- [TESTS-FOR-1] `mylang tests-for <entry.my> <qualified-name>` loads and checks the project, then prints the discovered tests that reach that declaration: a `direct` list of tests whose body calls it directly, and a `reached-by` list of every test that reaches it transitively. It is a selection aid, not a verdict: an unknown qualified name prints a message and exits 2, and a declaration no test reaches prints empty lists and exits 0.
- [TESTS-FOR-SYNTAX-1] `mylang tests-for` on a project whose bodies do not type-check still answers, exit 0 with the diagnostics on stderr, from a call graph read from the merged syntax: each call resolves by name through the calling module's own declarations and its import aliases, following re-exports, and the tests are the checked discovery rule's. The answer is the checked answer's three lines followed by a `basis: syntax` line; a project whose syntax does not parse says so and exits 1.
- [REF-DIFF-1] The commit-time rules identify changed declarations from `git diff --unified=0 <reference>` run in the entry file's own repository: the changed working-tree line ranges come from the `+` side of each hunk header, and a declaration is changed when its source line range intersects one. A failed git invocation is a host failure, and the reference revision is never recompiled.
- [CHANGE-WITHOUT-TEST-1] Under `mylang lint --gate --against REF` only, every non-test function whose declaration changed since `REF` must be the direct callee of a test whose own body changed or was added in the same diff, be exempt under `test-coverage.exempt`, or carry a `change-without-test` waiver; otherwise it reports `L_CHANGE_WITHOUT_TEST` at error severity with a related note naming the tests that currently call it directly. A plain `lint --gate` without `--against`, and a baseline row under `.mylang/lint/`, never suppress it, because the rule judges a diff rather than the standing tree.
- [API-DIFF-1] `mylang api-diff <entry.my> --write=FILE` records an `api-diff-baseline-v1` snapshot of every declaration's stable identity and its interface and body fingerprints; `--against=FILE` classifies the current project against one snapshot as `additive` (a new identity), `compatible` (only the body fingerprint changed), `breaking` (the identity disappeared or its interface fingerprint changed) and `unchanged`, printing a one-line summary and the changed declarations by default or one `api-diff-v1` JSON object with `--json`. It is a report and never blocks: exactly one of `--write`/`--against` is required, and a malformed or unreadable baseline exits 2.
- [API-FILE-1] `mylang api` renders one interface file per project module (every loaded module except the embedded standard library), named like its source with `.api` for `.my`: the line `# generated by mylang api; do not edit`, `module NAME`, the first paragraph of the module docstring, then every public declaration in source order as a blank-line separated block in mylang declaration syntax without bodies: records, classes, unions, enums and results with their fields and cases, domains with their codec and their public members' signatures nested beneath them, resources, type aliases, named constraints, `const` declarations with their type and value, re-exports as `export module.Name`, and functions with their whole signature (type parameters and requirements, `mut` markers, the keyword-only `*`, defaults, the return type, any `noalloc`/`noio`/`effects(...)` clause as written) and the `ceiling(...)` `list-interfaces` publishes, preceded by their `@deprecated` and `@requires`/`@ensures`/`@invariant` clauses with whitespace folded to single spaces. Types are canonical: aliases expanded, capabilities in their Python spelling, this module's own declarations unqualified and every other one qualified by its full module name. The text reads only the declaration index, so an edit to a body, a private declaration, a comment, a declaration docstring or the layout leaves it byte-identical, and the same sources render the same bytes whatever the cache state.
- [API-FILE-2] `mylang api <entry.my> --write` writes every missing or stale interface file, leaves current ones untouched, and deletes an orphaned one: a `.api` file under the entry's root, outside hidden directories and `zig-out`, that carries the generated header, belongs to no loaded module and whose `.my` source no longer exists (a hand-written file, or one whose source exists but another entry loads, is never touched). `--check` writes nothing and exits 1 when any file is missing, stale or orphaned, printing each such module with a short unified diff from the checked-in file to the regenerated one (at most 40 lines per module) and the `--write` command to run, or exits 0 with a one-line summary. `--json` prints one `interface-files-v1` object whose `modules` list each out-of-date file with its `state` and the `added`, `removed` and `changed` declaration names (`Domain.member` for a domain member, `__doc__` for the module docstring). Exactly one of `--write`/`--check` is required (exit 2 otherwise), a declaration error exits 1 with its diagnostics, and a file that cannot be read, written or deleted exits 2.
- [API-FILE-3] A `mylang.json` carrying `"interface_files": true` makes the interface files part of checking the project: once its bodies check, `check`, `check-project`, `run`, `run-project` and a whole-program `emit-project-qbe` (the `build` frontend) report each missing or stale file as `E_INTERFACE_FILE_STALE` at `PATH:1:1`, naming the module and the `mylang api <entry> --write` to run, and exit 1. Every checked-in interface file's bytes are folded into the configuration identity the successful-verdict caches key on, so editing or deleting one never replays an earlier success. The value must be a JSON boolean; without the key, or with `false`, nothing changes. Navigation, listing, lint and native-test preparation do not enforce it.
- [LINT-2] A `mylang.json` version 2 may carry a `lint` object: `profile` (`agent` default, `strict`, `safety`), `on_build` (`report` default, `block`), `warnings` (`report` default, `deny`), positive integer `min_chars` (the `undocumented-export` docstring floor, 12 by default), `block`/`ratchet`/`report` arrays of rule names, and a `rules` object whose per-rule settings carry positive integer `max`, `hard_max` and `recover_pct`, reasoned `exempt` and `allow` entries, and `modules` globs. Unknown or duplicate keys, malformed values, a non-object `lint`, and an exemption without a name or a non-empty reason are located configuration failures (status 2); `lint` requires `version: 2`, and a version 1 document keeps parsing without it. A configured `lint` object participates in verdict cache identity exactly as `import_policy` does.
- [LINT-1] `mylang lint <entry.my|dir>` loads the project through the shared frontend, runs every enabled rule over the checked program, prints each finding as an `L_*` diagnostic on stderr and one summary line on stdout. It exits 0 when the language is valid and no finding blocks, 1 for a source, type, effect or ownership error with no rule evaluated, 2 for a usage or configuration failure, and 3 when a blocking finding exists. Without `--gate` a `block` rule is reported at warning severity and never changes the exit unless the project sets `on_build: "block"`; `--gate` raises each block finding to error severity and refuses. `--json` (or `--diagnostics=json`) renders findings as `diagnostic-v1` JSON Lines on stderr, and `--profile=agent|strict|safety` overrides the configured profile for one run. A `# lint: allow <rule> reason="..."` comment on the line above a declaration waives that rule for it; a waiver without a reason is `L_WAIVER_REASON` and always blocks. The rules are `untested-export` (report under `agent`, block under `strict` and `safety`), `undocumented-export` (report under `agent`, block under `strict` and `safety`; a missing, too-short or name-restating docstring on an exported function, record, union or domain), `dead-private` (a private function with no call site), and the test-hygiene rules `test-no-assertion` (a test body whose every `return` is the literal `true` and which never asserts), `test-conditional` (a branch or match, or more than one loop, at a test body top level) and `test-empty` (an empty test body), all blocking under every profile. The advisory size and reach report-only rules — `function-length`, `parameters`, `nesting-depth`, `decision-points` and `dead-export` — are `.warning` under every profile and can never refuse; their thresholds come from `lint.rules.<name>.max` and default to 80 lines, 6 parameters, nesting 4, 10 decision points, and no caller anywhere in the project respectively.
- [LINT-3] `mylang lint --accept` writes the baseline and ratchet stores under `.mylang/lint/`, keyed on the checker's resolved declaration identity rather than message text. A baseline row `<module>|<qualified>` marks one existing finding as accepted debt: under `--gate` a blocking finding with a row is reported at warning severity and does not refuse, while a finding without a row refuses. A ratchet row `<qualified> <ceiling>` records a per-declaration ceiling for a threshold rule that may only shrink. A run without `--accept` never writes, prunes or lowers a store, baseline rows are sorted so the committed file is merge-friendly, and a store file containing merge conflict markers is refused rather than merged.
- [LINT-4] `integration-tests` (`L_TEST_CAPABILITY`) reports a discovered test whose declared parameters request `Filesystem`, `Network`, `Clock` or `Entropy` and whose module does not match the rule's `modules` globs (default `*_integration`, `integration.*`), because such a test is an integration test and belongs in an integration module. It reports under every profile and never blocks; a reasoned `exempt` entry or a matching module glob leaves the test alone.
- [LINT-5] The tests-reachability index built for `mylang tests-for` is shared: every `mylang lint` finding that names a declaration carries the discovered tests that reach it as a related note (a rule that set its own related note keeps it, and a declaration no test reaches gets none), and `mylang list-interfaces` prints a `tests(N)` column on each exported callable when bodies were checked, where `N` is the number of discovered tests that reach it directly or transitively.
- [LINT-6] The advisory section-9 report rules ship under every profile and can never block: `record-size` (`L_RECORD_SIZE`, a record or class over `rules.record-size.max`, default 7 fields), `file-length` (`L_FILE_LENGTH`, a module over `rules.file-length.max`, default 1000 non-blank non-comment lines), `bool-ops-per-condition` (`L_BOOL_OPS`, a single branch or loop condition combining more than `rules.bool-ops-per-condition.max` `and`/`or` operators, default 3), `naming` (`L_NAMING`, a function, parameter, local, record, union or domain that is not snake_case, PascalCase or off the vague-name list), `repeated-literal` (`L_REPEATED_LITERAL`, an integer literal other than `0`/`1` appearing at least three times in one module), `same-type-adjacent-params` (`L_SAME_TYPE_ADJACENT_PARAMS`, adjacent parameters sharing a non-capability type), `duplicate-body` (`L_DUPLICATE_BODY`, two or more checked functions with an identical normalized body, never blocking) and `output-in-library` (`L_OUTPUT_IN_LIBRARY`, a function whose inferred effects include `output` in a module outside `rules.output-in-library.modules`).

## Collection and statement surface

- [SURFACE-COLLECTIONS-1] Managed list push(value), len(), indexing, and indexed assignment preserve independent values across bindings; there is no shared-alias mode.
- [SURFACE-COLLECTIONS-2] Record construction names its type and fields — `Box[i64](value=42)`, `Empty()` — covering generic records, empty records declared with `pass`, and trailing argument commas.
- [SURFACE-COLLECTIONS-3] Generic managed list methods and indexing preserve abstract element type checking.

- [SURFACE-STMT-7] Condition expressions admit record constructors inside nested call arguments; record and field names remain case-sensitive identifiers.
- [SURFACE-STMT-8] Record constructor syntax accepts nested generic type arguments, including List fields initialized with contextually typed empty lists.
- [SURFACE-STMT-9] A newline separates postfix call suffixes from completed expressions outside parentheses, while parentheses permit multiline calls and suffixes.

- [SURFACE-COLLECTIONS-4] Managed list methods reject invalid argument counts, unknown methods, mismatched element types, and noninteger indices.

## Prelude types and contextual constructors

- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-1] List<T> and Result<T,E> are available without imports or explicit declarations and use existing managed-list and fallible-result semantics.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-2] Empty lists and Ok/Err payload constructors use expected binding, return, and concrete call-argument types.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-3] Record fields and managed list elements propagate their expected types into nested empty list expressions.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-4] Ok(()) constructs successful Result<unit,E> values with normal handling obligations.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-5] Generic List and Result expressions preserve symbolic type parameters through empty list creation, mutation, and Ok construction.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-6] Contextual constructors reject missing or incompatible expected types, invalid generic arity, and forbidden managed capability elements.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-7] Explicitly declared List and Result types retain their own nominal identities and contextual constructors.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-8] Builtin List and Result types share canonical identity across imported module signatures without imports of a prelude module.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-9] A module-local List declaration shadows the prelude in that module while unrelated modules continue to resolve the builtin List.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-10] Module-local functions named Ok or Err take precedence over contextual constructors in both ordinary and generic bodies, without spurious constructor diagnostics.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-11] A list method's operands are expected types too: `append`, `remove` and `index` expect the element type, `insert` an integer position and the element type, and `extend` the list type, so `rows.append([])` infers the empty literal as `rows: list[list[int]] = []` does and `octets.append(200)` on a `list[u8]` types the literal as `u8`; `bytes([])` and `byte_buffer([])` infer `list[int]`.

- [MODULE-DOT-1] Direct import aliases accept dot-qualified function and type references, including generic records and imported domain methods; local list methods retain their meanings.
- [MODULE-DOT-2] Dot-qualified imported functions can be passed as values, and generic function bodies resolve dotted module type references through their declaring module.
- [MODULE-DOT-3] Dotted qualification preserves private-symbol, private-domain-function, and import-alias-shadowing checks.
- [MODULE-DOT-4] Dotted module parsing, namespace normalization, checking, and execution release all allocations on allocation failure.

- [MODULE-DOT-5] Semantic caching recognizes dotted imported function values and keeps callback type catalogs fresh across repeated requests and imported function edits.
- [MODULE-DOT-6] An import admits the full dotted module path (`a.b.name`) and its local alias — the explicit `as` name, or the trailing segment (`b.name`) when there is none — as qualified function and type references. A bare prefix that does not continue a registered module path (`shared` when `shared.geometry` is imported) is not reserved, so a local or parameter may reuse it; only a segment that continues the path is joined. A multi-segment qualified name normalizes by splitting at its last dot.

## Python-oriented core values

- [PYTHON-CORE-1] Prelude List, Result and Option have one nominal identity shared across modules; unrelated user nominal declarations remain distinct.
- [PYTHON-CORE-2] Unit and Result<unit/int,int> entry functions adapt to the scalar runner status convention while retaining their ordinary guest signatures. The synthesized wrapper is not a source declaration, so no public interface names it and a project with such an entry emits native units like any other.
- [PYTHON-CORE-3] Representable unsuffixed integer constants inherit numeric contexts; explicit suffixes retain their types and out-of-range constants are rejected.
- [PYTHON-CORE-4] Decimal float literals and ordinary arithmetic/comparisons use binary64 numeric semantics, including equal signed zeros and unordered NaNs.
- [NUM-LITERAL-1] Integer literals take Python's spellings: decimal, `0x`/`0X` hexadecimal, `0o`/`0O` octal and `0b`/`0B` binary, digits in either case, with single underscores between digits and one directly after the base prefix (`1_000`, `0xDEAD_BEEF`, `0x_FF`, `0b1010_1010`). A spelling denotes the same value as its decimal form and keeps its type rules. A leading, trailing or doubled underscore (`1__0`, `1_`, `0_x`), a base prefix with no digit (`0x`) and a digit outside the base (`0b12`, `0o8`) are located `E_INTEGER_LITERAL` errors. A decimal integer may not start with a zero unless every digit is zero (`0`, `00`, `0_0` and `0u8` are legal), as in Python 3: `0755` is an `E_INTEGER_LITERAL` error whose message says to write `0o755` for octal or `755` for decimal, rather than silently meaning 755. Float literals keep leading zeros ([NUM-LITERAL-3]).
- [NUM-LITERAL-2] An integer width suffix (`i8` `i16` `i32` `i64` `u8` `u16` `u32` `u64`) follows the last digit directly and combines with every base: `0xFFu8`, `0b1010u16`, `0o17i32`, `1_000u32`. A suffix begins at the first byte that is not a digit of the base, and the suffix letters `i` and `u` are digits of no base, so a hexadecimal literal never swallows its suffix; an underscore between the digits and the suffix (`1_u8`) is a doubled or trailing underscore, and an unknown suffix stays `E_INTEGER_SUFFIX`. A `j` suffix (`1j`, `1.5j`) is rejected with a message that mylang has no complex numbers.
- [NUM-LITERAL-3] Float literals take Python's spellings: a leading dot (`.5`), a trailing dot (`5.`, `1.e2`), exponents (`1e10`, `2.5E-3`) and single underscores between digits in the mantissa and the exponent (`1_000.5`, `1e1_0`); leading zeros are allowed (`007.5`). `.5` is a float only where no operand ends directly before the dot, so `x.5` and `f().5` keep their field-access reading, and `5.name` keeps `5` and `.name` apart instead of forming a malformed float. A float takes no integer suffix and a hexadecimal, octal or binary spelling is never a float.
- [NUM-LITERAL-4] An unsuffixed integer literal in any base takes the integer type its context expects when its value fits it, in `const` initializers as in typed locals, fields, arguments and returns: `const MASK: u32 = 4278190080`, `const LOW: u8 = 0xFF`, `A + 1` beside a `u32` constant. Compile-time function bodies use the same rule for annotated bindings, call arguments, returns and assignments. A value outside the expected type stays an `E_INTEGER_RANGE` error, and an unexpected context leaves the literal `int`. The hint that gives a literal a constant's type inspects declaration policy before resolving a known constant and looks names up silently: a name that is not a constant, such as the module in `helpers.Kind.B`, leaves no diagnostic, so comparing against a module-qualified enum case beside module constants checks as before.
- [NUM-LITERAL-5] Malformed numeric spellings are reported once, at the literal, with a stable code: `E_INTEGER_LITERAL` for a misplaced underscore, a missing or invalid digit and a decimal leading zero, `E_FLOAT_LITERAL` for a malformed float, exponent or suffix, `E_INTEGER_RANGE` for a value that does not fit its type, and `E_INTEGER_SUFFIX` for an unknown suffix. No operator is added: `**`, `<<` and `>>` keep the [PARSER-HINT-10] alternatives.
- [NUM-LITERAL-6] `mylang fmt` reproduces a numeric literal byte for byte, so `0xFF`, `1_000` and `.5` are never rewritten to `255`, `1000` or `0.5`, and the checked constants in an interface listing keep the spelling the declaration wrote.
- [NUM-LITERAL-7] An array length `[N]T` and an explicit const generic length argument (`make[i64, 0x2](values)`) accept any unsuffixed integer spelling: `[16]u8`, `[0x10]u8`, `[0o20]u8`, `[0b1_0000]u8`, `[1_024]u8`. Every spelling of one value names the same type, and an interface listing prints the decimal form. A width suffix (`[2u8]`), a malformed spelling and a decimal leading zero (`[02]`) are rejected with a message naming the rule, as `E_ARRAY_LENGTH` for an array and `E_GENERIC_LENGTH` for a const generic argument; the host and 1048576-element bounds are unchanged.

## Surface syntax

- [PYTHON-1] Sources use `def`/`record`/`class` heads, colon and space-indented blocks, `#` comments, implicit continuation inside delimiters, and source-preserving virtual block delimiters. A suite opens at the `:` that ends its line and closes at its dedent; `{` and `}` are only a dictionary literal, so a brace-bodied declaration is rejected. A suite's reported bytes end at the last token it wrote, so the blank lines and comments before the next declaration belong to neither, and a function's measured length and navigation excerpt cover only the function. Missing blocks, inconsistent dedents, and indentation tabs are diagnosed. Indentation is the only block syntax, so `fmt` returns the text a program already has.
- [PYTHON-2] Functions support ordinary mutable assignment, annotated bindings, `True`/`False`/`None`, `and`/`or`/`not`, `pass`, `elif`, omitted unit return annotations, `int` as i64, and square type parameters whose omitted requirement defaults to copy. `pass` spells an empty suite wherever one is written, including a record with no fields and a domain with no members. A `;` separates two statements written on one line, as it does in Python.
- [ATTR-1] A declaration may carry a fixed-vocabulary attribute on the line above it: @deprecated("reason"), @test, or @allow("rule", reason="…"). The parser rejects any other @name with E_UNKNOWN_ATTRIBUTE and a repeated attribute on one declaration with E_DUPLICATE_ATTRIBUTE; the contract clauses @requires, @ensures and @invariant (CONTRACT-SYNTAX-1) are the attributes that may repeat. Attributes are part of the declaration header and the formatter round-trips them.
- [ATTR-2] @test makes a zero-parameter bool function a discovered test regardless of its name or export flag; mylang test, the native test schema and the W2 test rules agree on that set, and @test is reported in list-declarations and list-interfaces --contracts.
- [ATTR-3] A call to a @deprecated declaration from another module reports W_DEPRECATED naming the reason; the attribute participates in the declaration and project interface fingerprints, so removing a declaration deprecated in the baseline is an interface-compatible change once api-diff exists.
- [ATTR-4] @allow("rule", reason="…") on a declaration suppresses that lint rule for findings on the declaration and replaces the # lint: allow comment form; a reason is still mandatory (L_WAIVER_REASON). The comment form is still accepted alongside the attribute this round.
- [DOC-1] A triple- or single-quoted string literal that is the first statement of a module or of a function body is that declaration's docstring; it is inert, contributes no effect, and later standalone strings stay ordinary discarded expressions. list-interfaces shows its first line.
- [DOC-2] L_UNDOCUMENTED_EXPORT reports an exported function, record, union or domain with no docstring, one shorter than min_chars (12 by default, counted over non-whitespace characters), or one that merely restates the declaration name. It reports under agent and blocks under strict.
- [PY-RECORD-1] `record Name:` is the canonical declaration head for a nominal value record with annotated fields and named construction. `record` remains an ordinary identifier outside declaration position, so variables and parameters may still be named `record`; `class Name:` remains accepted as the compatibility spelling during the migration window.
- [PYTHON-3] Value records use `record` (or the `class` compatibility spelling) with annotated fields and named construction; square-bracket type applications and `match`/`case` preserve contextual variant names and underscore payload discards for semantic resolution. Every explicit type argument uses square brackets, including `todo[T]()`, `wrap[D](v)`, `codec_encode[D](v)` and the capability and resource operations.
- [PY-LAMBDA-1] `lambda name, ...: expression` is an expression-bodied spelling for the existing noncapturing function value. Its parameter and result types come from the expected `def(...) -> ... effects(...)` callback type, and invocation uses the same checked callback path as a named function value.
- [PY-LAMBDA-2] A lambda without an expected callback type is rejected. Its expression must match the expected result and exact effect ceiling; it may read its parameters and module constants but cannot capture a function local, whose diagnostic recommends a top-level function or explicit state parameter.

- [PY-VARIANT-1] `Type.case(value)` constructs an enum or tagged-union variant carrying its declared payload; `Type.case` without parentheses stays the payload-free construction and contextual `Ok`/`Err`/`Some`/`None` are unchanged. The payload evaluates once and `match`/`case` patterns keep the same spelling.
- [PY-GENERIC-1] `f[T](x)` applies explicit type arguments to a generic function, constant lengths included (`first[i64, 2](values)`), and a member call takes them the same way: `D.hidden[U](x)` on a domain method, `m.D.make[U](x)` on an imported one, and `m.make[i64, 2](v)` on an imported free function. An argument may name any type the lexer spells as a keyword, capabilities included: `hold[clock_cap]()`. A square-bracket application in call position names a function when the base is a declared function and a generic record otherwise, so `Box[T](field=v)` construction is unchanged and the nominal type catalog never resolves a function application as a type.
- [PY-GENERIC-2] Explicit call type arguments reach the same instance as inference, and the wrong number of arguments or an undeclared base name is rejected.
- [PY-FLOAT-1] `sqrt`, `floor`, `ceil`, `trunc` and `abs` are prelude operations on `float`, so guest code needs no `u64` bit pattern. Each takes one float and answers a float; a non-float argument is rejected, and a local binding or declared function of the same name shadows the operation.
- [PY-WILDCARD-1] `case _` in a `match` statement covers every variant no earlier arm named, discarding the payload of each one it covers.
- [PY-WILDCARD-2] A wildcard arm must be the last arm, must cover at least one variant, and is rejected when it would drop a result-bearing payload; that variant needs its own arm.
- [PY-SUBSCRIPT-1] A subscript index may begin with a parenthesis: `values[(n - 1) % 3]` is an index, because a square-bracket type application never contains a parenthesized group.
- [PY-RESERVED-1] `len` and `replace` are intrinsics only at the head of a call. Both name ordinary declarations, record fields, keyword arguments, members, exports and imported module members, so `strings.replace(...)`, `pool.len(p)`, `text.replace(old, new)` and a field named `len` are all spellable.
- [PY-LOOP-1] A `while True` loop with no `break` targeting it never falls through, so a function whose body ends in one needs no unreachable trailing return.
- [PY-PRELUDE-1] The `bytes` and `float` prelude types are registered wherever their names are written in source, including a variant payload, a record field and a bare annotation, rather than only where a literal or conversion appears.
- [PRELUDE-FOLD-1] `sum(xs)` adds every element of a `list[int]` or `list[float]` in order, starting from zero of the element type; an empty list sums to zero and any other element type is rejected.
- [PRELUDE-FOLD-2] `any(xs)` and `all(xs)` fold a `list[bool]` with `or` and `and`, stopping as soon as `any` sees `True` or `all` sees `False`; an empty list answers `False` and `True` respectively.
- [PRELUDE-EXTREME-1] `min` and `max` take a list of `int`, `float` or `str` values, or two scalars of one type, and answer an element. Text order compares the stored Unicode scalars directly and contributes no managed allocation, so a text extrema fold is accepted under `noalloc`. An empty list has no smallest element, so it traps `R_LIST_BOUNDS` at the call site.
- [PRELUDE-EXTREME-2] `min(a, b)` and `max(a, b)` of two scalars evaluate each argument exactly once, left to right, like any other call: `max(len(xs), push(mut xs) + len(xs))` runs `push` once. The second argument used to be evaluated for the comparison and again for the answer.
- [PRELUDE-SORT-1] `sorted(xs)` returns a new ascending list of integer, `float`, `str` or `bytes` elements, leaving the input list and every alias of it unchanged. It uses the same stable `O(n log n)` native merge engine as in-place sorting with one reusable work buffer. Equal elements and signed zeros keep their input order, NaNs follow every ordered float, and `str` values order by Unicode code point, matching `sort.compare_strs`.
- [PRELUDE-SORT-KEY-1] `sorted(xs, key=callback, reverse=flag)` copies the list and sorts the copy exactly as `values.sort(key=callback, reverse=flag)` sorts in place (LIST-SORT-KEY-1): any ordered key, each computed once in input order, stable with and without `reverse`. The input remains unchanged, the list is positional, and `key` and `reverse` are its only named arguments, both optional and keyword-only.
- [PRELUDE-ABS-1] `abs` is total for `int`: it answers the magnitude and traps `R_OVERFLOW` on the one value with no positive counterpart, `-9223372036854775808`. A `float` argument still lowers to the prelude float operation.
- [PRELUDE-ROUND-1] `round(value)` takes a `float` and answers the nearest integral `float`, with halves rounded away from zero, matching `math.round`. Not-a-number and the infinities pass through unchanged.
- [PRELUDE-ROUND-2] `round(value, ndigits)` takes a `float` and an integer and answers the `float` nearest the exact binary value rounded to `ndigits` decimal digits, a negative count rounding left of the point, with halves away from zero as `PRELUDE-ROUND-1` rounds: `round(2.675, 2)` is `2.67` because the binary value lies below the half, while `round(0.125, 2)` is `0.13` where CPython's ties-to-even gives `0.12`. As in CPython a count above 323 answers the value, one below -308 a zero of its sign, and not-a-number and the infinities pass through.
- [PRELUDE-ITERATION-1] `reversed(xs)` answers a new list of a list or string `xs` from last to first and is also a `for` source; `zip(a, b)` is a `for` source that walks two lists in step and stops at the shorter one; `list(...)` copies a list and materialises `list(range(end))` or `list(range(start, end))`.
- [PRELUDE-SHADOW-1] Every prelude builtin answers only when no local binding, declared function or module top-level name claims it, the same shadowing rule the other prelude operations follow.
- [PRELUDE-KEYS-1] `list`, `sorted`, `sum`, `min`, `max`, `any` and `all` of a dictionary read its keys, and of a `keys()` or `values()` view that view's snapshot, in insertion order as a `for` loop visits them; a set reads its elements the same way ([SET-1]).

- [PY-COMPREHENSION-1] `[for name in source if condition yield value]` eagerly builds a fresh list through one checked loop. The source evaluates first; the optional Boolean filter runs before the produced value. Its readonly binding is confined to the expression. Unfiltered results reserve the source bound, while filtered results grow as values are retained.
