Every runtime consumer reads octets through one accessor, so `len`, scalar indexing and slicing of non-ASCII text, search, `count`, `replace`, comparison, sorting, printing, f-strings, `int`/`float` parsing, `encode`, joining and every file, stream, host and codec boundary answer exactly what a copy answered, and a piece outlives every other reference to the text it reads.
- [TEXT-VIEW-2] A view is allocated with an element descriptor whose one traced field is its owner, so the collector and the arena scan keep the owner alive through the view with no change of their own, and the budget charges a view as the 48-byte object it is. A view's hash equals an owned text's hash of the same octets and is recomputed rather than cached, because its hash word holds the offset, so a view is a dictionary key interchangeable with any text of the same octets; a dictionary stores a view key as an owned copy of its octets in its own region, so every stored key reads its octets from its own block and a key never keeps a larger text alive. A piece made inside `with arena()` belongs to the innermost arena exactly as a copy did — the receiver is reused as a whole piece only when it already lives there — so the store barrier traps `R_ARENA_ESCAPE` exactly where it did, a view never lives in a shallower arena than its owner, and `escape` copies a view into an owned text of its own octets.
- [TEXT-VIEW-3] The bounded retention policy: an explicitly bounded slice, split piece or trimmed piece shorter than 9 octets is copied, because its copy occupies the same 48-byte size class, is contiguous and caches its hash; a longer piece is a view when it is at least a quarter of its owner's octets and a copy otherwise, so a live bounded view keeps at most four times its own octets reachable and a short bounded piece of a large text never keeps the large text alive; a piece starting past 4 GiB of its owner is copied. Repeatedly slicing the front off a text with explicit upper endpoints copies at most a geometric series of its length — each copy starts a new owner — and stays linear. Omitted upper endpoints use [TEXT-VIEW-5].
- [TEXT-VIEW-4] Number parsing, formatting, encoding, joining, case mapping, padding, comparison and sorting read a view's octets in place exactly as an owned text's, and a boundary that needs a contiguous or NUL-terminated buffer copies the octets out as it already did for every text.
- [TEXT-INDEX-1] A runtime-owned `str` of at least 256 octets that holds a non-ASCII scalar carries a position index in its own block, behind its payload: the last scalar a lookup found with the octet where it begins, and a table with the octet offset of every 128th scalar, four bytes per entry (about 3 % of the scalar count; no ASCII text, no text below 256 octets and no view has one; static literals use [TEXT-INDEX-4]). A scalar index becomes an octet offset by walking, a word of eight octets at a time, from the nearest of the payload's two ends, the last lookup and the table entry nearest the scalar, and the table is filled, in one pass over the payload, only by the first lookup that is more than 1024 scalars from every one of them, so a text that is scanned in order or read near its ends never builds it. `s[i]`, `for ch in s`, `enumerate(s)`, `ord(s[i])`, `for i in range(len(s)): s[i]` and any other order of lookups — reversed, strided, random, two positions closing on each other — cost time linear in the text where each lookup walked from the start of the payload, and the index is one function of the immutable payload, so filling or moving it is invisible to values, aliases and the collector: it lives in the text's own plain block, which the collector never walks and one free releases, never in a table that could outlive the text or name a freed address. An ASCII text tests its flag first exactly as before.
- [TEXT-INDEX-2] A slice `s[a:b]` of a non-ASCII text — and `strip`, `lstrip` and `rstrip`, which cut by scalar — locates both ends through the index and knows the scalar count `b - a` it asks for, so it counts no octet of the piece: a piece that [TEXT-VIEW-3] shares is described as a view in constant time, so `rest = s[pos:]` in a loop is linear where each slice counted the whole piece, and a copied piece copies its octets once. A view of an indexed text finds a scalar more than one stride from either of its own ends through the owner's table — the scalar where the view begins, once, then the owner's lookup that many scalars further on — and an octet offset turns back into a scalar index, as `find` and `strip` need, from the nearer end of its text or through the table when both ends are far.
- [TEXT-INDEX-3] Every constructor that knows the scalar count of the text it builds — decoding a byte string, concatenation, `join`, `replace`, case mapping, `zfill`, a slice, an escape copy, a stepped slice, a dictionary's copy of a key piece — allocates the index with the text and publishes an exact ASCII flag (a text whose scalar count equals its octet count is ASCII whatever produced it, so `replace("é", "e")` answers text that indexes by octet), and decoding a mostly ASCII byte string validates whole words of ASCII at a time. Results, the `R_TEXT_BOUNDS` trap and its detail, negative indices, slice clamping, immutability, equality, ordering and hashing are those of a text with no index.
- [LIST-SORT-RUNS-1] `values.sort()` — and `sorted`, `sort.sort_ints`, `sort.sort_floats` and `sort.sort_strs`, which sort a copy with it — is an adaptive stable merge in the native runtime: an ascending run already in the list is kept, a strictly descending run is reversed, a run shorter than the minimum run length, between 16 and 32 elements, is extended by insertion, and runs merge in powersort order through one scratch buffer of at most half the list, so an ordered, reversed or run-structured list sorts in `O(n)` and every list in `O(n log n)`. Each element type has its own comparison and moves — signed and unsigned 64-bit integers, binary64 values and `str` — and the narrower integer widths share one generic path. The order is exactly PY-COLLECTIONS-15's: integers by value, every NaN after every ordered float, equal values and signed zeros in input order, `str` by Unicode scalar value. The list keeps its identity, length, capacity and generation, every alias sees the sorted elements, and a sort allocates nothing managed, so no collection runs while it holds elements in its scratch buffer.
- [PY-DICT-2] A managed dictionary keeps an open-addressing hash index beside its insertion-ordered entry list, so reading, writing, `in`, `setdefault` and `update` are expected constant time in the entry count instead of a scan. The index is an untraced managed block owned by the dictionary's own region, reached through a traced field, so collection keeps it alive with the dictionary, an arena releases the two together and its bytes are charged to the memory budget. Hashing reads exactly the units equality compares — a `str` key's UTF-8 octets, any other key's bytes ([PY-DICT-3]) — with fixed constants and no seed, so a dictionary behaves identically on every run. Every observable answer is unchanged: iteration, `keys`/`values`/`items`, snapshots and printing stay in insertion order, structural equality still ignores insertion order, and `pop` removes its key without moving any other entry ([DICT-REMOVE-1]). An arena escape copies the entry list first and hashes the clone's keys only once every cloned key holds its final contents; a refused index allocation leaves the dictionary answering by scanning rather than failing the write.
- [PY-DICT-3] A dictionary key is hashed a word at a time. A scalar key's bytes, zero-extended to one 64-bit word, are hashed by one full 64×64-bit multiply whose high and low halves are xored together and folded to 32 bits. A `str` key's UTF-8 payload is read eight octets at a time — a payload of at most eight octets as two overlapping halves folded once, a longer one word by word into a state seeded by its length, ending on the word that finishes at its last octet, so no read passes the payload and every octet reaches the answer — and the result is cached in the text's header. Both use fixed constants and no seed. Equal keys have equal bytes and therefore equal hashes, and no observable answer depends on the hash: insertion order, iteration, `keys`/`values`/`items`, snapshots, printing, equality and every lookup answer exactly as they did under the octet-at-a-time FNV-1a hash. A `float` key is refused ([HASH-KEYS-1]), so there is no `0.0`/`-0.0` or NaN key case, and every other key type hashes structurally ([HASH-KEYS-2]).
- [PY-DICT-4] Every get-then-set spelling probes the table once for every key type. `counts[k] = counts.get(k, 0) + 1`, `counts[k] += 1` and `if k in counts: counts[k] = …` lower to separate runtime calls on the same key, and the dictionary remembers what its last probe learned: a found entry for any key — a `str` key's object pointer, an integer key's bytes — accepted only while the live entry at the recorded position still holds the queried key, and an integer key's absence together with the empty slot a write of it would fill, so a write after a missed read inserts without walking the table again. Every insertion, table growth or refusal, `pop`, `clear` and arena reindex drops a recorded absence first. A `str` key object's absence is never recorded, because a collected text's address can be reused by an equal text that is present (a described slice's absence is, dated by the heap's collection count — [DICT-RANGE-KEY-2]); the write after a missed `str` read walks the table again, comparing only stored hashes. `setdefault` probes once for either outcome. Lookups, insertion order, defaults, traps and arena ownership are unchanged. A successful ordinary probe publishes only its key, entry and found state; fields used only by absent or described-span states retain their previous bytes. Every consumer checks its exact state, and every publisher initializes the fields required by that state. Found-entry revalidation, scalar absent slot/hash, span offset/length and GC epoch, invalidation, ownership, allocation and refusal behavior remain unchanged. Reads and `get` of dictionaries whose key and value are each `i64` or `u64` may select a scalar-word probe. It retains the same key and output ABI, validates the ordinary found nomination against live stored bits, and falls back to the complete read for unsupported runtime shapes or scan-only storage. Missing output bytes, defaults, failure provenance, ownership and accounting are unchanged.
- [PY-DICT-5] Each index slot packs its key's 32-bit hash above one past the entry index it names, still eight bytes per slot, so a probe loads an entry only when the stored hash equals the queried key's and a table grows by re-placing its slots from their stored hashes without reading or rehashing a key. `pop` empties its key's slot by shifting the rest of the collision run back, so every remaining key stays reachable from its home slot, and leaves the entry for `settle` ([DICT-REMOVE-1]): it allocates nothing and hashes at most the popped key. A slot names at most 2³²−2 entries; a longer entry list drops the index and scans, exactly as a refused index allocation does.
- [DICT-RANGE-KEY-2] A lookup by a described slice that misses records the absence with the text it ranges over, the span and the heap's collection count, so the write of a read-modify-write inserts at the empty slot the lookup ended on without hashing or walking the table again. The record is believed only while no arena is open and no collection has run since it was written — a tracing-heap text is reclaimed only by a collection, so an unchanged count proves the recorded address still names the same immutable text — and every insertion, table change, `pop`, `clear` and reindex drops it first. A recorded found entry answers a described slice only after its stored key's octets compare equal. A slice's recorded absence is kept apart from an ordinary key's, so a lookup of a whole text is never answered by the record of a slice of it.
- [HASH-KEYS-1] A dictionary key or set element type must be hashable: every integer kind, `bool`, `unit`, `str`, `bytes`, and a tuple, record, union, enum, domain, `Option`, `Result` or fixed array whose parts are all hashable. `float` (NaN is not equal to itself), a `list`, `dict`, `set` or `byte_buffer` (a shared reference whose contents can change after it is stored), a function value (no equality) and a capability, file or resource are refused where the dictionary or set is built — a dict literal, a set literal, `set()`, `set(iterable)` and a set comprehension — with `E_DICT_KEY` for a key and `E_SET_ELEMENT` for an element, whose message names the key type, the path to the first offending part (`.field`, `[index]` for a tuple element, `.case` for a union payload, `[]` for an array element) and the reason. In a generic template a concrete key type follows the same rule, `str` is accepted, and a type parameter must promise `integer`.
- [HASH-KEYS-2] A key that is neither a `str` nor a plain scalar — a record, tuple, union, enum, domain, `Option`, fixed array, `bytes`, or any value holding a `str` — is hashed and compared by walking its descriptor: scalar bytes a word at a time, a `str` through its cached text hash and its octets, `bytes` through the octet hash a text of the same octets gets and its octets, each field in declaration order, a union's case tag and then only that case's payload, and each array element. Padding and an inactive case's bytes are never read, the constants are fixed and there is no seed, so equal values hash equally on every run and the comparison is exactly `==`. A stored key is a copy, so a later field assignment to the binding it came from cannot change the table, and dictionary equality looks each key up in the other table instead of scanning it.
- [DICT-REMOVE-1] Removing a key from a dictionary or set (`dict.pop`, `set.remove`, `set.discard`, `set.pop`) takes its slot out of the index and moves no other entry: an entry in the middle of the entry list is only counted dead, its bytes kept and traced until `settle` squeezes every dead entry out in insertion order, renumbering the slots of the survivors in place without allocating; the last entry is cut off together with every dead entry before it, so the last entry is always live. Every positional reader — iteration, `key_at`/`value_at`, key and value snapshots, printing, equality, the source of `update` and `copy`, and the arena escape copier — settles first; an insertion settles once dead entries are at least half the list, and a table about to grow or be dropped settles before it does. `len` counts live entries. A dictionary whose index was refused still shifts later entries down.
- [SET-1] `set[T]` is a dictionary-kind collection from its hashable elements to unit, kept in first-insertion order and independent on assignment. `{a, b}` builds one in source order (a first element followed by `,`, `}` or `for` starts a set literal or a set comprehension, and `{}` stays the empty dict); `set()` builds an empty set of the type the context expects and is `E_SET_INFERENCE` without one; `set(iterable)` copies a list, `str`, `bytes`, fixed array, dictionary's keys, dictionary view, `range(...)` or set; `{value for name in source if condition}` is the set comprehension. The methods are `add`, `remove` (a missing element traps `R_MISSING_KEY`), `discard`, `pop` (removes and answers the most recently added live element; an empty set traps `R_MISSING_KEY`), `clear`, `copy`, `update`, `union`, `intersection`, `difference`, `symmetric_difference`, `issubset`, `issuperset` and `isdisjoint`, and `union` is accepted as a member name after a dot although it is a declaration keyword. `update`, `union`, `intersection`, `difference`, `symmetric_difference`, `issubset`, `issuperset` and `isdisjoint` take another set or any iterable `set(...)` accepts, which they collect into a set first, as Python's methods do (`s.union([4])`); the operators still take two sets. `in`, `len`, `for`, `enumerate`, `list`, `sorted`, `sum`, `min`, `max`, `any` and `all` see the elements in insertion order, and a resize during iteration traps `R_LIST_ITERATION_MUTATION`. A set prints as `{1, 2}` and as `set()` when empty, wherever it is nested. `{}` against a set type is `E_DICT_TYPE`, indexing a set is `E_SET_INDEX`, and any other method is `E_LIST_METHOD` listing the set methods. A function that changes a set parameter declares it `mut`. A program's own function named `set` keeps its name and its calls beside the builtin type; in such a module set literals still build sets but the type spelling `set[T]` is not available.
- [SET-OPERATORS-1] Between two sets of one type, `|`, `&`, `-` and `^` build a new set (union, intersection, difference, symmetric difference) holding the left operand's surviving elements in its order followed by the right operand's new elements in theirs, each operand evaluated once, left first; `<=`, `<`, `>=` and `>` test subset, proper subset, superset and proper superset, stopping at the first element that settles the answer; `==` ignores insertion order; the methods of the same names answer the same. `|`, `^` and `&` parse with Python's precedence, below arithmetic and above comparison. On operands that are neither sets nor integers ([PY-SET-OPS-1]), `|`, `&` and `^` are `E_SET_OPERATOR` naming `bit_or`, `bit_and` or `bit_xor`, any other operator on a set is `E_SET_OPERATOR`, an augmented assignment to a set is `E_SET_OPERATOR`, and `|=`, `&=` and `^=` report that a set has no augmented assignment operator.
- [LIST-SORT-KEY-1] `values.sort(key=f, reverse=b)` sorts a list in place and `sorted(values, key=f, reverse=b)` sorts a copy, with Python's keyword-only `key=` and `reverse=`, both optional. The key maps one element to an ordered key — any integer width, `bool`, `float`, `str`, `bytes`, or a flat tuple of those compared field by field — and is evaluated exactly once per element, in input order, before any element moves. The order is stable: elements with equal keys keep their input order, and `reverse=True` orders the keys descending while equal keys still keep input order, so it is not sort-then-reverse. Float keys order as PY-COLLECTIONS-15 orders floats — signed zeros equal, every NaN after every ordered value — and `reverse=True` puts the NaNs first. Without a key, `values.sort(reverse=True)` and `sorted(values, reverse=True)` order an integer, `float`, `str` or `bytes` list by its elements under the same rules; a bare `values.sort()` and `sorted(values)` are unchanged. The list keeps its identity, length and capacity, and every alias sees the new order. A key that traps propagates the trap before any element has moved.
- [LIST-SORT-KEY-2] A keyed sort's operands are checked where they are written: a positional operand, an unknown keyword or a repeated one is `E_CALL_ARGUMENT` with the accepted signature, and `cmp=` also names the key alternative; a key that is not a callback of exactly one element of the list's element type, that declares its argument `mut`, or that returns a type with no order is `E_SORT_KEY` naming the types; `reverse=` of any type but `bool` is `E_TYPE`; a sort without a key over an element with no native order is `E_COLLECTION_METHOD`. A lambda key's key type is read off its body when the body is the element, a field path from it, a negation or `len` of one, or a tuple of those, and is otherwise the body's ordinary type (LAMBDA-KEY-INFER-1). The key is invoked like any callback value, so its declared effect ceiling is charged to the enclosing function. Named arguments on any other collection method are `E_CALL_ARGUMENT`.
- [LAMBDA-KEY-INFER-1] A one-parameter sort-key lambda (`sorted(values, key=lambda v: ...)`, `values.sort(key=...)`) whose body is not one of the shapes LIST-SORT-KEY-2 reads takes the body's ordinary checked type as its key type: the body is checked once with its parameter bound to the list's element type and no expected result, and every trace of that trial (typed nodes, statements, locals, resolved entries, the parameter binding, option facts, ownership state and diagnostics) is undone before the lambda is checked for real against the slot `def(T) -> K effects(managed_allocation)` it fixes. The slot admits allocation because its one call site is the keyed sort, which the enclosing function is already charged `managed_allocation` for, so a key that builds a `str` (`p.name.lower()`, a slice, a helper that concatenates) is exact rather than exempt; a declared key function with a narrower ceiling still fills it. So a method call, a call to a declared function, a conversion, a slice or a conditional expression gives a `str`, `float` or integer key as written, where it used to be typed `int` and fail with `expected int, found str`. A body that does not type on its own is one `E_SORT_KEY` at the lambda that quotes the underlying diagnostic and says to write a top-level key function with a declared result; a key type with no order is still LIST-SORT-KEY-2's `E_SORT_KEY`.
- [LIST-SORT-KEY-3] A keyed sort lowers to one key list sized to the list, filled by one pass that calls the key once per element, and one native `myl_native_list_sort_keyed` call. A numeric key becomes an order-preserving 64-bit word — the sign bit flipped, binary64 bits reflected with signed zeros merged and every NaN the largest word, all complemented for `reverse` — and when the words span fewer distinct values than there are elements, a stable counting sort gives each element, in input order, the next slot of its key; otherwise, when every word minus the smallest fits above the position bits, `(word - smallest) << bits | position` words are sorted by the integer engine in the key list's own storage; keys spread wider take two such sorts of 32 bits each. Text and tuple keys sort (key, position) entries with a stable merge. For `values.sort(...)` the elements then move once along the permutation's cycles through element-sized temporaries, prefetching a few steps ahead on each cycle, so the extra memory is the key list plus the sort's scratch and no second list of elements exists; for `sorted(values, ...)` they are gathered in order from the original list into a new list allocated at the list's length, so the copy is never built only to be permuted. The native sort calls no guest code and allocates nothing managed; scratch memory the host refuses is the allocation-failure trap, raised before any element moves.
- [SYNTAX-METHOD-KEYWORDS-1] A method call on an expression receiver, `holder.items.sort(key=f)`, records a keyword for each named argument exactly as the named-receiver form `items.sort(key=f)` does, with the same rule that positional arguments precede keyword arguments.

## Local-reasoning syntax foundation

- [SYNTAX-MUT-1] `mut name: T` declares a writable function or callback parameter, and `mut argument` marks caller storage. Callback parameter modes retain both mut capability and named labels. The parser records the marker; semantic checking validates writable targets and exact call-site positions.
- [SYNTAX-KEYWORD-ONLY-1] A bare `*` in a declared function's parameter list sets `FunctionSignature.keyword_only_from` to the zero-based index of the first parameter after it, which is also the positional parameter count, and is null when the list has no `*`. Only the positional group keeps the required-before-defaulted rule, so a required keyword-only parameter may follow a defaulted one. A `*` with no following parameter is `E_SYNTAX` at the `*` naming the missing parameter, a second `*` in one list is `E_SYNTAX` at that `*`, and a `*` in a callback type is `E_SYNTAX` because a callback slot is positional. Record field lists and lambdas gain nothing.
- [SYNTAX-TUPLE-1] A parenthesized comma list builds a `tuple` node whose element nodes are in source order, and a trailing comma after two or more elements is accepted; a parenthesized expression with no comma stays that expression, so `(a)` is `a`. A bare comma list builds the same node after `return` and on both sides of a multi-target assignment, where the target list is a `tuple` of `name` nodes and the value is the right-hand `tuple`, reached through the same assignment node inside a discard that `rec.field = value` uses. One element is never a tuple: `(a,)`, `x, = pair` and a bare comma list with no `=` are `E_SYNTAX`.
- [SYNTAX-TUPLE-2] `tuple[T1, ..., Tn]` in any type position appends one `TupleType` with the element spans to `TypeExpressions.tuples`, from the `tuple` keyword through the closing bracket. The parser accepts any comma count, including one, so arity outside two to eight is a checker rule (`E_TUPLE_ARITY`) rather than a parse error, and `tuple` stays an ordinary type name when no bracket follows it.
- [SYNTAX-CONDITIONAL-1] `value_if_true if condition else value_if_false` parses as a `conditional` node and binds looser than every binary operator, with its condition parsed above `or` and its else arm at the lowest precedence, so `a or b if p else c` takes `a or b` as its then-arm and `a if p else b if q else c` puts a nested conditional in its else arm. A `kw_if` that begins a new logical line still starts an `if` statement, never a conditional.
- [SYNTAX-CONDITIONAL-2] A conditional expression is accepted in every expression position — a call argument, a keyword-argument value, a list literal element, an f-string hole, a `return` value and an assignment value — because it is parsed by the one expression entry point.
- [SYNTAX-FMT-1] The token formatter prints every new spelling through its ordinary spacing rules, so `mylang fmt` returns them unchanged: one space around `mut` and around a bare `*`, no space before a comma and one after it in a tuple or a `tuple[...]` type, and one space around `if` and `else` in a conditional. A trailing comma before a closing delimiter is the one exception: canonical text drops it consistently, in tuples as in argument and field lists, which never changes a program's meaning because no one-element tuple is spellable.
- [MUT-PARAM-1] Declared `mut name: T` positions form the caller-update contract. Every corresponding argument requires `mut argument`, and markers on other positions reject with `E_MUTATION_MARKER`. Direct calls, generic calls and callbacks enforce the same contract; mutating methods require writable receiver roots.
- [MUT-PARAM-8] A missing or superfluous call-site `mut` marker does not end its function's check: the checker records the `E_MUTATION_MARKER` diagnostic with its fix, checks the call as if the marker were right, and fails the function once its body is done, so one `check` reports every marker a function needs rather than one per run. A `mut` on an argument that cannot be assigned through still ends the function's check at once.
- [MUT-PARAM-2] Ordinary parameters are readonly snapshots; mutation through their roots rejects with `E_IMMUTABLE`. Declared mut parameters may be unused and still retain caller-update capability. Forwarding mut storage requires a writable root. Match payloads are readonly; copy them into a mut local and explicitly store back when needed.
- [MUT-PARAM-3] Mut parameters support scalars, text, bytes, collections, records and fixed arrays, including whole-value rebinding. A mut argument must name assignable caller storage: a writable variable, field or indexed slot rooted in one. Temporary constructors cannot be mut targets.
- [PY-ARRAY-MUT-1] `mut a: FixedArray[T, N]` permits element writes and whole-array rebinding through caller storage. Fields and indexed elements may be mut targets. Readonly inputs are independent snapshots. Mut array callbacks use the ordinary native inout convention; external C ABI restrictions remain separate.
- [MUT-PARAM-4] A `mut` parameter contributes `mutation` to the declared-ceiling check; exceeding an effect ceiling is `E_MUTATION_PERMISSION`. Forwarding a caller parameter as `mut` requires that parameter to be declared `mut`. With default value semantics, a read-only argument overlapping a writable argument receives a snapshot.
- [MUT-OVERLAP-RECEIVER-1] An assignment or update receiver may not overlap a `mut` argument evaluated in that same statement: the same root and paths that may agree until either ends give E_MUTATION_OVERLAP at the mut argument with the receiver as related evidence. Make the call in its own statement first and use its saved result. Disjoint roots and paths, a call in a preceding statement, and read-only snapshots remain legal. A simple unconditional one-line update with no other effectful evaluation carries the exact structured split-call fix; conditional or effectful rewrites require the programmer to choose their ordering.
- [ORIGIN-WIDE-1] The checker's mutation and alias analysis names every parameter position of a function exactly, so a `mut` collection, a returned read-only collection or a returned string at any parameter position checks, builds and runs (a 17th such parameter used to panic the checker). A list, string, record or other aggregate parameter must be one of a function's first 64 parameters (`E_PARAMETER_POSITION` at a later one, advising to move it earlier or group parameters into a record); scalar parameters are never tracked and stay unlimited. An unattributed write publishes every parameter position as possibly written.
- [MUT-EXCLUSIVE-1] Mut arguments of a call must name disjoint caller places. Known same-place or enclosing-part overlap rejects with `E_MUTATION_OVERLAP`. Dynamic indices and keys are evaluated once in source order and compared before invocation; overlap traps before the callee runs.
- [MUT-EXCLUSIVE-2] MUT-EXCLUSIVE-1 applies to every call that takes `mut` arguments: direct and module-qualified calls, keyword and mixed arguments (compared after binding to parameters, so `f(second=mut xs[0], first=mut xs[0])` is rejected and `f(second=mut xs[2], first=mut xs[0])` is not), and calls through function values and callbacks; builtins take no `mut` arguments. It compares `mut` arguments only: a read-only argument next to a `mut` one keeps the VALUE-SEMANTICS-5 snapshot (`touch(mut a, a)`), and a `mut` call nested inside another argument (`emit(mut p, make_constant(mut p, c))`) finishes before the outer call begins and stays legal.
- [MUT-EXCLUSIVE-3] Static overlap diagnostics point at the later argument and relate the earlier one. Dynamic overlap guards compare the captured normalized indices or keys along common root paths, so negative and positive indices naming one element trap. Independent field paths remain legal.
- [MUT-PARAM-5] With `mut` supplying the footprint, the `mutates(...)` clause and the opt-in `&` call-argument marker are removed spellings: a clause is `E_REMOVED_SPELLING` at `mutates` saying “mark each written parameter `mut name: T`”, `&` before an operand is `E_REMOVED_SPELLING` saying to write `mut value` (between two operands it is the set intersection, [SET-OPERATORS-1]), and the `--mutation-markers` switch is rejected on every command. The reference-typed `class` exemption and the advisory `W_MUTATES_MISSING` warning are gone with them: a compatibility-mode write through a class parameter needs its own `mut` like every other position, and the check is an error, not a warning.
- [MUT-PARAM-6] Signature surfaces render `mut name: T`, preserve mut positions and named labels in their public contract identity, and format call-site `mut argument` markers unchanged.
- [KEYWORD-ONLY-1] A `named name: T` parameter must be supplied with its label. Positional use rejects with `E_KEYWORD_ONLY`; labeled operands may be written in any order and still evaluate once in their source order.
- [KEYWORD-ONLY-2] Named mode belongs to each parameter contract, including callback types. Defaulted and required named parameters retain normal default and arity checking; generic and compile-time binding enforce the same labeled-call rule.
- [KEYWORD-ONLY-3] Rendered signatures preserve named parameter labels, such as `func(host: Host, named code: int) -> int`; public interface identity includes those labels, while positional binder names do not affect callback identity.
- [CONDITIONAL-EXPR-1] `value_if_true if condition else value_if_false` is an expression whose condition must be `bool` and whose two arms have one type: the contextual expected type when the destination supplies one, and otherwise the then arm's type, with an integer literal arm adopting the expected width and no numeric widening between arms. Arms that disagree are `E_TYPE` `conditional arms must agree: \`int\` here, \`str\` in the else arm`, reported at the first arm; a non-`bool` condition is the ordinary `E_TYPE` `expected bool, found int`.
- [CONDITIONAL-EXPR-2] A conditional is one expression wherever an expression is accepted: a `return` value, an assignment or binding value, a call or keyword-argument value, a list or record-constructor element, an f-string hole, and the else arm of another conditional (which is why `a if p else b if q else c` nests to the right). `Some(x) if p else None` and `Ok(x) if p else Err(e)` take their arm type from the expected `Option` or `Result`, exactly as those constructors do in any other contextual position.
- [CONDITIONAL-EXPR-3] Only the chosen arm is evaluated, so `a if p else f()` never calls `f` when `p` holds and an unchosen arm that would trap does not. Both arms are still checked: each contributes its reads (including `E_UNBOUND` for a name no path reaching it assigns), its effects and its origins, and a standalone conditional whose arms are not unit keeps `E_EXPLICIT_DISCARD_REQUIRED` like any other non-unit expression statement.
- [CONDITIONAL-EXPR-4] The check lowers the conditional to the if/else the rest of the compiler already understands — a fresh unnamed temporary that each arm assigns and the block reads — so no new checked node, effect rule, origin rule or bytecode shape is introduced, and a conditional inside a generic function body is checked symbolically with the same arm agreement and condition rules.
- [KEYWORD-ONLY-4] The `positional-literal` lint (report-only, `L_POSITIONAL_LITERAL`) reports the last operand a call of three or more arguments passes positionally as a `bool`, integer or `None` literal, at the callee: `` `True` is passed positionally to `draw`'s 4th parameter `filled`; name it: `draw(canvas, x=10, y=20, filled=True)` ``. The reported parameter is the one that operand binds to, an intrinsic or a call through a callback value is exempt because it has no parameter to name, and the repaired call comes from the shared call renderer with every operand from the first positional literal on written by name, so the paste is a call that binds.
- [TUPLE-1] A tuple is an anonymous record interned in the record catalog, keyed by its element types in source order, with positional fields named `$0`, `$1`, … that no source can spell; a module program keeps every `tuple[...]` spelling through the merge that combines its units, so a tuple type is one identity in a single file and in a project alike; the element list always interns to one identity, whether it is reached from a `tuple[T1, ..., Tn]` spelling through the layout graph or from a value through `record_layouts.tupleRecord`, so `tuple[int, str]` is one type in signatures, comparisons, caches and interface rendering. A spelling outside the two-to-eight element range is `E_TUPLE_ARITY` at the whole `tuple[...]` spelling, and a tuple is a plain record everywhere else: construction is a record construct, a read is a positional field read, and the contract of the whole (storable, move-only, result-handling, managed-bearing) is the element-wise rule `record_layouts` already derives from fields.
- [TUPLE-2] `(a, b)` and the bare forms after `return` build a tuple value from elements checked in source order, with the expected-type rule for literals: when the destination already demands `tuple[T1, T2]` each element is checked against its positional type, and otherwise the element types are inferred and interned. A tuple may be stored in a list, as a dictionary value, in a record field, in an `Option` or `Result` payload, and nested in another tuple; it is managed-bearing exactly when one of its elements is, and a value whose arity differs from the demanded tuple type is `E_TUPLE_ARITY` naming the count it has and the count it needs.
- [TUPLE-3] `x, y = value` destructures a tuple: the value is evaluated once into one temporary, its arity is checked against the number of names (`E_TUPLE_ARITY` naming the source of the value and the number of names to bind), and each name is bound to the positional field of that temporary, so `a, b = b, a` swaps with no intermediate list string. `_` in a target list binds nothing and reads nothing, a name may be rebound by the assignment like any other assignment, and a right-hand side that is not a tuple is `E_TYPE` naming the single-binding alternative.
- [PY-DESTRUCTURE-PLACE-1] A destructuring target list may name elements and fields as well as names: `a[i], a[j] = a[j], a[i]` swaps two elements of a fixed array or list, and `d[k], r.f, xs[-1].g, name = value` writes a dictionary entry, a record field, a field of an element and a binding. The value is evaluated once, before any target, into one temporary whose arity is checked as `TUPLE-3` says; then each target in order evaluates its own receiver and indices and takes its element exactly as `target = element` would, with the same type, mutability, `mut`-parameter, arena and negative-index rules. A target that is not a name, element or field is `E_ASSIGNMENT_TARGET`.
- [TUPLE-4] `for number, letter in pairs` destructures each two-element tuple element through the same two-binding path `DictItem`, `zip` and `enumerate` already use, so the loop variable names stay scoped to the body and the element type is the tuple's two positional types; a tuple element of any other arity is `E_TUPLE_ARITY` naming the one-name alternative.
- [TUPLE-5] A tuple has no elements to index, no fields, no methods and no `len`: `pair[0]` is `E_TUPLE_INDEX` naming the destructuring alternative, `pair.head` and `pair.count()` are `E_TYPE` naming the same alternative, and `len(pair)` is `E_TYPE` naming it too. A tuple type compared against a different tuple type is `E_TYPE` through the ordinary expected/found message, dictionary keys stay `int` and `str` so a tuple is never a key, `DictItem`, `zip` and `enumerate` are unchanged, `match` patterns over tuples are not accepted, and the `extern def` and JSON boundaries reject a tuple graph like any other non-data graph.
- [TUPLE-6] A tuple prints as `(1, 'a')` — parenthesized, comma-space separated, each element rendered exactly as `print` renders that element alone, so a text element is repr-quoted — and `==` on two tuples follows the aggregate rule: structural, element-wise and recursive, comparing text content rather than reference.
- [TUPLE-7] A tuple type renders in its source spelling, `tuple[int, str]`: `mylang fmt` returns a tuple value `(a, b)`, a `tuple[...]` type and a destructuring target list unchanged, and `list-interfaces` renders a signature that names a tuple through the canonical catalog name `tuple<i64,str>`, which the same surface rewriter turns back into `tuple[int, str]`.
- [TUPLE-8] A tuple type in a template keeps its elements abstract: `tuple[T, int]` is the ordered argument list of the anonymous tuple declaration, not a representative concrete type, so a generic function may take, return and destructure such a tuple and the bound element keeps its parameter identity. Requirements on a template tuple are its elements' requirements rather than a declaration-level guarantee, and a fully concrete element list materializes the same interned record that a closed `tuple[...]` spelling does, so a generic body and its specializations agree on identity.
- [OPTION-METHODS-1] `Option[T]` answers three intrinsic methods and nothing else: `unwrap_or(default: T) -> T`, `is_none() -> bool` and `is_some() -> bool`. They are reachable on both receiver spellings — a named local, whose call is checked where the collection receiver arm runs, and any other expression, whose method call is checked on the `E_COLLECTION_METHOD` dispatch path — and `unwrap_or`'s default is checked against the payload type with the ordinary expected-type rule, so `head(narrow).unwrap_or(7)` adopts a `u8` payload and a mismatched default reports `E_TYPE` with `` `unwrap_or` default must be `int` (the Option's payload), found str ``. All three are pure, allocate nothing, and lower through `option_flow.zig`'s existing exhaustive variant dispatch: presence tests to the tag test and `unwrap_or` to a two-arm match that roots the receiver, evaluates the default eagerly into a fresh temporary and binds either the payload or that temporary into one result slot.
- [OPTION-METHODS-2] A generic body accepts the same three methods through the symbolic checker, so a template whose parameter is an `Option[T]` may call `unwrap_or`, `is_none` and `is_some` without an instantiation: the default is checked against the symbolic payload and the method contributes no effect, so an `effects()` ceiling accepts every one of them. An `Option` carrying an owned value or a checked result still requires explicit `match`, exactly as a presence test does, and a receiver that has no such method reports `E_COLLECTION_METHOD` naming `unwrap_or(default)`, `is_none()` and `is_some()` rather than the collection vocabulary.
- [OPTION-METHODS-3] The `sentinel-int` lint reports an `int` used as a sentinel for the absent case: a function whose declared result is an integer and which returns a negative integer literal on some path, where a local bound from that call (or the call itself) is compared against a negative literal — or the `< 0`/`>= 0` presence guards — is reported at that comparison with `` `find_pin` returns -1 on one path and its result is tested against a negative literal here; return `Option[int]` and test `is None` or use `unwrap_or` ``, and a record field or a parameter compared against `-1` in two or more places is reported once with the same rewrite. The rule is `L_SENTINEL_INT`, report-only under `agent` and blocking under `strict` and `safety`; it is exempt by name for `find` and `find_any`, whose `-1` is their own contract.
- [MUT-PARAM-7] A `mut` argument marker inside a generic function body is transparent to the symbolic checker: the marked value is checked with the type it would have unmarked, so a template that forwards `mut` to a declared `mut` parameter checks and its concrete specializations re-check every call with the declared footprint and full origin analysis. The marker therefore has one spelling and one meaning in an ordinary body and in a template alike.

- [DERIVED-EFFECTS-1] A function's effect ceiling is derived from its parameter list: a `Stream` parameter permits `output`, `Filesystem` `filesystem`, `Network` `network`, `Clock` `clock`, `Entropy` `random`, `Environment` `environment`, `Host` `host`, a `mut` parameter `mutation`, a callback parameter the bounded ceiling its type declares, and `managed_allocation` is always permitted; the ownership a parameter or the result type carries contributes the effect of the resource family it holds, because a value the body may drop is authority the signature already grants. A body that exceeds that ceiling is rejected in that body with `E_EFFECT_BOUND` at the operation or call that reaches the effect, and the message names the parameter to add (`add `out: Stream` to the parameters`). A `host def` or `extern def` has no body to derive from, so its clause stays its ceiling as written, and a callback type whose ceiling is omitted is unbounded rather than a promise: it contributes nothing to the derived set and an invoke through such a value contributes nothing either, because the value's own signature is where that authority is declared.
- [DERIVED-EFFECTS-2] `noio` and `effects(...)` narrow the derived ceiling; `noalloc` is the Core warning contract in [FRONTEND-CORE-NOALLOC-1]. A clause that permits exactly what the parameters imply — `effects(host, managed_allocation)` on a `Host` parameter, or `noio` on a body with no io-capable parameter — is `W_DERIVED_EFFECTS` at the clause, whose message quotes the clause and says to remove it; `noalloc` is not a derived-effects restatement. The warning blocks under `lint --profile=strict`, where it is the `derived-effects` rule.
- [DERIVED-EFFECTS-3] A clause that names an effect no parameter can supply is `E_DERIVED_EFFECTS` at the clause, because it can never be satisfied: the message lists every unsupplied effect and the parameter paste that would supply each one (`add `fs: Filesystem` to the parameters or drop the clause`). Only an explicit `effects(...)` list can name one; `noio` withholds effects and is checked for restating the derived set; written `noalloc` is the separate Core warning contract.
- [DERIVED-EFFECTS-4] The published `ceiling` is the derived set narrowed by an explicit clause, for a declaration whose body was never checked as much as for a checked one: `list-interfaces` prints `ceiling(host,managed_allocation)` from the parameter list alone beside `analysis(declaration-only)`, the contract manifest's `permissions` and the public schema publish it, and `interface_sha256` carries it in place of the inferred mask. The published `effects` fact stays the checked body fact, so a body-only edit that adds an allocation inside the derived ceiling changes `body_sha256` and leaves every declaration interface and caller type check untouched.
- [UNUSED-CAPABILITY-1] A capability parameter the body never reads is `E_UNUSED_CAPABILITY` at the parameter, replacing the `W_UNUSED_BINDING` it used to be: the message names the parameter and says the signature claims an effect the body cannot have (`remove the parameter or use it`). Reading it at all counts as use, including handing it to another call, so a frame that only forwards its `Host` keeps it; `Stream` and `Host` are covered like every other capability, and a `host def`/`extern def` declaration, which has no body, is exempt.

## Self-hosting oracle

- [SELFHOST-LEXER-DUMP-1] `mylang dump-tokens <file.my>` lexes exactly one file and writes a single-line `tokens-v1` JSON document to stdout with the compiler's own token stream: a `version` string, the `path`, a `count`, and one record per token in source order carrying the exact `@tagName` kind spelling, the half-open byte `start`/`end`, and 1-based `line`/`column`. It stops at the lexer — it never loads imports, parses, checks or lowers — and it is the stage-0 reference a differential self-host lexer is compared against. A lexical failure prints the ordinary diagnostics to stderr, writes no partial document, and exits 1.

## Contracts

- [CONTRACT-SYNTAX-1] `@requires(cond[, "message"])` and `@ensures(cond[, "message"])` attach to a `def` with a body — top-level, a domain member (public or `private`), generic or not — and `@invariant(cond[, "message"])` attaches to a non-generic `record` or `domain`. Each is repeatable and checked in source order; the message is a plain string literal. The clause on any other declaration (`class`, `union`, `enum`, `result`, a type alias, a constraint, a `const`, an `extern` or `host` def, a generic record) is `E_CONTRACT_TARGET`, and inside a domain body only contract clauses attach to a member (`E_ATTRIBUTE_TARGET`). A condition that is not `bool` is `E_TYPE`. The condition is part of the parsed declaration: the project merge relocates it with its module and the persisted frontend syntax cache stores it.
- [CONTRACT-REQUIRES-1] A `@requires` condition is checked at function entry, after the parameters are bound and before the body, in a scope holding only the parameters (plus module constants and functions). A false one traps `R_REQUIRES` located at the clause; the report names the function, the clause text and its message, says `precondition violated`, and names the caller that broke it — the next frame out — as `broken by the caller NAME at PATH:LINE:COLUMN`.
- [CONTRACT-ENSURES-1] An `@ensures` condition is checked at every normal return, including the implicit end of a procedure, in a scope holding only the parameters (a parameter's value at exit, so a `mut` parameter shows the body's writes), `result` (the returned value) and `old(...)`. A function returning `Result[T, E]` checks it only on an `Ok` return, with `result` the `T` payload; an `Err` return and a `?` that propagates one skip it. A false one traps `R_ENSURES` located at the clause. In a function with `@ensures`, `result` names the returned value, so a parameter named `result` is `E_CONTRACT_RESULT`, as is `result` in the postcondition of a function that returns no value; a body local named `result` is not visible to the clause and stays legal.
- [CONTRACT-OLD-1] `old(expr)` inside `@ensures` is evaluated once at entry, after the preconditions, in the parameter scope, and the postcondition reads the captured value. It is `E_CONTRACT_OLD` anywhere else — in `@requires`, in `@invariant`, in a body — nested inside another `old`, or with other than one argument. Under default value semantics, a captured collection value is an independent snapshot and `old(xs)` is accepted.
- [CONTRACT-PURE-1] A contract condition — every `@requires`, `@ensures` and `@invariant` clause and each `old(...)` capture — may allocate but has no other effect. The effect pass judges it with the same inference it runs for every function: an effect of its own node or of any function it calls (`output`, `filesystem`, `network`, `clock`, `random`, `environment`, `host`, `mutation`, a callback's declared ceiling) is `E_CONTRACT_EFFECT` naming the effect and the operation it comes from. The checker also rejects, as `E_CONTRACT_EFFECT`, a `mut` call-site marker, an assignment, a collection method that changes its receiver, `?`, and a condition that would take ownership of a move-only or result-bearing value, since a condition only observes. `debug(...)` contributes no effect, and a list a literal or comprehension builds is the condition's own.
- [CONTRACT-INVARIANT-1] An `@invariant` condition, which sees only `self`, is checked wherever a value of its type is built or changed: every record construction (named, with defaults), every `replace(...)`, after every field write to a value of that type (`r.f = v`, `r.f += v`, a destructuring target, and a nested `outer.inner.f = v`, which checks `inner`'s type), and every `wrap[D](v)` of a domain; a domain invariant may `unwrap(self)`. A plain copy is not re-checked. A false one traps `R_INVARIANT` at the construction or write that produced the value, naming the type, the clause text and its message.
- [CONTRACT-TRAP-1] A violated contract ends the program like any other trap, with exit status 1, in every build mode and backend — `run`, `mylang test`, a Debug or ReleaseFast build, the fast frame-map protocol and `--release` (LLVM); no mode removes a contract. The runtime reports the contract's own code (`R_REQUIRES`, `R_ENSURES`, `R_INVARIANT`) where a plain `assert` reports `R_ASSERT`, the structured trap record carries the same text as its `operation`, a `--diagnostics=json` record adds a `contract` object with `kind`, `subject`, `clause`, the optional `message` and, for a precondition, the `caller` location, and `mylang test` prints `CODE: text` beside the frames.
- [CONTRACT-TRAP-2] Lowering preserves assertion kind separately from message text and calls a dedicated native entry for each contract kind. An ordinary `assert` always traps `R_ASSERT`, even when its literal message starts with `R_REQUIRES: `, `R_ENSURES: ` or `R_INVARIANT: `; its full message is preserved in accounted and fast runtime protocols.
- [CONTRACT-INTERFACE-1] Contracts are part of the published interface. `mylang list-interfaces` prints each exported declaration's clauses after its signature, the `--contracts` manifest carries them as `contracts`, `mylang context` shows them above the signature, a declaration's `interface_sha256` covers their normalized text, so `api-diff` classifies a contract edit as a breaking interface change, and a `mylang test` receipt's implementation hash folds in the text of every contract clause its body checks, including an invariant written on another declaration.
- [CONTRACT-CACHE-1] Every cache sees an edit to a contract clause: a function's semantic-cache region and dependency context start at its first attribute line and collect the names its clauses reference, a record's or domain's declaration fingerprint frames its `@invariant` lines, and the native object and metadata caches key on the emitted code and messages. Editing only one `@requires` makes the next `run` and `mylang test` over the same caches trap under the new clause, with no stale body, object or receipt reused.
- [CONTRACT-CACHE-2] Invariant checks synthesized inside a function retain only spans inside that function's declaration region, including operator positions and generated locals. Unchanged bodies constructing invariant-carrying values can be reused after source relocation; edits to a referenced invariant still invalidate them.

## Value semantics (default, D9)



- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-1] Value semantics is the only model. The one-release legacy-sharing mode is retired: `--legacy-sharing` and `--value-semantics` are refused (NEW-CORE-S5-RETIRED-1), the front end has no mode switch and reads no `MYLANG_VALUE_SEMANTICS`, and `"value_semantics"` in `mylang.json` stays an optional boolean (a non-boolean or repeated one is `E_PROJECT_CONFIG`) whose `false` a build refuses as unsupported.
- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-2] Under default value semantics a `list`, `dict`, `set` or `byte_buffer`, and every record, tuple, union payload, `Option`/`Result` or fixed array holding one, is an independent value. Assignment to a named local (a `for` variable included), a store into a record field, tuple, payload, fixed-array element, list element (`append`, `insert`, `xs[i] = v`), dictionary value (`d[k] = v`, `setdefault`), the elements an `extend`, `update` or keyed `sorted` takes from another collection, a named `match` payload binding of a value another name holds, the default a `get` or `pop` may hand back, and a `return` each give the destination its own value: lowering places one `value_copy` after every such transfer that is not fresh (a literal, constructor, call result, `pop`, `escape`), and the ownership analysis (`OWNERSHIP-ANALYSIS-1`) drops it where the transfer is a move (the source is never read again) or a borrow (neither side is written while both are read). A local that may hold a shared value — a parameter, a compiler temporary filled from a place, a local some store filled with a borrow — never hands its value over. A shallow copy, slice or concatenation of a collection whose elements hold collections copies those elements too. A read-only argument or other operand is lent, not copied: the callee cannot write it, and where a later operand of the same expression may write it, it is copied when it is evaluated instead (`VALUE-SEMANTICS-14`). Every real copy shallow-copies its outer value into private scratch and marks each directly reachable mutable collection payload shared: fixed flat values mark up to eight leaves inline, while dynamic and recursive shapes use rooted `myl_qbe_cow_transfer`. A later write clones shared storage in its payload's owning region; a pinned payload is cloned at transfer. When an inner arena is open around a write to an outer value, using the owner's region prevents the replacement from dangling at arena exit. Immutable `str` and `bytes` stay shared; `class` objects and Cells exist only under `--legacy-sharing`. A copy is not a `managed_allocation` effect in this stage, and `.api` interface files do not change.
- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-14] Under default value semantics the operands of one expression evaluate left to right, and each operand's value is fixed when it is evaluated. A lent read-only operand (`VALUE-SEMANTICS-2`) whose root local a later operand of the same expression may write in place — a nested `mut` argument, a mutating collection method such as `pop`, `append` or `clear`, or a store in a lowered block — is copied right after it is evaluated: `lst(xs, xs.pop())` passes the three-element list, `lst(ys, push(mut ys))` the list before the push, and `out.print(zs, push(mut zs))` prints `zs` as it was. This covers the arguments of direct, module-qualified and function-value calls (positional, or keyword arguments staged in source order), `print` operands, both operands of a comparison or other binary operator, the base of `replace(...)` and of a fixed-array read (pending writes whose receiver overlaps an evaluated `mut` argument are rejected by MUT-OVERLAP-RECEIVER-1), the arguments of a collection method, and a run of source-order temporaries such as `zip`'s two sources or membership's needle. A read-only collection method with arguments whose receiver is a named local (`d.get(k, f(mut d))`, `xs.index(g(mut xs))`) reads a copy of the receiver taken before its arguments evaluate, as `COW-READ-RECEIVER-1` already captures a receiver a compiler temporary holds. The copy only marks the payload shared under the collector or takes an owner under reference counting; the later write clones it. The check is static and per expression, following compiler temporaries to the local they read: a root no later operand can write, a fresh operand or a value without a mutable collection needs nothing, and nothing is emitted for it.
- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-3] A declared `mut` parameter receives caller storage: scalar or whole-value rebinding and nested ordinary fields or collection elements update that storage. Its argument must be a writable variable, field or indexed element rooted in one. Function values and lambdas preserve this capability through exact callback mut positions. Readonly loop and match bindings cannot be mut targets.
- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-11] An explicit `mut` parameter can receive a whole writable variable or an interior field or element. Captured target indices evaluate once in written argument order, and whole-value rebinding writes back to that same target. Scalar-only records and fixed arrays follow the same caller-storage contract as other ordinary values.
- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-10] Under default value semantics, a `class` declaration is `E_VALUE_CLASS` even if unused: shared mutable identity must be expressed as a pool handle or other explicit id, while finite value trees use records or recursive unions. The builtin `Cell[T]` is `E_VALUE_CELL` both as a type in a signature or field and as a constructor, because it is a shared mutable slot; use a value record with `mut` and store-back, or a pool handle for identity. A user-declared type named `Cell` is ordinary and remains legal. Under `--legacy-sharing`, class and builtin Cell keep their previous behavior during the migration window. Arenas and `escape` continue to work for value trees.
- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-4] A value copied into an explicit `mut` local is an independent writable snapshot: `mut copy = xs; copy.append(1)` does not change `xs`. Ordinary parameters, const bindings, loop binders and match payload binders are transitively readonly and reject writes with `E_IMMUTABLE`. Copying one into a mut local permits deliberate private updates. Updating an unbound returned temporary rejects with `E_ASSIGNMENT_TARGET`; bind it first to retain its updated value.
- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-5] Under default value semantics `E_MUTATION_OVERLAP` is never reported between a read-only and a `mut` argument: a read-only argument is a value of its own. Where one could share storage with a `mut` argument of the same call — the same root local or an untraceable temporary — lowering passes a real copy taken before the call, so `touch(mut a, a)` gives the callee a snapshot of `a`. A read-only argument evaluated after a `mut` operand may mark its payload shared; the call makes that `mut` operand unique again before pinning it, and writes the replacement back to its owning place. Two `mut` arguments of one call may not name one value or overlapping places: that is `E_MUTATION_OVERLAP` under MUT-EXCLUSIVE-1, with or without the switch.
- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-6] A declared mut parameter may remain unused or may be copied into private writable storage. Changes to that private snapshot stay private unless explicitly stored back through the parameter. For example, `mut row = rows[i]; smooth(mut row)` leaves the caller row unchanged until `rows[i] = row`; retaining the mut parameter capability is legal.
- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-7] Under default value semantics `old(expr)` of a value whose writable storage is all collections (a `list`, `dict`, `set`, `byte_buffer` or a record of them) is accepted: the capture is a value of its own.
- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-8] Documentation examples use the default value model. `# doc: value-semantics` explicitly selects that model and `# doc: legacy-sharing` selects the migration release's legacy model, for both checking and execution, including grouped projects. The doctest runner passes the corresponding CLI mode flag to child commands; contradictory directives fail through the CLI's multiple-mode rejection.






- [COW-MUT-ARGUMENT-1] A nested `mut` collection argument retains its selected writing place while later arguments are evaluated. Its copy-on-write replacement is published into that place, read-only same-call arguments remain independent snapshots, and field/key/index evaluation is not repeated. Normal and failed calls release all temporary pins and holds.







- [COW-READ-RECEIVER-1] A collection read captures its receiver value before evaluating its index or other arguments. A later callback that mutates the source cannot change that captured value or the length used to normalize a negative index. Capturing a pinned receiver creates a private copy; writing receivers obey MUT-OVERLAP-RECEIVER-1. GC and RC preserve the same evaluation order and results.
- [D1-LIVE-PLACE] Iteration over a place rooted at a local or parameter sees element writes and mut-callee writes through that place, including record-field write-back, fixed-array places, comprehensions and generators. Produced expressions, filters and nested clauses retain the same live-source rule. Indices are evaluated once at entry; every indexed managed ancestor's length is checked exactly each step, including ancestors of immutable str/bytes. A live list or byte buffer sees growth and traps a decrease from its last observed length, including after earlier growth; dictionaries, sets and immutable str/bytes terminals trap any observed length change. Same-length str/bytes field replacement is visible; text length and indexing count Unicode scalars. Fixed-array terminal lengths are static. Direct rebinding of the root in the body selects an independent entry snapshot; non-place sources keep their entry behavior. Guards run before each element and after each continued or final iteration, while break exits without another guard.








- [MUT-REBIND-CALLER-1] Whole-variable rebinding of any ordinary mut parameter reaches its caller storage, including scalar, fixed-array, record and managed-collection values. Updates through nested targets and recursive forwarding retain this behavior, while readonly inputs and named snapshots stay independent.













- [MUT-EXPRESSION-STACK-1] A transparent `mut` argument marker consumes exactly one checked target expression, regardless of whether that target lowers to an indexed read, a field or a synthesized block. Nested dictionary mutation arguments compose with later call arguments without checker stack underflow, while missing-marker and nonassignable-target diagnostics remain unchanged.

- [RECURSIVE-FILE-CONTRACT-1] Recursive immutable box admissibility detects builtin `File` and `TextFile` resource leaves through records, variants and nonempty fixed arrays, including declaration-order and generic wrappers, and rejects them with the existing `E_RECURSIVE_RECORD` rule before execution. Zero-length resource arrays contain no obligation; ordinary scoped file handling and acyclic resource/Result transfers keep their existing contracts.





















- [RC-ITERATION-HOLD-TRANSFER-1] An entry-snapshot iteration source remains an observer when a named field transfer would change its stored value. A D1 live place reads its root on each step, so field transfers preserve independent values while record-field write-back stays visible to the loop.



















## Compile-time file embedding

- [EMBED-1] `embed_bytes("path")` answers `bytes` and `embed_str("path")` answers `str`, both read from a project file while the program loads, so the data is immutable static content of the binary. They are prelude builtins that a binding, declaration or module name may shadow. Each takes exactly one plain string literal (a variable, `b"..."`, a second argument or a computed path is `E_EMBED_ARGUMENT`), and `embed_str` requires valid UTF-8 (`E_EMBED_UTF8` names the offending byte offset). A call needs no capability parameter and contributes no effect, not even `managed_allocation`, so it is legal under `noio`, `noalloc` and `effects()`, in generic bodies, and as the whole initializer of a module `const` whose type is `bytes` for `embed_bytes` and `str` for `embed_str`; reading that constant, locally or through an import, loads static data and allocates nothing. Every byte is kept, including NUL, and an empty file is an empty value. Native embedded objects are ordered by semantic symbol in both whole-program emission and module linking. Behavior is identical under reference counting, `--gc-runtime` and `--release`.
- [EMBED-2] An embedded path is relative to the directory of the source file spelling the call and uses `/`. Absolute paths are `E_EMBED_ABSOLUTE`, an empty path or one holding NUL or a backslash is `E_EMBED_PATH`, and a path that leaves the project root through `..` or through a symbolic link is `E_EMBED_ESCAPE`, decided before the target is probed. The project root is the directory of the nearest `mylang.json` above the entry, else the entry's own import directory. A missing file is `E_EMBED_MISSING`, a directory or other non-regular file `E_EMBED_DIRECTORY`, an unreadable file `E_EMBED_UNREADABLE`, a file over 16 MiB `E_EMBED_TOO_LARGE`, and a source that was not loaded from a directory `E_EMBED_UNAVAILABLE`; each is located at the string literal. A symbolic link that stays inside the root is followed.
- [EMBED-3] The loader finds every `embed_bytes("...")` and `embed_str("...")` call while it reads a module's header, reads each file once with the project provider, and records its outcome and BLAKE3 content digest on the module's source, so the checker only looks results up and a failed read is an ordinary diagnostic. That digest enters every key that covers the module: the configured identity the frontend verdict, in-memory verdict, run-manifest and emit-replay caches key on; the dependency context of each declaration whose region holds an embed, which keys checked bodies, check summaries, stand-in units and stored fragments; the implementation hash of a native test; and the test snapshot's resolved identity and materialized copy, where captured files sit at the paths their relative references need. Editing only an embedded file therefore rechecks and re-emits exactly the declarations that embed it, an unchanged file reuses everything, and deleting or replacing a file with a directory changes the keys as well.
- [EMBED-5] A `project-dependencies-v1` receipt lists every embedded file as `{module, path, status, blake3, bytes}` beside the module inputs and `--explain-invalidation` reports an edit to one at the `load_source` stage. `mylang fmt` leaves an embed call as written, and `mylang check` and `check-session` report embed diagnostics and never replay a verdict across an edit, deletion or replacement of an embedded file. An interface file (`mylang api`) prints an embedded constant as its initializer text, `embed_bytes("path")`, never the file's bytes, so editing the file leaves the interface unchanged.

## Typing papercuts

- [DISCARD-NAME-1] `_` is the discard name and never shadows anything. A `for` target (`for _ in ...`, `for _, x in ...`, `for _, _ in ...`, the second name of `enumerate`, `zip` and dictionary items included), a comprehension element name and a `with ... as _` scope may bind `_` again inside another scope that binds it, so `for _ in range(2):` around `for _ in range(3):` is legal, and a loop that discards both of its names binds nothing. The inner `_` hides the outer one for its own scope and the outer one is visible again after it. `_` does not become an error to read where it is bound today, and a `match` payload of `_` still binds nothing. Every other name keeps the rule: a `for` variable, comprehension element or payload binding may not reuse an active local or top-level name (`E_SHADOWING`), because loop variables are block-scoped.
- [OPTION-EQ-1] In `left == right` and `left != right`, a `Some(...)`, `None`, `Ok(...)` or `Err(...)` operand that has no expected type takes the type of the other operand, in either order and whatever that operand is (a call, a method call, a conversion such as `int(text)`, a field or a local), in ordinary and generic function bodies. The other operand is checked first; the typed comparison keeps the source order and operands still evaluate left to right. When both operands wait for a type (`Some(1) == Some(1)`) nothing is inferred and `E_OPTION_INFERENCE` or `E_RESULT_INFERENCE` still reports it; a payload of the wrong type (`half(4) == Some("a")`) is `E_TYPE`. `x is None` and `x == None` agree on a known `Option`. Each equality link in a comparison chain uses the same peer inference, checking a shared operand once and retaining left-to-right runtime evaluation and short-circuiting, in ordinary and generic bodies.
- [CONST-METHOD-1] `NAME.method(args)` on a module constant declared `str` or `bytes` is checked as the same method on a local bound to the constant: the constant is read once through its getter and the method applies to that value, in ordinary and generic function bodies, for an `embed_bytes`/`embed_str` constant, for a private constant of the same module and for a public one reached through an import (`alias.NAME.method(args)`). The method vocabulary, arities, diagnostics and effects are the local receiver's: a literal constant contributes `managed_allocation`, an embedded one none. A constant of another type has no methods.
- [CONST-FLOAT-1] A module `const` may be declared `float` (or an alias of it), with an initializer that is a float literal, an integer literal (which takes the float type from the declaration), another visible constant, a parenthesized expression, unary `-`, or arithmetic and comparison over those. The constant folds at check time and every read is the same wrapped binary64 literal a float literal checks to, in ordinary and generic bodies, through an import and under `noalloc`. `-0.0` keeps its sign, an overflowing literal is `E_FLOAT_RANGE`, a non-float initializer for a `float` constant (or a float one for an `int` constant) is `E_TYPE`, and the interface listing prints the initializer as written.
- [CONST-FLOAT-2] A constant initializer folds float operations exactly as the checked program computes them, so a constant and the same expression evaluated at run time are the same bits (a NaN is the canonical NaN). An integer operand beside a float, and both operands of `/`, convert to the nearest binary64 (ties to even; `u64` as unsigned) and divide as floats; `+`, `-`, `*`, `/` are separate IEEE operations; `//` floors the quotient and `%` is `x - floor(x / y) * y`; comparisons are numeric (`-0.0 == 0.0`, every comparison with NaN is false except `!=`). `float(x)`, `int(x)`, `sqrt`, `floor`, `ceil`, `trunc` and `abs` fold when no declaration of the same name claims them. Bitwise and shift operators on floats are `E_FLOAT_OPERATION`, and compile-time source function calls still take and return integers and `bool` only.
- [CONST-FLOAT-3] A constant initializer converts a float to an integer type by truncating toward zero, as `int(x)` does: `int(PI * 100.0)`. NaN, an infinity or a value the target type cannot hold is `E_CONSTANT_ARITHMETIC`, located at the conversion.

- [FLOAT-INTEGER-CAST-1] `i8`/`i16`/`i32`/`i64` and `u8`/`u16`/`u32`/`u64` accept a `float` operand in ordinary and generic bodies. Every conversion truncates toward zero first, then checks the exact half-open integer range before converting: `i8(-128.5)` is -128 and `u8(-0.5)` is 0. NaN, either infinity and a truncated value outside the target's range trap `R_FLOAT_CONVERSION` at the conversion. `int(float)` is the same checked signed 64-bit conversion as `i64(float)`; no out-of-range floating-to-integer conversion reaches native or LLVM arithmetic.

- [PY-BYTES-SORT-1] `sorted` and list `.sort()` accept bytes elements and bytes-valued scalar or flat tuple keys in ordinary and generic bodies, comparing unsigned octets and shorter prefixes first, stably in both directions. Existing str and integer/float key encodings and the keyed-sort ABI remain unchanged; the formerly invalid nibble 7 denotes an eight-byte bytes pointer.
- [PY-TEXT-TRIM-1] `str` and `bytes` accept `strip(chars)`, `lstrip(chars)` and `rstrip(chars)` with a same-family set, duplicates ignored and an empty set removing nothing. No-argument str trim uses CPython Unicode16.0.0 whitespace; bytes trims six ASCII whitespace octets. Trimming reads scalar/octet boundaries linearly, leaves aliases unchanged and preserves indexed/view behavior; a large charset's temporary membership storage obeys the memory budget.
- [PY-TEXT-RANGE-1] `find`, `rfind`, `index`, `rindex`, `count`, `startswith` and `endswith` on str and bytes accept `sub[, start[, end]]`, negative bounds count from the end, end clamps and a positive start beyond length invalidates the window. Text indices count scalars and byte indices count octets; search answers are global indices, count is nonoverlapping, and an empty needle matches every boundary in a valid window. Find/index return start, reverse search returns end, count is end-start+1 and affixes match; invalid/reversed windows yield -1, a checked miss, zero or False. Reverse substring search is constant-space and linear, including periodic needles; no temporary text view is built for a window.
- [PY-TEXT-RANGE-2] A missing str/bytes `index` or `rindex` traps `R_SUBSTRING_NOT_FOUND` at the call; `find` and `rfind` return -1. List.index retains its separate element and R_LIST_VALUE contract.
- [PRELUDE-REPR-1] The shadowable one-argument `repr` builtin answers exact CPython scalar spelling for str, bytes, every integer width, float and bool, with Unicode16.0.0 quote/printability/escape rules and shortest binary64 exponent layout, including -0.0 and unsigned64 maximum. The operand evaluates once and unsupported collections/records are E_REPR_TYPE. Ordinary print/str formatting stays separate; a large non-ASCII repr result carries the TEXT-INDEX position index and inputs/views are rooted across its single result allocation.

## Tooling and GC continuation

- [CLI-CACHE-ISOLATION-1] Native artifact location is independent of frontend reuse: `build --no-cache --cache-dir DIR` uses DIR, `test --no-cache` stages native objects inside its owned scratch snapshot, and doctest keeps all scratch and child caches under TMPDIR (or the host temporary directory), without writing the documentation root or caller's default cache.
- [MEMORY-HOST-GUEST-1] Native guests restore the original caller's soft address-space ceiling only when the inherited soft limit still equals the limit this compiler installed; the inherited hard limit is preserved. The guest then installs its own max-memory ceiling, so a larger guest budget can exceed MYLANG_HOST_MEMORY_LIMIT without loosening the compiler, build tools or external caller limits. Frame-budget preflight uses that same matching receipt and restored caller ceiling, while a real external ceiling still produces a refusal before building. Unaddressable frame reservations are refused without overflow.
- [ITERATION-FIELD-SNAPSHOT-1] A last-read inline record field transferred into a named value while a loop retains that field's original header must preserve an independent loop snapshot; rebinding the record after mutating the transferred value does not raise R_LIST_ITERATION_MUTATION. This rule applies to both RC and GC.

- [EMBED-CONTEXT-1] Navigation and agent-context snapshots include each embedded file's module, path, read status, byte count and BLAKE3 identity. Context rechecks actual held bytes and, when supplied, the loader's embedded-file provider before publishing facts; a changed outcome or same-length edit rejects the answer as stale, and a missing recheck callback is unconfirmed. Partial answers retain these identities. Budgeted answers name omitted embedded rows, while projects with no embeds retain their existing wire envelope and snapshot digest.
- [EMBED-PROJECT-BUDGET-1] Project max_file_bytes and max_total_bytes bound embedded inputs as well as source inputs. The loader passes each remaining read allowance to the provider before allocation, charges each successful module/path embedded identity once against the total, and reports E_PROJECT_BYTES at the embed literal when a project allowance is exceeded. The independent 16 MiB embed cap retains E_EMBED_TOO_LARGE when it is the limiting bound.












### RC-NATIVE-VARIANT-BACKEND-1 — checked native variant implementation policy


The policy is a versioned emission input, included before fragment, whole-manifest
replay, project/standard module variant and imported emission-config lookup.
Selective accounted native-test compilation carries the policy before producing
a manifest; native test result identities include it. External manifest input
performs no new proof admission or instruction transformation. The policy changes
no source semantics, runtime contract, guard, ownership permission or opcode.




## New compiler core (S0)

The separate `mylang-core` executable (`zig build core`, `docs/design/new-core-ir.md`) builds a program from the checked tree through the Core IR and LLVM, beside the old core.

- [NEW-CORE-FRONTEND-1] `mylang-core` loads an entry path as the module named by its file stem under its directory (a header-less file is checked alone), through the unchanged front end, and maps a merged-source offset back to the file, 1-based line and column it came from.
- [NEW-CORE-FRONTEND-CACHE-1] New-core build and test sessions reuse the shared versioned syntax, checked-body and fact-summary stores under their cache root. Complete typed requests explicitly opt into exact checked-body hydration even when fact summaries are attached; legacy facts requests preserve their existing check and retention policy. Validation-only checks may stand in unchanged bodies. Changed bodies and fresh declaration/effect/mutation dependencies remain authoritative, and cold checking or a damaged/unwritable store preserves the same checked trees and diagnostics. `mylang-core check --typed` measures complete typed checking separately from validation-only checking, with optional actual-work and phase receipts.
- [NEW-CORE-PROJECT-FRONTEND-1] The new-core session accepts strict project loader budgets, an explicit nested entry module and caller-validated source providers over exact relative module paths.
