Provider bytes obey the fresh source budget, the test suffix appends only to the canonical entry path, and the fresh interface index is available for policy/interface validation. Its generated compiler identity covers all production compiler/runtime/property adapters and the toolchain. Legacy and new command frontends use the same exported strict project-configuration module, preserving one configuration and budget type identity.
- [NEW-CORE-IR-1] The Core IR builder turns checked loops, branches, option matches and short-circuit `and`/`or` into SSA: each loop gets header, body, latch and exit blocks, loop-carried locals become phis in the header, and every checked arithmetic, conversion and list read keeps an exact check.
- [NEW-CORE-IR-2] A construct the new core does not support yet stops the build with `E_CORE_UNSUPPORTED`, naming the construct and the source position where it occurs; nothing is emitted.
- [NEW-CORE-IR-3] A `for` loop over a list place follows live-place iteration (D1): refreshed SSA reads observe writes, and each step checks the current length against the entry length. Read-only loops borrow at entry and omit the check; syntactic root rebinding selects an entry snapshot.
- [NEW-CORE-LLVM-1] LLVM emission lowers exact checks to overflow intrinsics and range tests that branch, weighted cold, to `myl_core_trap` with a site index; the site table records trap kind, line, column, width, operation and function, and the C entry builds `main`'s arguments and exits with its result's low 8 bits.
- [NEW-CORE-DRIVER-1] The native driver compiles the runtime once per runtime content into the cache directory, compiles the program's LLVM units to objects and links them with the runtime through `zig cc` (-O0 for debug, -O3 for the host CPU for release); a compiler or linker that cannot run or fails ends the build with its output reported.
- [NEW-CORE-FACTS-1] A release build proves, per function: a counted loop's counter stays in `[start, bound]`; a list filled by a counted loop from 0 with one push per iteration has length `max(bound, 0)` and elements in the hull of the pushed values; after such a list exists its bound is at most 2^47 (one-byte elements in the 47-bit address space); a reduction `acc += s` with non-negative `s` stays within `init + trips * s`; and a read under `index < bound` of that list is in range. Checks those intervals rule out (overflow, floor division and remainder of non-negative operands, conversions, list bounds) are not emitted, comparisons they decide fold to constants, and element ranges become `!range` metadata; debug builds keep every check.
- [NEW-CORE-FACTS-3] Platform rule (L4): a list has at most 2^47 elements, because x86-64 Linux gives a process a 47-bit user address space and every element occupies at least one byte; the runtime's allocator never asks for a mapping above 2^47 (no LA57 address hint). The fact pass uses this as its only assumption: once a list of length `n` exists, `n ≤ 2^47`.
- [NEW-CORE-FACTS-2] A list whose length or elements are unknown (a parameter) keeps its bounds check, and a sum whose terms are unbounded keeps its overflow check; ranges that do not reach a fixed point within the iteration limit prove nothing and leave the emitter no interval (`!range`, `nuw`, narrowed conversions).
- [NEW-CORE-FACTS-4] Threshold widening: a phi whose interval keeps changing (after eight changes) widens each growing bound to the nearest of 0, 2^47 (NEW-CORE-FACTS-3) and 2^47 + 1 that still holds it, else to its type's limit, and keeps a bound that stays inside; values computed from phis keep their exact transfer. A binary search `while low < high: middle = (low + high) // 2 …` over a list parameter thereby gets `0 <= low, high <= 2^47`: `low + high` and `middle + 1` cannot overflow, `middle >= 0` decides the negative-index normalization, and the floor division is unsigned.
- [NEW-CORE-FACTS-5] Length bounds: the fact pass tracks `value <= len(p)` (or `<`) for a list parameter `p` (one immutable list for the whole call): `len(p)`, value-preserving conversions, `x + c` with `c <= 0`, `x + 1` under `x < len(p)`, `x // d` of a non-negative `x`, the midpoint `(x + y) // 2` under a dominating `x < y` (strictly below `y`), and phis whose live operands share the bound. A read `p[i]` with `i >= 0` and `i < len(p)` by such a bound or by a dominating `i < y` (`y <= len(p)`) needs no bounds check; `high = len(p) + 1`, `low <= high` or a bound from another list keep it.
- [NEW-CORE-INLINE-1] A release build inlines every call to an internal function whose body is one block ending in `return value` with no call or print, before the fact pass, so the helper's arithmetic is analysed (and its checks proven) in the caller; trap positions stay the helper's lines. Debug builds keep the calls. Caller facts and private trap-proof trials are reclaimed from independent scratch per caller, while cloned instructions and inline ancestry remain owned by the result arena.
- [NEW-CORE-VECTOR-1] A release build lowers a counted loop whose body is one straight chain of proven `+ - *`, divisions and remainders by positive constants and reads `xs[counter]` of a list built before the loop, summed into one accumulator, with every per-element value non-negative and below 2^16 (or 2^32), to a narrow-lane AVX2 loop over `floor(trips / lanes) * lanes` elements in front of the unchanged scalar loop, which finishes the rest from the vector part's counter and partial sum: `packusdw` loads of the i64 storage, a multiply-high reciprocal checked exhaustively over the proven dividend range, and `pmaddwd` into 32-bit lanes folded into the 64-bit sum before they can overflow. Verify builds never vectorize.
- [NEW-CORE-VECTOR-2] A select arm or operand defined inside the loop but outside the lane body (the counter itself, `v = j`) keeps the loop scalar. The vector lowering flattens an `if`/`else` (either arm may be empty) whose arms are single blocks into lane selects, running both proven, trap-free arms on every lane; a `list[bool]` condition loads as a byte mask permuted exactly like the `packusdw` order of the `i64` lists in the same loop, so every lane combines values of one element; integer comparisons in lanes are unsigned because every lane value is non-negative.
- [NEW-CORE-VECTOR-3] A vectorized reduction whose body only reads byte storage, converts and `^`s values proven within 0..255 runs in 8-bit lanes (32 per 256-bit vector) and, on x86 AVX2, sums them eight at a time into 64-bit lanes with `psadbw`; on other targets the bytes widen to 64-bit lanes.
- [NEW-CORE-FLOAT-1] The fact pass keeps an interval of finite doubles per float value (one ulp of outward rounding per operation; a possible NaN or infinity has none), proves `int(f)` in range from it, and a release build converts proven-`i32` integers to and from doubles through `i32`, which AVX2 vectorizes.
- [NEW-CORE-VERSION-1] A counted loop from 0 whose reduction `acc += s` (with `s ≥ 0`) the 2^47 rule cannot prove gets a trip-count guard: when `bound ≤ (i64 max − hi(acc0)) / hi(s) − 1`, a copy whose sum is proven runs (and may vectorize); otherwise the original loop with its exact checks. Values the loop defines merge in its exit block.
- [NEW-CORE-UNSWITCH-1] A release build unswitches innermost loops: a branch whose condition compares only values defined outside the loop (or constants) is decided once by a guard before the loop, which enters a copy where the branch is constantly taken or the original where it is constantly not, up to 16 copies per function; values the loop defines merge in its exit block. The compute benchmark's per-element dispatch on `kind` thereby leaves one straight loop per kind, each eligible for facts and vectorization, with the same output.
- [NEW-CORE-TARGET-1] Every target-specific choice of the new core (LLVM triple, CPU flags, vector width, x86 intrinsics, the address-space bound behind the list-length rule, C ABI, trap mechanism) is read from one target description, `src/core/target.zig`; x86 AVX2 intrinsics are used only when it allows them, and the generic LLVM vector code used otherwise (`--generic-vectors` forces it) computes the same results.
- [NEW-CORE-VERIFY-1] `mylang-core build --release --verify-facts` emits every check the facts proved, asserts every fact interval after its definition and that blocks the facts call unreachable never run, gives LLVM no fact-derived promise (`nsw`, `!range`, narrow conversions, vector code), and reports a violated fact as `R_CORE_FACT`; `--sabotage-facts` builds the same with one deliberately wrong rule (`x % c` taken to lie in `[0, c−2]`), which the verify build must catch.
- [NEW-CORE-OWN-1] Lists are values: a `list_push` whose operand may share its block (a parameter, a call result, or a local whose other name or earlier version is used after the push) appends to a fresh copy (`list_copy`), so `copy = xs; copy.append(v)` leaves the caller's `xs` and every other alias unchanged at debug and release; a list built in the function and dead after each push (a comprehension or append loop) is still appended in place.
- [NEW-CORE-LLVM-2] A release build emits a proven `+ - *` whose operands and result the facts prove non-negative as `nuw nsw`, a comparison of two proven non-negative values with unsigned predicates, and proven floor division as `udiv`; verify builds do none of this. Release code generation aligns innermost loops to 64 bytes (the target description's flags), so a small hot loop sits in one fetch and prediction window.
- [NEW-CORE-VERIFY-2] A verify build also asserts every length bound after its definition (`at most` / `below the list length`, trapping `R_CORE_FACT`); `--sabotage-facts=widening` (widening rounds a growing bound to the threshold on the wrong side) and `--sabotage-facts=length` (`len(p)` taken as below itself) build it with that one wrong rule, which binary_search's verify build catches.
- [NEW-CORE-SLICE-1] `mylang-core build` compiles the integer_math slice (`test/core/int_math.my`) at debug and release; its checksums for 4096, 64 and 4100 elements (300 iterations, seed 7) equal the old core's, and invalid arguments exit 2 as they do there.
- [NEW-CORE-SLICE-2] The control-flow program `test/core/control.my` (while, break and continue, floor division and remainder of negative operands, the `int(text)` grammar including overflow, short-circuit operators, boolean printing) prints and exits exactly as the old core's build does, at debug and release.
- [NEW-CORE-KERNELS-1] The S0 compute kernels as single-kernel programs under `test/core` (`float_math`, `call_direct`, `branch_predictable`, `branch_mixed`, `binary_search`, kinds 1, 6, 2, 3 and 10 of `benchmarks/runtime/expanded/compute/main.my`) build at debug and release and print the old core's checksums at 4096 and 64 elements; floats, list[bool] elements, helper calls and a data-dependent while loop over a list parameter are covered.
- [NEW-CORE-TRAP-1] An overflow or list-bounds failure flushes stdout written before it, prints the old core's first stderr line (`path:line:column: error[CODE]: native execution failed (detail)`) and `MYLANG-TRAP` record, and exits 1.
- [NEW-CORE-IR-4] The Core IR's compound types are `list(T)` of any element type, `record(fields)`, `union(cases)` (one payload type or none per case, the tag being the case index in the checker's order, so `Option[T]` is `{none, T}`) and `box(T)`. A record has C layout (its size a multiple of its alignment); a union is an 8-byte tag word followed by its largest payload's memory image in 8-byte words, except that an `Option` of a `str`, list or box is the pointer itself with null for the empty case. A record or union of at most four words travels in registers, a larger one by pointer and `sret`. A value is managed (needs counting) when it is or holds a `str`, list or box. A `list_get_field` path selects record fields and, on a union, the tag (step 0) or case `k`'s payload (step `k + 1`), at most five steps deep.
- [NEW-CORE-RECORD-1] `record_make`, `record_get` and `record_set` are LLVM struct values (`insertvalue`/`extractvalue`); a list of records stores each element inline at the record's stride, loaded and stored field by field, and `list_get_field` reads one field (also of a nested record) with one address computation from the element's slot; a record over four words is passed to an internal function by a read-only pointer and returned through `sret`; output is identical at -O0 and -O3.
- [NEW-CORE-UNION-1] A union value is `{i8 tag, T}` when every case with a payload has the same scalar or pointer type, `{i8 tag, [N x i64]}` holding the payload's memory image otherwise, and a bare pointer for the null niche; `union_tag` and `union_payload` read each form, and a union list element is a tag word plus payload words, whose tag or payload fields `list_get_field` reads directly.
- [NEW-CORE-RC-1] `retain`, `release` and `is_unique` operate on the block header's 32-bit count (TBAA type `myl.count`, distinct from header and element data, and `!range [1, 0)`), with the plain/shared encoding and atomic reads of SHARED-COUNTS-1: a count of `0xFFFFFFFF` is saturated and never written (so immortal string constants stay in read-only memory), `retain` adds one, `release` subtracts one and frees the last owner, and `is_unique` is `count == 1`. Counting a record, union or niche option counts every block it holds: null and payload-free cases, and a case whose payload words hold a non-pointer, are skipped. `list_set` releases the element it replaces; `list_pop` leaves the popped element to the read before it. `box_make` allocates a box block through `myl_core_box_new`.
- [TSAN-BUILD-1] The internal MYLANG_CORE_SANITIZE=thread switch marks every emitted LLVM definition sanitize_thread, instruments the runtime and linked C sources, and links ThreadSanitizer. Runtime, unit, foreign-object, program and replay cache identities distinguish sanitized and ordinary builds; invalid switch values fail before native compilation. The native scheduler and real emitted fastcc mixed-capture/range programs run at eight threads without reports; ordinary outputs and ownership remain unchanged.
- [LAYOUT-SWITCH-1] The internal measurement environment `MYLANG_CORE_LAYOUT=4|5|6` passes `-mllvm -align-all-functions=K` to every emitted-unit and runtime compile. Layout participates in runtime, unit, program and replay cache identities; changing K compiles fresh objects and returning to a previous K reuses them. Other values are rejected before native compilation. An unset switch preserves default flags and cache identities.
- [RUNTIME-THREADS-1] The native allocator, error traces, IO sites, worker stack metadata and counting slots are thread-local. The executable runtime and both LLVM emitter paths use the measured local-exec TLS model. A persistent allocator registry preserves retired-thread free lists; leak, text-walk and counter reports aggregate only after quiescence. Shared heap/live-byte accounting is atomic: before worker publication the sole runtime thread may use atomic load/store for live-block updates; after publication updates use atomic RMW. Quiescent live counts are exact both with allocations outstanding and after worker retirement. After workers start, complete print/write/debug units (including dynamic print options and units above 64 KiB) stage in TLS and append under one stream mutex; before workers start the ordinary buffered path remains. Full provider operations and handle changes serialize under a separate IO mutex. Fatal reporting has one winner, a bounded stream-lock attempt with raw-fd fallback, and skips provider/Host cleanup after workers start. Task reports use captured guest PCs, with `<unknown>` when unavailable. Each worker installs its own alternate signal stack and stack bounds and blocks SIGALRM.
- [SHARED-COUNTS-2] Publication marks every reachable plain managed block shared with an iterative walk before enqueueing a task; shared and immortal roots stop traversal. One child iterator serves publication and destruction, preserving alias multiplicity, union guards, dictionary tombstones, view owners and provider pairs. Plain saturation first shares its descendant graph before becoming permanently immortal, so later publication may safely stop at that root. Counting builds report each newly marked block and its allocated bytes; repeated publication adds neither marks nor owners.
- [TASK-SCHEDULER-1] Structured CPU tasks use a lazy fixed OS-thread pool with Chase–Lev owner deques, round-robin stealing and futex parking. Waiting runs queued work, including the waiter's own deque. The submitting thread participates; first submission starts N−1 workers, and one thread executes synchronously without sharing or retaining the environment. Task results are published with release/acquire synchronization and each result call returns an owner. Group close joins and releases its handle list; finalization also joins. Thread counts are positive MYLANG_THREADS values or affinity count; tests default to one and step/frame budgets force one. Range chunks are zero for empty input, otherwise min(items, 4×threads), with one at one thread. Batch parents cannot start a pool and a process with workers cannot fork tests. Workers inherit the process stack limit (64 MiB if unlimited), have a guard page, and block SIGALRM. Every LLVM unit supplies a C-to-fastcc task bridge; submit and group return PCs identify task trap sites. Generated range thunks update the current item through an internal task-item helper, with nested execution saving and restoring it.
- [SHARED-COUNTS-1] Plain reference counts are 1 through 0x7FFFFFFE; a retain at that limit saturates to immortal 0xFFFFFFFF without storing 0x7FFFFFFF. Shared counts have bit 31 set and 1 through 0x7FFFFFFE owners; shared retains/releases use relaxed/release atomic updates, with an acquire on the last-owner CAS before final destruction and saturation that remains immortal under concurrent updates. Emitted count reads are atomic unordered with unchanged TBAA and nonzero range metadata. Plain retain below 0x7FFFFFFE and plain release below 0x80000000 stay inline; other counts enter one unlikely preserve-most helper call, including immortal counts; the cold helpers handle shared counts and plain saturation and never write immortal counts. Uniqueness remains count == 1, so shared blocks always copy on write, and text caches never write shared or immortal blocks. Runtime count accesses are confined to count helpers and block initialization, checked by compiling a renamed count field in both ordinary and counting runtime source. Shared child destruction stays iterative; counting builds report shared retains/releases and reserve walk-mark/byte slots.
- [NEW-CORE-RC-2] The ownership pass (`own.zig`, after the facts in release builds, after construction in debug builds) gives every in-place list update one copy-on-write form: a list that is owned, dead after the update and statically unique (fresh from `list_new`, a copy or another update, or phis of those, never passed to a call or retained) is updated in place with no test; an owned list that is dead after the update but may be shared tests `count == 1` and otherwise updates a copy after releasing the original; a borrowed list (a parameter or a part of another value) or one still used afterwards is copied first. Results print as under the old core.
- [NEW-CORE-RC-3] Owned values (constructors, updates, calls, phis, copies) are released after their last use, or on the CFG edge where they die (an early return or `?` path included; never on a path into a trap), and their last consuming use moves them; a borrowed value a consuming use takes (stored, returned, a phi operand) is retained first, and a part read from a value that is consumed while the part is still used is retained when read. Records, unions and boxes count every block they hold, a `record_set` releases the field it replaces, the read before a `list_pop` owns the popped element, and lists and boxes whose elements hold blocks carry an element layout (offsets, guarded by union tags) from which the runtime's free releases and its copy retains the children. With `MYL_CORE_LEAKCHECK=1`, a program that returns from `main` with blocks still allocated reports `myl-core leakcheck: N blocks live at exit` on stderr.
- [NEW-CORE-RC-4] The ownership pass emits no cancelling pair by construction, and a final peephole drops any `retain x` followed in the same block by `release x` with only pure reads between them (no call, update, store or release).
- [NEW-CORE-RC-5] A verify build (`--verify-facts`) asserts before every statically unique in-place update that the list's count is 1 and traps `R_CORE_FACT` (`count == 1 before an in-place update`) otherwise.
- [NEW-CORE-RC-6] The runtime frees a block and every block it alone holds iteratively, through a pending chain linked in the dying blocks' headers, so a list-of-lists chain a million deep frees without recursion; a copy of a list retains its elements' blocks; a count that reaches `0xFFFFFFFF` is saturated: it is never written again and the block is never freed.
- [NEW-CORE-RC-7] Every `test/core` program that runs to its end does so with no block live under `MYL_CORE_LEAKCHECK=1`, at debug and release.
- [NEW-CORE-RC-8] A list released at its death whose origin is fresh (an allocation, a copy, an update, or phis of those) and that no call received and no retained use shared is freed without its count test (a `release` with `data = 1`); verify builds assert `count == 1` there and trap `R_CORE_FACT` (`count == 1 before freeing a unique block`). A list a call received keeps the test, since the callee may have kept it.
- [NEW-CORE-STACK-1] An optimized build puts a list on the stack when it is built in the function from `list_new` by a straight chain of pushes or by a counted fill loop the facts recognize, its elements hold no blocks, every other use of it only reads it (length, element or field reads, or the proven borrowing calls of NEW-CORE-STACK-2: no return, store, other phi or update), and its capacity (the number of pushes, or the upper end of the loop bound's interval) takes at most 4 KiB of elements, within the function budget of NEW-CORE-STACK-3: the block is an entry-block `alloca` with the same header, set up where the `list_new` runs, and is never counted, released, freed or grown (its pushes skip the growth test). Verify builds assert before each such push that the length is below the capacity and trap `R_CORE_FACT`. Output and the leak count are unchanged; unoptimized builds promote nothing.
- [NEW-CORE-STACK-2] A bounded list may remain on the stack across a direct internal call only when every occurrence is an ordinary parameter proven to use that list exclusively for unmanaged length, element or field reads, or forwarding to another proven borrowing parameter. Proofs resolve copies and propagate through known calls without syntax or ABI changes; returned or stored aliases, managed phis, mutations, ownership transfer, reference-count operations, indirect or foreign calls, missing bodies and unproved recursion stay conservative. The proof is stronger than absence of an escaping alias: no callee may count, free or grow the stack block. Snapshots, loop ordering, checked traps and output retain their value semantics.
- [NEW-CORE-STACK-3] Promoted list storage is limited to 8 KiB per function in addition to the 4 KiB element limit per list. Each selected list charges its 32-byte header and element capacity rounded up to 32-byte alignment; candidates exceeding the remaining budget stay on the heap. The budget resets for each function and bounds this optimization's contribution, not the entire native frame.
- [NEW-CORE-STACK-4] Borrow-only call proofs are persisted with dependency-validated effect summaries and included in final optimization cache identities independently of field read/write effects. Cold, warm and deferred-body builds agree with uncached ownership and emission. Editing a transitive callee to retain or return an argument invalidates its unchanged callers' stack promotion; restoring the borrowing implementation restores promotion.
- [NEW-CORE-DEST-1] Optimized builds expose a small fresh-list constructor to its caller by cloning its CFG before ownership. The constructor has ordinary unmanaged arguments, one fresh list of unmanaged elements, one return, no calls, at most 100 live instructions, 128 instruction slots and 20 blocks, and a straight push chain or recognized counted fill bounded to 4 KiB of elements. Constructor expansion adds at most 256 instructions per caller. Existing stack promotion then checks the entire caller-side lifetime and its 8 KiB budget: escaping, snapshotted, unsupported or oversized lists retain ordinary storage. Source create-and-return syntax, checked traps and inline ancestry are preserved; this policy does not introduce a destination-passing ABI.
- [NEW-CORE-DEST-2] Constructor body dependencies participate in CFG inline cache identities. Cold, warm and deferred-body optimized builds agree with uncached ownership, emission and ancestry; changing a constructor beyond the stack bound invalidates its callers, and restoring it restores eligibility.
- [NEW-CORE-CONSUME-1] Optimized builds may infer an ordinary internal parameter as consuming when a leaf helper returns that unmanaged-element list through copies, phis and list updates. The initial bounded proof excludes source-mut, managed elements, calls, recursion, explicit counting, missing bodies and bodies over 4096 instructions. Installation excludes the entry function and any module whose type catalog contains a function value type, covering indirect and runtime callbacks even with omitted bodies. A distinct compiler-only signature mask transfers one owner without changing source-mut result projection or its in-out ABI. Ownership liveness moves dead arguments and retains live snapshots, repeated consumed/borrowed arguments and borrowed parts as required; uniqueness tests still protect shared updates, and the emitter invalidates list-header knowledge across consuming calls.
- [NEW-CORE-CONSUME-2] Local consuming eligibility is retained in dependency-validated effect summaries, while the closed-module guard is checked anew before ownership for all functions together. The installed mask participates in canonical signatures and final cache identities. Cold, warm and deferred-body builds agree with uncached emission when updater bodies change or function values appear or disappear; no changed convention may reuse stale owned code.
- [NEW-CORE-RC-9] A list is updated in place without a count test only when no owner it gained can still hold its block at the update: the pass follows the block through the phis that carry it from a retaining use (a store into a container, a call argument, a retained phi operand) to the update, so a list published into another list in one loop iteration is tested, and copied, before the next iteration appends to it.
- [NEW-CORE-MUT-OWN-1] A `mut` parameter (passed by value and returned, `ir.Signature.owned`) is owned by the callee: the call takes the caller's argument without a retain when the caller's value is dead until the write-back, and the callee updates it in place after a count test. A read of a list field of an owned record that the same block then replaces with `record_set` takes the field over (the set drops nothing), and an owned record read field by field in its own block hands each block-holding field to its read, so a `mut` record's `r.items.append(x)` and the write-back of a call's results copy nothing. Appending through `mut` in a loop is linear; an alias taken before the call keeps its value.
- [NEW-CORE-RC-10] A call that takes a `mut` argument and reads the same value through another parameter is handed a retained owner, so the callee sees it shared and copies before writing, and the caller's own reference is released after the call. `mylang-core test` run with `MYL_CORE_LEAKCHECK=1` fails a test that ends with blocks live, with the reason `leak: N blocks live at exit`; `conformance.py --leakcheck` runs the new core that way and `--verify` builds its release mode with `--verify-facts`.
- [NEW-CORE-RC-11] `undef` is never owned, retained or released: a managed `undef` is emitted with every block pointer null (null, a zero struct, or zeroed memory for a record or union over four words), and the release of a phi that may hold it (an operand is `undef` or such a phi) skips null pointers, so a loop phi fed `undef` on entry and released on a zero-trip exit does not crash.
- [NEW-CORE-RC-12] A record whose only uses are field reads in its own block hands each block-holding field to its read and is never counted, unless it is a phi: a phi holds only what its operands hand it on their edges, so it stays an ordinary owned record and its operands are taken there, not released (the record joined at the exits of a release build's unswitched loop versions).
- [NEW-CORE-RC-13] A borrowed part (a field, element or payload read from a counted value) is promoted to its own count only when its owner is consumed while the part is live, which is after the part is read. An owner consumed earlier in the same block, such as a record whose copy had a field rebound through a `mut` call before the read, leaves the part borrowed. An update of that part while the owner is still used, for example through a snapshot binding, then copies instead of testing a count that belongs to the owner.
- [NEW-CORE-ALLOC-1] The new core's runtime allocates blocks of up to 1 KiB (header included) from per-size-class free lists, one class per 32 bytes, carved from 64 KiB chunks that are never returned, and larger blocks with `malloc` (a large list grows with `realloc`); a list's capacity is everything its block holds. Taking a small list from its free list, pushing into a list with room, and freeing a small block that holds no blocks are emitted inline in the program; the rest calls the runtime. The leak count stays exact: blocks carved or allocated large, less large ones freed and small ones on free lists at exit.
- [NEW-CORE-TRAP-2] A trap report names, after the trapping frame, every caller as `  in f at path:line:column` (the call's position), innermost first, then the `MYLANG-TRAP` record after an ASCII record separator, byte for byte as the old core writes it: names are module-qualified (`trap_frames::leaf`) and the path is the module-root-relative one for a file with a `module` header and the absolute one for a header-less file, in trap reports and error traces alike. This holds at debug and release alike: the build carries DWARF 4 line tables and inline records (`!dbg` on calls and traps, a subprogram per function and one for the C entry, which is never printed) and every function has unwind tables, and the trap walks the stack (glibc `backtrace`, i.e. `_Unwind_Backtrace`) and maps each return address through the executable's own `.debug_info` and `.debug_line`, one frame per inlined call. No instruction is added to the program's code. `--trap-frames=posix_exit` builds without the tables, and its reports name only the trapping frame.
- [NEW-CORE-ERRTRACE-1] The error trace keeps its origin (an `err_trace` origin restarts it) and the last 32 appended sites in a ring, and records nothing else; recorder calls carry `"call-inline-cost"="0"`. A `main` returning `Result` that returns `Err(value)` flushes stdout, writes `error: VALUE` and one `  path:line:column  function  action` line per site (origin first, columns aligned, `... N more entries dropped ...` where the ring overwrote appends) to stderr, releases the value and exits with it when it is an integer, else 1; `Ok(code)` exits with `code` (`Ok(None)` with 0).
- [NEW-CORE-ERRTRACE-2] An `err_trace`'s `data` is its mode: 0 `origin` (`Err(...) created`) and 2 `runtime` (`Err produced by the runtime`) restart the trace; 1 `append` (`? propagated`), 3 `converted` (`? converted to Union.case`, the case named by the string constant in `a`) and 4 `returned` (`returned`) append. A `returned` site on the same line of the same function as the line before it is folded into that line, and only the last 32 appends survive, after a `... N more entries dropped ...` line.
- [NEW-CORE-LIST-1] `list_set` and `list_get_field` check their index like `list_get` (`R_LIST_BOUNDS`, index and length), and `list_pop` of an empty list traps `R_LIST_BOUNDS` with the old core's pop detail (`(length 0)` and a `pop` record); stdout written before the trap is flushed.
- [NEW-CORE-VERIFY-IR-1] The Core IR verifier checks that every reachable block ends in exactly one terminator with its phis first, every phi has one operand per predecessor, predecessor lists match the terminators, every operand has the type its operation's signature asks for, and every use is dominated by its definition (a phi operand by the end of its predecessor); a vector plan must read only values that exist before its loop. Debug builds of `mylang-core` verify after construction and after the release passes, and the unit tests verify every module they build or run, reporting `E_CORE_INVALID_IR` with the function, block, instruction and reason.
- [NEW-CORE-RECORD-2] The Core IR builder lowers a checked `record` to `record_make` (fields evaluated in the order written, placed in declaration order), a field read to `record_get` and a field write (`update`) to `record_set`; a nested record is a field whose type is a record, so `a.b.c = v` reads `a.b`, replaces `c` and replaces `b` in `a`. A `class` or a record holding a fixed array, dictionary, bytes or function value is refused with that reason, and a type that failed to map is refused again at its next use.
- [NEW-CORE-UNION-2] A checked union, `Option`, `Result` or payload-free enum is a Core IR `union` with one case per checked case in the checker's order: `variant` is `union_make`, `match` is a `union_tag` dispatch binding each arm's payload with `union_payload`, a `unit` payload is a case without one, and `==`/`!=` of two enums compare their tags.
- [NEW-CORE-TRY-1] Building an `Err(...)` emits `err_trace` with the origin mode at its site, and `value?` on a `Result` tests the tag, appends the `?` site with `err_trace`, converts the error into the caller's error union case when the types differ and returns the failure, then continues with the payload; on an `Option` it returns `None` of the caller's option type. A successful `?` executes no `err_trace`.
- [NEW-CORE-LIST-2] `xs[i] = v` is a bounds-checked `list_set` of the list's current value; `xs.pop()` reads element `len - 1` (a failing index is the empty-list pop trap `R_LIST_BOUNDS`) and then drops it with `list_pop`; reading a record element's field is `record_get` of the element. An append to a list element (`xs[i].append(v)`) pushes onto the element read out and stores it back with `list_set`; ownership releases the element a write replaces.
- [NEW-CORE-PLACE-1] The checker roots a receiver in a hidden local (`s.items.append(v)`, `xs[i].f = v`, `xs[i][j] = v`); the builder remembers the place each hidden local was read from (a local, then field and element steps) and, after a write through it, rebuilds that path from the root's current value (`record_set` for a field, `list_set` for an element) and writes the root. Checker-generated D1 refreshes re-read writable places at each iteration boundary; named entry copies remain independent.
- [NEW-CORE-PLACE-2] The checker's narrowing of an `Option` after `if x is None: return` binds a hidden local to the payload; the builder records it as the place "payload of case Some of `x`", so a write through it (`x.append(v)`, `x[i] = v`) rebuilds the union with the written payload and stores it back into `x`, as the old core's shared payload makes visible; without the place such a write was lost.
- [NEW-CORE-PLACE-3] A read through a hidden receiver (`xs[poke(mut xs)]`, `frame.code[replace(mut frame)]`, `len` of one) sees the receiver as it was captured, before the operation's arguments ran: the old core's evaluation-order snapshot of a lent operand (VALUE-SEMANTICS-14). A write through a receiver whose place overlaps an evaluated `mut` argument is rejected by the shared checker with `E_MUTATION_OVERLAP` (MUT-OVERLAP-RECEIVER-1).
- [NEW-CORE-MUT-1] A function with `mut` parameters takes each by value and returns a record of its own result (unless `unit`) followed by each `mut` parameter's final value in parameter order (the one value alone when it is the only part); every `return`, `?` failure and fall-off-the-end returns it. A call passes the current whole caller variable after all arguments evaluate and writes each returned `mut` value back into its place. An interior mut argument retains the value selected before later arguments. A later mut write to an ancestor supersedes its captured place, so the interior result is not published, including when a whole-root mut callee writes only a scalar field (COW-MUT-ARGUMENT-1). Sibling writes preserve publication, a same-place write is read from the current binding, and an untaken conditional argument cannot invalidate a captured place. Keyword arguments capture their places in source evaluation order.
- [NEW-CORE-MUT-2] The by-value mut ABI returns each parameter's final value on every return (MUT-REBIND-CALLER-1). SSA joins and loop phis carry whole-variable rebindings through conditional arms and loops without detachment tracking or special refusals.
- [NEW-CORE-STR-1] `==` and `!=` of two `str` values (alone, as a record field or union payload, or as list elements) compare their bytes: the builder declares the runtime's `myl_core_str_eq` as a `runtime_c` function in the module on first use and calls it, `!=` being its `not`; the emitter declares it `memory(argmem: read)`. A character read whose every use compares a valid one-scalar literal or appends the character may decode once to `str_code`, preserving the original located bounds check and evaluation order. Source extern declarations confer no fusion authority. Ordering and concatenation of strings stay refused.
- [NEW-CORE-TEXT-1] The text a program builds comes from the runtime: `a + b` of two strings, `separator.join(parts)` of a list of strings (what an f-string lowers to), and `str(x)` of an integer (decimal, signed or unsigned), a boolean (`True`, `False`) or `None` (`None`); each result is a fresh owned string, freed at its last use. Float text, formatting options and string indexing stay refused. `assert` of a condition known at build time (the checker's `assert false` of a narrowing arm) is nothing when true and ends the path when false, leaving no dead branch whose stale values a join would carry.
- [NEW-CORE-TEXT-2] Text equality compares lengths, then (past 256 bytes) the first and last eight bytes before the bulk compare; `find` confirms candidates with a byte loop and turns a match in non-ASCII text into a scalar index by counting from the nearer end, using the cached scalar count. Results are the old core's.
- [NEW-CORE-TEXT-CURSOR-1] A scalar index into non-ASCII text is found by walking from the nearest of the text's start, its end, a cursor the text keeps at its last looked-up position (in its `cap` word, or in eight spare octets past the terminating NUL of a text with spare capacity) and, for a range, the range's other end; `find`, `rfind`, `index`, `rindex`, `count`, `startswith` and `endswith` with bounds leave the cursor on their match, and a stepped slice walks from element to element. Scans that move an index or search bound forwards or backwards through a text therefore cost the distance moved, not the distance from the start. With `MYL_CORE_TEXTWALK=1` a program reports the octets its lookups walked on stderr at exit. Results are the old core's.
- [NEW-CORE-SORT-1] The new core lowers `xs.sort()` and `sorted(xs)` for integers, floats, strings and bytes to consuming `list_sort` with one-count copy-on-write, then the independent runtime's adaptive stable powersort (`myl_core_s2_sort_in_place`): natural runs, stable insertion and shorter-run scratch. Aliases retain their original order; integers order by value, floats put NaNs last and tie signed zeros, text and bytes compare their octets. Keyed and reverse sorting compute each key once in source order, then `myl_core_s2_sort_keyed` uses counting or packed-word ordering for scalar numeric keys and a stable merge for text, bytes and flat tuple keys. It receives the exact source/key strides, preserves equal-key input order in both directions and returns an owning sorted copy. Unsupported element or key types receive E_CORE_UNSUPPORTED.
- [NEW-CORE-UNIT-1] A `list[None]` is a Core IR list of `bool` that only ever holds `false`: an append or an element write stores `false` for the `None`, a read or a pop reads the element (keeping the bounds check) and yields no value, so the length, iteration, copies and equality behave as the old core's.
- [NEW-CORE-LOOP-1] A loop whose condition is the constant `True` (`while True:`) has no exit edge in the Core IR: its header jumps into the body and only a `break` or a `return` leaves it, so a function that ends in such a loop needs no return after it. Rebinding the name of a list a loop iterates (`names = [7]` in the body) leaves the loop on the list it started with; other writes use D1 live-place iteration and structural length guards.
- [NEW-CORE-DOMAIN-1] A `domain` over an integer or a bool has the Core IR type of its wire type: `wrap[D](v)` and `unwrap(d)` are the value itself, and `==` and `!=` of two domain values compare the wire values; a `float` is still the `f64` its prelude spells.
- [NEW-CORE-VERIFY-4] The assertion a verify build emits for a `proven` division or remainder (`R_CORE_FACT` when its operands are not non-negative or its divisor not positive) tests only the divisor's zero for an unsigned type: a `u8` 254 is not negative, so `x // 2u8` of 254 runs under `--verify-facts` as it does without it.
- [NEW-CORE-FACTS-8] The fact pass joins the facts of a list phi from its settled live operands: the hull of their elements and lengths, and their common `length` (the counted fill loop's bound) when they agree. The back edge of a loop is skipped until it is settled (a counted loop's list phi gets its pattern at the end of the first round), so a list a loop rewrites with `xs[i] = v` keeps the hull of its entry and of the values written; element hulls and common lengths that keep changing are widened to unknown after eight changes, counted apart from the length's. An index `x % n` (`floor_rem`, or `rem` of a non-negative `x`) of a list whose `length` is `n`, with `n` at least 1, is below its length: the bounds check is proven.
- [NEW-CORE-FACTS-9] `a or b`, `a and b` and `not a` in the condition of an `if`, a loop or an `assert` are lowered as control flow (each comparison ends its block in a branch), not as a boolean joined by a phi, so the fact pass's edge refinement (a comparison on a single-predecessor edge narrows its operands in every block it dominates) sees `size >= 2` after `if size < 2 or n < 1: return`. A condition that is none of these is evaluated before the blocks of its branch are made, so the numbering (and the emission order) of a loop's blocks does not change with the lowering of compound conditions elsewhere.
- [NEW-CORE-FACTS-10] A release build gives each parameter of an internal function that no `func_ref` names the hull of its argument intervals over every reachable call, each computed by a first fact pass with the caller's own parameters unknown (so recursion needs no fixed point); an argument without an interval, a caller the first pass did not analyse or any function value of the function leaves the parameter its type's range. Verify builds assert each such interval on entry and trap `R_CORE_FACT`.
- [NEW-CORE-ARENA-1] The interim arena rule (docs/design/new-core-s1.md §11): a `with arena() [as n]:` block lowers as a plain block and `n.escape(v)` as `v`, a `break` or `continue` out of such a block is an ordinary jump, and values are freed by reference counting at their last use. A `with` over a resource (an open file) is refused.
- [NEW-CORE-ASSERT-1] An `assert condition[, message]` (and the `@requires`, `@ensures` and `@invariant` clauses the checker places as assertions) is a branch on the condition to a block that ends in an `unreachable` carrying the clause kind and the message; a false condition flushes stdout and prints the old core's first line `path:line:column: error[R_ASSERT]: native execution failed: MESSAGE` (the condition's position; `R_REQUIRES`, `R_ENSURES`, `R_INVARIANT` for the clauses; the condition's source text when no message is given), the `  in f at` frames and the `MYLANG-TRAP` record carrying only the message, and exits 1, at debug, release and verify. An assertion whose message is computed from values follows NEW-CORE-S4-ASSERT-MESSAGE-1.
- [NEW-CORE-LIST-3] `xs.clear()` replaces the list at its place with an empty one (the old value dies by ownership) and `xs.copy()` is the list itself, the next write to either copying (copy on write); `len(text)` of a string counts Unicode scalars, as the old core does, through the runtime's `myl_core_str_chars` (a count of bytes would be wrong for non-ASCII text). A call whose keyword arguments the checker reordered (`call.evaluation`) runs the evaluation block first, binding the arguments in source order; a `mut` argument the checker turned into a temporary is refused.
- [NEW-CORE-BOX-1] A record field or union payload the checker marks as closing a recursive type has Core IR type `box(T)`: construction wraps the value in `box_make`, and a read, a `match` binding or a `?` unwraps it with `box_get`; a recursive type is mapped once and its reserved type found again through the box.
- [NEW-CORE-FUSE-1] Before ownership runs, an element read of a list that is only taken apart (`xs[i].f`, `match xs[i]`, and chains of `record_get`, `union_tag` and `union_payload` on the result) becomes one `list_get_field` per projection: the first keeps the read's bounds check and span and the others none. It is fused only when every use is a projection and nothing that can write, call, print, allocate or (while the read can trap) trap lies between the read and any projection, so no whole element is loaded to read one field and no observable order changes. The read a `pop` starts with is never fused.
- [NEW-CORE-VERIFY-IR-2] The verifier admits the five `err_trace` modes (0 `origin`, 1 `append`, 2 `runtime`, 3 `converted`, 4 `returned`); a `converted` trace carries the `str` naming its case in `a`, every other mode carries no operand, and any other mode number is invalid IR.
- [NEW-CORE-TEST-1] `mylang-core test FILE.my [--filter S]` checks FILE with the old runner's stub `main` appended (a file defining its own `main` is checked as written) and discovers tests by the old rule (NATIVE-TEST-1): a function named `test_*` or declared after `@test`, always when `public`, when unexported only as `def() -> bool`, and an unexported `@test` one whatever its shape; the report lists them sorted by qualified name `module::name`, and `--filter` keeps those whose qualified name contains S, or matches S as a shell glob when S has `*`, `?` or `[`.
- [NEW-CORE-TEST-2] `mylang-core test` builds one executable per file, at debug or with `--release`, whose synthesized entry serves a request naming each trial's row of the runner's table and calls that test with a stand-in for each `Stream` parameter; every test runs in its own forked trial (NEW-CORE-TEST-BATCH-1), so a trap, a crash or a timeout ends only that test, and reads the verdict from the exit status (0 passed, 2 returned false) and the trap report on stderr.
- [NEW-CORE-TEST-3] The test report is graded per test function: a test whose reachable functions the new core cannot build, or that takes parameters other than `Stream`, is `unsupported` with its `E_CORE_UNSUPPORTED` reason and position and is left out of the executable, so the rest of the file still builds and runs; a test the backend rejects after the combined build is found by building each alone and is `unsupported` (emission) or `failed` (toolchain error) while the others run.
- [NEW-CORE-TEST-4] `mylang-core test` prints the old runner's `passed NAME` / `failed NAME: REASON` lines (a trap's `R_CODE (detail)` and its `  in` frames, or `expected true, got false`), `unsupported NAME: E_CORE_UNSUPPORTED (REASON) at L:C` for a refused test, and the summary `N passed, 0 cached, M failed, 0 skipped` with `, K unsupported` appended when some test was refused; `--json` prints the same as `{"version":"native-tests-v1","tests":[{"name","status","reason","at"}],...}`; the exit status is 1 when a test failed, 3 when none was selected and 0 otherwise.
- [NEW-CORE-CONF-1] The pinned conformance list (`test/core/conformance.pins`: the 31 S1 files of docs/design/new-core-s1.md §9 and four S2 Wave A language files, 35 files total) runs under `mylang-core test` at debug and release as part of `zig build test-core`: a test the pins record as passing must still pass, no other test of a listed file may fail unless it is pinned `known-fail`, a crash or a vanished pinned test fails the gate, and tests the new core cannot build yet are `unsupported` and never fail it.
- [NEW-CORE-ROWS-1] The fourteen S1 microbenchmark rows exist as single-row programs (`test/core/row_NAME.my`: `record_reads`, `tagged_stack`, `mutate_unique`, `mutate_shared`, `record_construct`, `record_update`, `option_handle`, `result_propagate`, `list_scan`, `list_append`, `list_filter`, `alloc_short`, `alloc_retained`, `mutate_nested`), each the row's setup and kernel from benchmarks/runtime with other rows' setup removed, taking `size iterations seed`; the old core's build of each prints the original program's checksum, and every row the new core builds prints it at debug and release, with at least six rows built (a row it refuses is skipped, but the built count never shrinks).
- [NEW-CORE-RECORD-3] A record or union of more than four words is built, read, written, passed, returned, stored in lists and boxes and carried around loops with the same results as the old core's; `test/core/v_wide.my` prints the old core's output at debug, release and verify.
- [NEW-CORE-EQ-1] `==` and `!=` of two records, unions (enums, `Option`, `Result`) and lists compare structurally: record fields and the payload of equal union tags with the type's own equality (floats as IEEE numbers: NaN differs from itself, 0.0 equals -0.0), lists by length and then element by element, recursively. Text compares by its octets. Recursive values through boxes or lists use an explicit worklist with shared-pair memoization, preserving NaN inequality even when the pointers are identical.
- [NEW-CORE-NUMERIC-1] A `unit` argument is evaluated and not passed (a `unit` parameter is not an IR parameter); an integer literal in `a + 1` takes the other operand's type (`i16 + 1`); a float converts to an integer by truncating toward zero first and then checking the result's range, so `i8(-128.5)` is -128 and `i8(-129.0)` traps `R_FLOAT_CONVERSION` at debug and release.
- [NEW-CORE-FACTS-6] The fact pass keeps, per record value, an interval for each integer or boolean field: `record_make` takes its operands', `record_set` replaces one field of its base's, a phi joins its operands' (a field that keeps changing becomes unknown), and `record_get` reads the field's interval, so arithmetic on fields is proven the way it is on locals. A field of a parameter, a call result or a nested record has no interval.
- [NEW-CORE-FACTS-7] The fact pass keeps, per list value, an interval for its length (a new list is empty, a push adds one, a pop removes one, a write or copy keeps it, a phi joins its operands' and widens to the largest length) and, through `list_set` and `list_push`, the hull of its integer elements with the written or pushed value. A read, write or `list_get_field` at an index below the length's lower bound and a pop of a list at least one long need no check. Facts are about the value: a list parameter, a call result and a list a `mut` call returned have none.
- [NEW-CORE-VERIFY-3] A verify build also asserts, after the definition of each list value with a length interval and each record value with field intervals, that the length (or each field) lies in its interval, trapping `R_CORE_FACT` otherwise; a construction stored straight into its consumer is asserted through the values it was built from.
- [NEW-CORE-LLVM-3] A record or union of more than four words is never an LLVM aggregate: its SSA value is a pointer to its bytes in memory — a home it may share with values never live at the same time (NEW-CORE-CE-RECORDS-1), a parameter's memory, or a place inside another value; `record_get` of a smaller field is a load at the field's offset, `record_set` copies the old value into the new one's home and stores the field there, or stores it where it is when both share a home, and a value read out of a list, box or dictionary is copied into its home; output is identical to the register form at every optimization level.
- [NEW-CORE-LLVM-4] A `record_make` or `union_make` whose only use is a `list_push`, `list_set`, a return through `sret`, a `box_make`, a field of such a construction or a field of a construction that lives in memory (NEW-CORE-CE-RECORDS-2) is never built on its own: its fields, or its tag word and payload, are stored straight into the destination, with no temporary, no `memcpy` and no aggregate store.
- [NEW-CORE-LLVM-5] A phi of a record or union in memory lives in its home and is filled on each incoming edge whose value lives elsewhere (a conditional branch to such a block goes through a block of its own that does the copies); the copies of one edge are made as a parallel copy — one that reads a home another writes goes first, and only a cycle (two records swapped) goes through a temporary.
- [NEW-CORE-LLVM-6] A `match` on a union read from a list loads the tag word with the element's bounds check, then only the payload fields of the arm taken, each with no further check; the whole element is never loaded.
- [NEW-CORE-CE-RECORDS-1] A record or union of more than four words shares its home — one stack slot, or memory its caller passed — with every value it is never live at the same time as, so a phi needs no copy on an edge whose incoming value already lives there: the emitter (`emit_homes.zig`) coalesces each phi with its incoming values, and a record carried around a loop with `break` and `continue` is built once and never copied. Two values interfere when one is live just after the other is defined (a phi's operands are read at the end of their predecessor, a construction stored straight into its consumer reads its operands at the consumer), when the other's defining instruction reads it after writing its home, or when both are written at one point (two phis of one block, the values one call defines).
- [NEW-CORE-CE-RECORDS-2] A field of a record, or the payload of a union, that lives in memory and is itself over four words is a view: a pointer into its parent with no copy, and every use of the view keeps the parent from being updated in place while the view lives; `retain` and `release` count a nested record or payload in memory where it lies; and a construction whose only use is a field of a construction in memory is built straight into that field.
- [NEW-CORE-CE-RECORDS-3] A `record_set` of a record over four words updates it in place — the new value shares the old one's home — whenever the old value has no later reader, wherever it was made: a parameter passed in-out, a phi, a call result or a construction; a snapshot still read after the store keeps its own copy. A write-back `p.f = y` that updates `p` in place builds `y` and the values it was updated from in the field itself (`wb = it.rt; ...; it.rt = wb` copies nothing) when no read of the field's old bytes and no write of `p`'s whole home falls inside their lives.
- [NEW-CORE-CE-RECORDS-4] An internal function's `mut` parameter over four words is in-out: passed as a writable `noalias` pointer to the caller's memory, which the callee updates in place and leaves holding the final value, so the result names no copy of it — a function whose result is just that value returns `void`, and one whose result record holds it next to other values leaves that field to the caller, which reads the final value where it passed it (and copies it into the result record only when something reads that record whole). The caller passes the argument's own home when the argument dies at the call, and otherwise a copy, so another argument of the same call that reads the `mut` record, or a part of it, sees it from before the call. Direct and indirect calls and cross-unit declarations follow one convention, decided by the signature; `extern` C functions are unchanged. A value returned through `sret` is built in the caller's destination when it shares that home.
- [NEW-CORE-CE-RECORDS-5] Records over four words that share homes, are read where they lie, are updated in place or cross calls in-out behave exactly as copies would: phis in loops with `break` and `continue`, records with list and text fields, unions and `Result` payloads, nested records, write-backs (`wb = it.rt; push(mut wb, v); it.rt = wb`), recursion through `mut`, `mut` arguments next to read-only arguments that overlap them, and a trap in the middle of an in-place update print the same at debug, release and `--verify-facts`, with no block live at exit.
- [NEW-CORE-CE-RECORDS-6] When a value of a function lives in its caller's destination `%ret` (it is returned whole on some path), a `ret` of another value that would overwrite a part of it before reading it builds its value whole in a temporary first and then copies it to `%ret` (`Plan.staged`, NEW-CORE-CE2-RESULTS-2), so `return Pair(x=w.y, y=w.x)` next to `return w` reads every part before a store overwrites it.
- [NEW-CORE-TRACE-1] The error return trace is recorded as the old core records it (ERROR-TRACE-1): `origin` where an `Err(...)` is built, `append` on a `?` that passes the error on unchanged, `converted` (the site naming `Union.case`) on a `?` that wraps it into a case of the caller's error union, and `returned` on the `Err` arm of a `return` of a value the statement did not construct (after testing the tag). The entry adapter's `error_report` print goes to the runtime's `myl_core_main_error_*`, so a `main` that returns an `Err` writes `error: VALUE` and the trace to stderr, line for line as the old core does at debug and release.

- [NEW-CORE-S2-IR-1] S2 Core IR provides layout-independent dictionary operations, bytes, inline fixed arrays, top-level function values and SSA-only string spans; every new operation verifies its operand and result types. Dictionary insert probes originate from a lookup of the same dictionary and key. String spans cannot be stored, merged or returned and are admitted only by designated borrowing operations or explicit extern-C span parameters. Consuming updates use copy-on-write under the existing one-count ownership rules. Until source lowering is implemented the builder reports E_CORE_UNSUPPORTED; unfinished hand-built IR operations trap E_CORE_UNSUPPORTED before any result is observable.

- [NEW-CORE-ITERATION-1] The new core lowers D1 live-place refreshes to current SSA reads, preserving COW snapshots and caller write-back. A refreshed receiver whose guarded length differs traps R_LIST_ITERATION_MUTATION; lists accept growth and reject shrink according to the shared checker. Read-only entry receivers keep their immutable borrow fast path.

- [NEW-CORE-TEXT-BORROW-1] Text encode changes the pointer type to bytes without allocation or count traffic when borrowed. Byte uses keep their source alive; escaping byte values retain the source, so early source release and COW writes preserve exact values.
- [NEW-CORE-TEXT-BYTES-1] The new core shares counted str/bytes leaf storage, preserves UTF-8 scalar indexing and slices, maintains value semantics through consuming appends, and lowers split, join, search, encode and byte XOR without linking the old runtime. Character appends snapshot the exact scalar bytes before destination mutation or growth. Width and code operations may return directly for ASCII only after an explicit valid-byte-offset guard; Unicode and invalid offsets preserve the runtime path, and a checked code producer keeps its original located bounds check.

- [NEW-CORE-TEXT-BYTES-2] An immutable byte reduction may widen u8 loads to bounded integer lanes, including XOR of nonnegative bounded operands; the scalar tail and verify assertions preserve the scalar result.

- [NEW-CORE-DICT-1] The new core lowers insertion-ordered dictionaries and unit-valued sets through pure SSA probes, consuming COW updates, borrowed entry reads, deletion tombstones and order-independent equality. Hybrid 16-slot control probes and cached hashes preserve key equality; missing required keys report R_MISSING_KEY. Immutable dictionary lengths and scalar key/value hulls are asserted by fact-verification builds. Unknown call and parameter inputs settle to unknown facts through copies and updates, weakening loop joins. Direct views retain precise mapping identity during their immediate consumer; D2-VIEW-STORAGE-1 rejects source storage before new-core lowering. Internal view origins remain conservatively guarded.
- [NEW-CORE-DICT-SMALL-1] A dictionary or set probe on a table of at most eight entries (which has no index) scans the cached entry hashes inline and compares a key only on a hash match, with no runtime call; a table that ever lost an entry, and so carries a deletion bitmap, asks `myl_core_dict_find` instead. A miss answers -1 either way.
- [NEW-CORE-S1-VIEW-1] A release build turns a string slice whose only uses are the keys of text-dictionary lookups (`get`, `in`, indexing, `pop`) and inserts into a borrowed view of its source (`str_slice_view`, clamped exactly as the slice is): the lookup hashes and compares the source's octets in place, and an insert materializes its own key (`str_from_view`) right before it and keeps the view's probe. The view lives only in SSA and ownership keeps its source alive and unchanged while any use of it remains, so a key inserted and then read back after its source is rebound, replaced or extended is its own copy. A slice with any other use, or of a source updated in place in the same function (NEW-CORE-S1-VIEW-4), stays a copy.
- [NEW-CORE-S1-VIEW-2] A borrowed text-key probe is inline for every table shape: it hashes the span once, scans an index-less table's cached hashes (skipping entries a deletion left dead), probes an indexed table's first 16-slot control group, and asks the runtime only when that group is full. A candidate compares its length, then its octets.
- [NEW-CORE-S1-VIEW-3] A borrowed key of at most 16 octets is compared with a candidate key inline: its first and last eight-octet words (four-octet words below eight; the first, middle and last octets below four), which together cover every octet and never read outside either key; a longer key is compared with `memcmp`. Keys whose hashes are equal and which differ in a single window at the start, middle or end stay distinct.
- [NEW-CORE-S1-VIEW-4] A string slice whose only uses are text-dictionary keys and the reading text helpers (`==`, `!=`, `len`, `startswith`, `endswith`, `in`) is a borrowed view in a release build: each helper is called in its span form, whose `str` parameters take `(data, length)` spans, and answers what the text form answers. A slice of a source that is updated in place in the same function (directly or through a phi, or handed over as a `mut` argument) stays a copy, so the update is not turned into a copy of the whole source.
- [NEW-CORE-S1-ASCII-1] A text copied from part of another (a slice, a materialized view, a trim, a stepped slice) is marked ASCII without re-scanning its octets when its source is ASCII; a copy of any other text is scanned as before. A byte-string slice is never scanned: it keeps an ASCII source's flag and otherwise carries none, which no byte operation reads.

- [NEW-CORE-NUMBER-1] Integer text parsing reads plain decimal inputs of at most sixteen digits in safe one-to-eight-byte SWAR words; whitespace, separators, long numbers and invalid inputs use the full pinned grammar. Signed and unsigned decimal formatting and printing write two digits per division with exact zero, sign and 64-bit edge results.

## New core S3 foundation

- [NEW-CORE-S3-IR-1] Mutable bytes, capability authorities and external resources have counted pointer layouts; all new operation operands and results are verified before emission.
- [NEW-CORE-S3-IR-2] Mutable-byte and scalar numeric operations emit valid LLVM and execute with verified operands and the exact bounds, empty and width checks.
- [NEW-CORE-S3-ABI-1] Runtime-C helper calls pass declared capability tokens and pointers in the C ABI; source extern capability parameters retain their erasure rule.
- [NEW-CORE-S3-OWN-1] Mutable-byte updates consume their receiver, copy borrowed or shared values and assert static uniqueness in verify builds; record phis remain owned.

- [NEW-CORE-S3-NUMERIC-1] the core lowers integer shifts with an exact width check,
  saturating addition, subtraction and multiplication at the operand width, and strict binary64 unary intrinsics.
  Unsigned conversion retains its full width; verify mode rechecks proven shift counts
  and asserts saturation and right-shift intervals.


- [NEW-CORE-S3-VALUES-1] Recursive equality and dictionary-key hashing use explicit worklists and shared-node memo tables; padding and inactive union payloads do not participate, and identical pointers do not bypass NaN inequality. Aggregate text preserves nominal record and variant names, tuple positions, insertion order and the existing depth-24 ellipsis rule. List repetition creates independent values under one-count ownership.

- [NEW-CORE-TEXT-S3-1] Remaining text and immutable-byte methods match the old core: scalar search bounds, non-overlapping count/replace, Unicode charset trims and repr, ASCII case/classification, line splitting, validated chr/ord, packed text layout, UTF-8 decode/count and immutable byte ranges. Inputs borrow and results own independent counted blocks; failed index and scalar operations retain located traps.
- [NEW-CORE-LIST-S3-1] List extend and reverse use value-functional updates over a captured source; self-extension and nested/shared snapshots preserve their old values, with ordinary ownership COW and verify assertions.
- [NEW-CORE-LIST-S3-2] List index, insertion, removal, indexed pop and step slices retain source evaluation order, Python bounds normalization, structural equality and independent snapshots. Missing-value and indexed-pop traps retain their distinct runtime diagnostics; unit elements retain their source meaning despite erased physical placeholders.

- [NEW-CORE-S3-NUMBER-1] The new core's float/text runtime ports the canonical Zig grammar and exact number formatting, in a separate object that `zig build core` compiles once for x86_64-linux-gnu.2.31 and the baseline CPU and embeds; a build that calls a number helper publishes it in the per-user store under its content digest (rewriting a damaged copy) and links it, starting no process. Mutable bytes preserve independent snapshots and updates under the frozen buffer IR and verified ownership rules; embedded assets resolve by checked digest and length.


- [NEW-CORE-S3-RUNNER-1] Core property trials preserve the old name-derived seed, generated type shapes, 100-run default, precondition/invariant discard rule and greedy shrink order. Copied trial inputs do not share capability or filesystem state, completed outcomes survive a later fatal trap, and only True or Ok(True) passes. Counterexamples retain the original and minimal inputs, shrink/discard counts and reproduction command.

## New core S4 values

- [NEW-CORE-NESTED-LENGTH-S4-1] A list value whose child lists all have the same immutable parameter or constant length may prove nested bounds from that length. Push, replacement, pop, copy and phi facts preserve this only when every child agrees; changed child lengths invalidate it. A counted fill's final length applies after its loop exits, and a copy captured during that loop retains its captured length. Verify builds assert each loaded child's symbolic length while that child is live.

## New core S4 emission

- [NEW-CORE-S4-UNITS-1] Independent source-module, generic, adapter and entry units have deterministic LLVM text with stable program symbols and explicit fastcc calls. Reached strings, layouts, dictionary and value/render descriptors, zeros and metadata use local first-use numbering. Unrelated edits preserve unit text; consumed inline bodies and facts change it. Native linking unions number and math dependencies over every unit.
- [NEW-CORE-STAGE-CACHE-5] Independent development LLVM units and generated helper seeds persist under verified compiler-bound emission input keys. Keys bind canonical function/type/string identities, aliasing and descriptor graphs, located spans and inline ancestry, effects, facts, vector plans, linkage and emission options. Eager facts and verified deferred fact bytes share one semantic identity; deferred dense tables decode only for functions of units that need rendering. Unchanged units skip rendering, changed units render in bounded job and estimated scratch-memory batches with independent scratch allocators, and final unit order, helper deduplication and LLVM bytes match uncached emission. Compact emission receipts retain final inline ancestry identities and validate the current names and source locations of referenced inline scopes without decoding bodies. Source text outside those validated body and scope dependencies does not invalidate a receipt. A unit too large for the admission estimate runs alone; corrupt or unavailable entries are cache misses.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-RELEASE-1] A release build emits the whole program as one LLVM unit, keyed by its text like any unit, so LLVM optimizes across every module boundary; a debug build emits one unit per source module plus generic, adapter and entry units, so an edit recompiles only the units whose text changed (S4-1a).
- [NEW-CORE-S4-RUNTIME-CACHE-1] The core runtime and number-text objects, keyed by their source, settings and toolchain identity, are published in one per-user directory: `MYLANG_CORE_RUNTIME_CACHE`, else `$XDG_CACHE_HOME/mylang/core-runtime`, else `$HOME/.cache/mylang/core-runtime`. A new work directory reuses them instead of recompiling. An empty `MYLANG_CORE_RUNTIME_CACHE`, no usable variable, an uncreatable directory or an unproved toolchain keeps them in the build's own work directory.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-CACHE-1] The core's native driver keeps its own content-keyed stores. A unit's object is keyed by BLAKE3 over the store format, the optimization level, the target triple, the CPU flags and the resolved host CPU, the toolchain's content identity, the runtime source (its ABI) and the unit's exact LLVM text. It lives in the per-user directory of NEW-CORE-S4-RUNTIME-CACHE-1 as `units-v1/<key>.o` beside a `.sum` record of its size and digest, so new worktrees and test directories share objects. A miss compiles beside its entry and is renamed into place after the toolchain revalidates. A hit starts no process and is served only when its bytes match the record, so a damaged entry is recompiled, never linked. Misses compile in parallel with `zig cc -c`, largest first, up to `MYLANG_JOBS` or the CPUs of the process's affinity mask. `--link` C sources compile with `-MMD` into the work directory's `foreign-v1`, keyed by their path, spelling, working directory, bytes, settings and toolchain, and are served only while every header their last depfile listed keeps its recorded bytes; a file that changed during the compile pins nothing. The linked program, keyed by every object's bytes and the link settings, is kept in the work directory's `programs-v1` and copied to the output, so a build whose objects all match starts no process. Every `zig` process gets a fresh private local cache inside a staging directory removed when the build ends, and an unproved toolchain uses no shared or persistent store.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-REPLAY-1] An unchanged `mylang-core build` replays its last identical build: the work directory's `replay-v1` slot for the entry and settings (compiler identity, flags, working directory, entry and project configuration resolved afresh, toolchain identity, and the environment except the shell's `_`, `SHLVL` and `OLDPWD`) lists every module source, embedded file, generated interface file, `--link` source and depfile header the build read, with the digest of the bytes it used. When all of them, the toolchain and the program entry are unchanged, the build copies the recorded program to the output and repeats the recorded report without checking, lowering, emitting or starting a process. `--emit-llvm` builds neither replay nor record, and an unpinned `--link` input, an unresolved embed or an unproved toolchain records nothing.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-VERIFY-CACHE-1] `mylang-core build --verify-cache` also recompiles every unit the object store served and fails the build, naming the unit, when the fresh object's bytes differ from the cached one; such a build never replays. `--cache-report` adds one line with the units linked, reused, compiled and verified, whether the program was reused, and the processes started, or says that the build replayed.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-COLD-1] A first build on a machine compiles the core runtime object with `-g0` (the trap walker reads only `mylang-core` compile units and the machine code is unchanged) and runs no `zig build-obj` for the number-text runtime, which ships prebuilt (NEW-CORE-S3-NUMBER-1): the processes of a cold build are its unit compiles, the runtime object and the link.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-LINK-1] A debug `mylang-core` link with no `--link` C source passes `-fno-sanitize=undefined`, so Zig neither builds nor links its UBSan runtime: no core unit or runtime object calls it, and building it cost a fresh Zig cache about 10 s. A debug build with a `--link` source, which `zig cc -O0` may instrument, and every release link keep Zig's default; the flag is part of the linked program's store key.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-TOOL-1] A build's toolchain identity covers the `zig` executable's content, each library root's own generation (entries added, removed or retargeted), the runtime and discovery inputs and the native CPU; it does not rescan every library file. A library tree's contents are reviewed once against the approved distribution, memoized by that identity, and an in-place edit inside an installed toolchain is not a build input.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-ASSERT-MESSAGE-1] An `assert condition, message` whose message is a computed `str` evaluates it only when the condition fails, writes it and a newline to stderr, then traps `R_ASSERT` at the condition with no message on the first line and an empty `MYLANG-TRAP` operation, exactly as the old core does; `assert False, e` in a match arm does the same.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-STEPS-1] A core test's `--max-steps` budget (default 1,000,000) charges one step per executed block of an original program function, as the old core charges one per dispatch, so a test the old core finishes within its budget finishes within the core's; generated adapters are not charged.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-CAP-FIELD-1] A capability token (`Stream`, `Host`, a provider capability) may be a record field or payload in the new core: it is an `i64` with no count, so ownership and authority rules are unchanged; fixed arrays of them follow NEW-CORE-S5-CAP-COLLECTIONS-1, and the checker rejects lists and dictionaries of them (`E_LIST_CAPABILITY`) in both cores.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-FRAMES-1] `mylang-core test --max-frames N` (also `--max-frames=N`, or `limits.test.max_frames` in `mylang.json`) bounds the calls of original program functions one test trial may nest, 1,024 by default as in the old core: each call enters a frame on its first block and leaves it before every return, and the call past the budget traps `R_FRAME_LIMIT` with the old core's advice line (`R_STEP_LIMIT` and `R_MEMORY_BUDGET` end with theirs); generated adapters are not counted and programs built outside tests are not instrumented. The trial runs on a stack mapped for the whole budget (32 KiB per frame plus 8 MiB, one guard page), so recursion within the budget never depends on the native stack limit. A zero budget is a usage error, and a budget whose stack the byte ceiling cannot back is refused before anything is built with the old core's text and exit status 2.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-MEMORY-1] `mylang-core test --max-memory SIZE` (bytes or a `K`/`M`/`G` size; else `MYLANG_MEMORY_LIMIT`, else `limits.test.max_memory`, else 512 MiB; `0` removes it) bounds each test process: the runtime counts the chunks small blocks are carved from and every live large block, and an allocation past the ceiling traps `R_MEMORY_BUDGET`; the process's `RLIMIT_AS` is lowered (never raised) to the ceiling plus 256 MiB, and an allocation the OS then refuses reports `R_MEMORY_BUDGET` too. The ceiling and the frame budget are part of the test receipt key. Programs built outside tests carry no ceiling and pay nothing on the inline allocation paths.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-TAKE-1] A `mut` record's managed field read and written back in one block is taken over by the read even when a branch leaves the record untouched on another edge into a join: a phi sees the record only on edges that cannot follow the read, so `if c: r.items.append(x)` updates the list in place when unique instead of copying it per call. `byte_buffer.append_byte`, `append_range` and `append_utf8` read the receiver, hand it to one runtime call (an owned argument that is updated in place when its count is one and copied otherwise) and write it back in the statement's block, so `byte_builder` appends through a `mut` builder are linear; an out-of-range octet or an invalid range appends nothing and the method still returns the validity.
- [NEW-CORE-TAKE-READS-1] A `mut` record's managed field that a read takes over and a later `record_set` replaces may be read before that read, and the two may sit in different blocks. The take needs three things: the taking read runs ahead of the set on every path, every path from it reaches the set or a trap, and every earlier read of the field, with the parts read out of it, is used only before the taking read or only after the set. A part still used after the set holds its own count, because the set consumes the record it was read from. Every `record_set` that replaces a field of the record is considered, so arms of a branch can each update another field. Identical reads of one record's field in one block are merged first (a record value never changes), so a nested `h.marker.marks.append(x)`, which reads `h.marker` twice, takes `h.marker` and then its list. Then `slot = len(rt.objects)` ahead of `rt.objects.append(x)`, `rt.objects[i] = v` (whose negative-index branch splits it into blocks), and the Lox heap's reuse-or-grow allocation update their lists in place when unique instead of copying them on every call, so appends and writes through list fields of a `mut` record stay linear. A copy of the field still read after the update keeps the record's reference, so that update copies the field and the copy keeps the old list.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-SITES-1] Traps, assertions, effect sites and error traces carry pointers to private immutable site records containing the original source path.
