Debug compile units and file tables preserve caller paths across separately compiled modules. Core clones retain their exact specialized callee and original call positions in sparse optimizer metadata outside the IR, composed through loop duplication, ownership edits and LLVM inline records. Ancestry deeper than 32 frames refuses expansion before changing IR.
- [PY-REPEAT-2] A contextual list constructor in repetition keeps its list element type independently of a runtime integer count: `[0] * width` and `width * [0]` typecheck in both cores, including a declared `list[u8]` result.
- [NEW-CORE-BUFFER-S4-1] Numeric byte-buffer append formats integers and binary64 values in bounded stack storage with the ordinary string spelling, then reserves once and copies the fragment once. COW and fact-verification uniqueness checks precede the consuming append; no temporary string block or per-octet append loop is constructed.
- [NEW-CORE-PLACE-S4-1] Nested indexed assignments detach the outer collection before reading its managed child and transfer the selected slot owner through the child update and replacement. Each parent detachment and child update follows ordinary COW, preserving both root aliases and shared inner rows; verification builds assert every transferred parent is unique. A fresh collection whose stores move unique child blocks and whose child reads never escape can also prove the transferred child unique; verification asserts that premise before its update.
- [NEW-CORE-ROW-COW-1] A nested store's child update (`b[i][j] = v`, `b[i].append(v)`, `b[i][j] += v`, at any depth and inside any loop) runs in place without a count test only when no ordinary read of a child of the outer list holds its own count: a child read kept while the outer list is consumed (`kb = b[1]` before a loop that stores through `b`, an alias read in an earlier iteration, or a `mut` helper's loop inlined into the caller) is promoted to its own count, so that child has two owners and its update tests the count and copies the child when it is shared. The kept value never changes; rows nothing kept are still updated in place with no test.
- [NEW-CORE-ROW-COW-2] A fresh outer list proves its children unique only through pushes and stores that ran in place: a push or element store into an outer list that may be shared there (`snap = b` or a call that may keep `b`, then `b.append(row)` or `b[k] = row`) may copy the outer list, and the copy shares every child it carried over with the other holder, so a later nested store through it tests each child's count and the other holder keeps its children unchanged. The original parent also loses the child-uniqueness guarantee: a subsequent write through either version must account for children shared by that copy.
- [NEW-CORE-INLINE-S4-1] Release builds also inline internal helpers with consuming mut parameters, at most 20 blocks and 100 instructions when they contain no calls, prints or trace operations, cloning predecessor order, loop metadata and trap spans. A mut return record used only through field projections is decomposed into SSA results before ownership analysis, preserving scalar and collection return values without an aggregate materialization or retain.
- [NEW-CORE-FILL-S4-1] After stack promotion, a heap list filled once per counted iteration from zero may reserve an initial prefix of `max(count, 0)`, capped at 4096 elements. Only the push that supplies that loop's backedge, whose receiver remains unique after COW, uses the counted index and skips capacity checks within the prefix; longer fills and later appends retain ordinary growth. Verify builds assert the current length and room before each such store. Capacity hints do not change value semantics or loop evaluation.

## New core S4 projects

- [NEW-CORE-S4-PROJECT-1] Core project entries resolve nested module paths and standalone test files, discover the nearest strict mylang.json, and enforce source budgets, import policy, embedded-asset roots and required generated interfaces before lowering.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-TEST-1] Core file and directory test runs share one worker budget from explicit jobs, MYLANG_JOBS or available CPUs; empty selections exit 3 and small suites never start surplus workers.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-TEST-2] Directory test discovery includes mains, standalone test entries and orphan test libraries. Every test-declaring source must be accounted for by a checked root's report, from a child or from the run's own process (NEW-CORE-TEST-UNION-1); a missing program or inventory fails the run.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-TEST-3] Successful core test receipts key the exact reachable program (NEW-CORE-S4-TEST-6), generation schema, settings, compiler/runtime/toolchain identity, native inputs, provider settings and the environment variables of NEW-CORE-S4-TEST-5. Checksummed manifests replay only while all loaded source, embedded asset and configuration bytes remain unchanged. Force and verification execute trials; failures never become receipts.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-TEST-5] A core test receipt keys only the environment variables that can change an outcome: every `MYL_` and `MYLANG_` variable except `MYLANG_JOBS`, `MYLANG_CORE_RUNTIME_CACHE`, the parent tool ticket and the process-placement oracles `MYLANG_TEST_ISOLATE` and `MYLANG_TEST_CHILDREN`, and the C compiler and Zig toolchain inputs (`CPATH`, `C_INCLUDE_PATH`, `CPLUS_INCLUDE_PATH`, `OBJC_INCLUDE_PATH`, `LIBRARY_PATH`, `COMPILER_PATH`, `GCC_EXEC_PREFIX`, `CCC_OVERRIDE_OPTIONS`, `SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH`, `ZIG`, `ZIG_LIB_DIR`, `ZIG_LIBC`), as the old core does (NATIVE-TEST-12). A shell's `_` or `OLDPWD`, a terminal's variables or a wrapper such as `time` leave receipts valid; a test receives only `Stream` parameters, so it cannot read the environment, and dynamic-loader overrides still disable reuse.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-TEST-6] A core test's receipt key hashes its program's Core IR before passes, every function, instruction, block, loop, type and string, with each span replaced by the file, line and column emission reads, and the entry path, rather than the emitted LLVM. Passes and emission are pure functions of those and the keyed settings, so looking up a cached test emits no code, and an edit that only moves code in other files keeps the key.
- [NEW-CORE-TEST-QUEUE-1] A directory run starts its roots largest root file first (ties in path order) and runs them on a work queue: each of its workers starts the next root as soon as its last one ends, instead of waiting for a whole wave. Each child's `--jobs` is the run's budget divided by the roots that can still run beside it — those running, itself included, and those not yet started, at most the worker count — and never below one; when roots also run in the run's own process (NEW-CORE-TEST-UNION-1), each such root may use the whole budget and holds one of the budget's permits per test process, and a child holds its share of the permits while it runs, so the run never has more test processes than the budget. A test program's own test processes are likewise taken from a queue by its worker threads. Reports keep root order and test order whatever order the work finishes in.
- [NEW-CORE-TEST-BATCH-1] A test executable's plain tests (every test but a property test, which keeps its own processes under PROPERTY-TEST-4) share processes. Each worker takes the next run of pending tests — the pending count over the worker count, rounded up — and one process answers them in table order, each in its own forked trial with fresh capability stand-ins, its own captured output and its own step, frame, memory and millisecond wall-clock budgets, so every verdict, reason and trace is the one the test gets alone. A test that ends its process without answering keeps the answers before it: its trial's timeout is its verdict (`timed out after N ms`), any other ending (a signal or an exit) reruns it alone for a verdict of its own, and the tests after it continue in a new process. `MYLANG_TEST_ISOLATE=1` gives every plain test its own process, the differential oracle.
- [NEW-CORE-SPAWN-1] The core starts a child process — a test executable, a build tool, a directory run's child, the program `mylang run` runs — without copying its own address space: a `clone(CLONE_VM | CLONE_VFORK)` child on a stack of its own takes the argv, environment and standard streams the caller names, resets every signal handler, restores the caller's signal mask and calls `execve`, searching `PATH` for a bare name. A program that cannot start fails with the error `std.process` gives (`FileNotFound`, `AccessDenied`, ...), and a run's timeout and output limits behave as `std.process.run`'s.
- [NEW-CORE-TEST-UNION-1] A directory run runs its roots in its own process where they can share one check: a root that opens with its `module` header and declares no `main`, in a project whose configuration names no import policy and no interface files, with no providers in its cases file, in a run that selects by no git revision. Such a root first replays its recorded report as a child would; the roots of one project that miss are checked once together, as a synthetic entry importing each of them, and each root then selects, builds and runs its tests from its share of that program — the modules its own import graph reaches, which are the modules its own check would load — so its tests, receipts, report and exit status are the ones its child gives. Every other root, and every root of a project whose shared check fails, runs in a child process, whose own check reports its own diagnostics. `MYLANG_TEST_CHILDREN=1` runs every root in a child, the differential oracle.
- [NEW-CORE-TEST-SHARED-1] Explicit shared native test storage reuses only completed deterministic successes whose portable reachable-code, logical source locations, exact inputs, compiler/runtime/toolchain and execution-context identities agree. Each worktree checks its own current sources and reports its own diagnostics; literal path values and observable traces are never blindly remapped. Live effects and unproved portability remain fresh. Private manifests and mutable frontend state stay private. Versioned bounded receipts publish atomically under process locks; corrupt or missing receipts miss, invalid configured storage fails explicitly, and force bypasses shared reuse.
- [COMPILER-SHARED-CACHE-1] Explicit shared compiler artifact storage transports immutable parsed syntax, qualified ordinary typed bodies and exact linked programs between compatible worktrees. Parser authority, compiler and serializer contracts, semantic source layout and context, and native object/tool/runtime/linker inputs determine admission; source literals and diagnostic paths are never blindly rewritten. Fresh declaration and policy checks, mutable indexes, rendered verdicts and materialized executables remain local. Bounded checksummed records publish atomically under per-key locks, differing valid outputs for one key fail explicitly, and damaged or absent records miss. No-cache and verification requests suppress shared compiler participation; configured invalid storage fails before private replay.
- [NATIVE-CACHE-RETENTION-1] Shared native compiler artifacts and test successes use retention-aware namespaces isolated from older nonparticipating producers. Every storage operation holds a shared process lease through reading, staging, publication and publication-lock waits; collection acquires an exclusive nonblocking lease and skips an active store. Verified hits renew eviction recency without establishing validity. Configurable byte and entry budgets evict least recently used records; optional age limits use last verified access, with no age expiry by default. Opportunistic collection is throttled and explicit native collection previews unless applied. Bounded inventories preserve unknown files and report incomplete or unmet budgets, permanent coordination locks survive collection, and evicted or damaged results safely miss and refill. Policy changes are explicit and persistent; neither collection nor configuration requires Python or server admission.
- [NEW-CORE-TEST-WHOLE-1] A test program also keys all its ready tests at once, by joining their per-test keys (NEW-CORE-TEST-KEYS-1) and configured cases. When that key has a receipt, every one of them is `cached` with a single receipt read; the key's receipt is saved when every test it covers passed or was reused. Otherwise each test's own key decides, so a test whose own reachable program did not change stays `cached`. Tests that may call `Host`, a builder refusal and an unreadable result skip the whole-file key.
- [NEW-CORE-TEST-KEYS-1] A test program lowers all its ready tests once and derives each test's receipt key from that one lowering instead of lowering each test's program again: every function gets a canonical digest — callees by name and signature, strings by their bytes, types by structure (a cycle through a box by its depth), equality adapters by the type they compare and rendering adapters by their descriptors — and a test's key hashes its own root, the sorted digests of every function it reaches, its entry's inputs (streams, capability families, seed, parameter and result type names and result tags), the schema of the types its parameters and result reach when its entry generates or encodes values (types it cannot reach stay out), the settings of NEW-CORE-S4-TEST-3 and the entry path. Keys therefore change exactly when NEW-CORE-S4-TEST-6's per-test program does, independent of where the shared lowering placed functions, types or strings. Lowering metadata the digests do not recognize falls back to per-test lowering; `MYLANG_TEST_KEYS=per-test` forces it, as the oracle the derived keys are checked against.
- [NEW-CORE-TEST-KEYS-2] Neither a derived key (NEW-CORE-TEST-KEYS-1) nor a per-test program key (NEW-CORE-S4-TEST-6) hashes a loop's checker id, which counts loops program-wide and so moves with which modules the checker rechecked rather than loaded from its cache; a loop is keyed by its blocks. An edit that adds or removes a function elsewhere, or its revert, therefore no longer reruns tests whose code it did not touch.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-TEST-4] Declarative test cases accept explicit arguments, expectations, reasoned skips and exact provider overlays. Providers replace existing dependency paths, preserve the replaced module's own exported types and functions and the selected test signature with every type it reaches (other modules' exports are not compared, so a provider may import fewer modules) and retain original paths and embedded-asset roots. Invalid names, fields or traversal paths fail.

## New core S5

- [NEW-CORE-S5-RUNTIME-LAYOUT-1] The core runtime object compiles with `-falign-functions=64`, and its stack-floor signal stack is mapped when the program starts rather than reserved as a 256 KiB static array. Every runtime function starts on a cache line, so a change to one runtime function no longer moves the others' alignment, and no large `.bss` moves where the heap begins. Before this, unrelated runtime edits moved allocation- and text-heavy rows by 5–10% (S5-X: `packet_decode`, `csv_aggregate`, with identical instruction counts and more branch misses).
- [NEW-CORE-S5-COLD-INDEX-1] A list or fixed-array bounds trap that reports the negative index the program wrote (NEW-CORE-S5-UNITS-1) recovers it inside the trap's cold block (a list's written index is tested first and reported as written, NEW-CORE-F2-WRAP-1), so an in-bounds index costs no more than before: the in-bounds path carries only the comparison and the branch.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-VIEW-LEN-1] `len()` of a dictionary's `keys()` or `values()` view, or of any buffer operation typed `int`, is an `int` like `len(d)`: the IR's `u64` length is converted exactly, so the value can be stored, added or returned from `main`; it was an invalid-IR internal error before.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-CAP-COLLECTIONS-1] A fixed array of capabilities (`[N]Stream`, `[N]Host`, `[N]Filesystem`, …, nested or inside a record or union payload) builds in the new core as the old core runs it: a `Stream` or `Host` element is an uncounted `i64` token and a provider capability element keeps its count, so indexing, element writes, iteration, `enumerate`, `len`, copies, `mut` parameters and results print, exit and release exactly as the old core's build does.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-RUNTIME-ABI-1] Calls to the core's C runtime helpers follow the same SysV rule as source externs: a `bool`, `u8` or `u16` argument is zero-extended and an `i8` or `i16` one sign-extended on the declaration and the call (`myl_core_text_case`, `myl_core_text_repr`, the trims, the sorts, `myl_core_str_from_bool` and the rest), so a Clang-compiled helper that reads the widened register never sees undefined upper bits.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-EXTERN-ABI-1] A source `extern def` argument of type `i8` or `i16` is passed sign-extended, and one of type `u8`, `u16` or `bool` zero-extended, as SysV x86-64 C callers do (LLVM `signext`/`zeroext` on the declaration and the call), so a C callee that reads the widened register sees the value; wider integers, `float` and results are unchanged. Aggregate (record, array, union, `str`, collection, capability) parameters and results and `mut` parameters stay rejected by the checker (`E_EXTERN_SIGNATURE`, `E_MUTATION_MARKER`), as in the old core.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-CLI-1] `mylang build` (and `build --run`), `run`, `run-project` and `test` run the new core (`src/core/cli.zig`), and `dump-ir`, `dump-typed` and `coverage` are `mylang` developer commands. They take the old CLI's spellings: `--flag VALUE` or `--flag=VALUE`; `--qbe PATH` names a tool the new core never runs and is ignored; `--project` and `--whole-program` ask for nothing more (S5-D6); `--mode ReleaseFast` is `--release`. A build or run prints the front end's warnings to stderr before the program runs, as the old CLI did, and `--diagnostics=json` renders them, check errors, the new core's refusals and a trap as `diagnostic-v1` records (a trap's record carries its frames and the runtime's `trap_detail`).
- [NEW-CORE-S5-RUN-1] `mylang run FILE.my [options] [-- ARGS...]` builds the program with the new core and runs it: its exit status is `main`'s value `& 0xFF`, a trap exits 1, a check error 1, a usage or host failure 2, and a signal 128 plus its number. Every value after `--` is the program's. Grants (`--restrict`, `--allow-*`) and `--print-result` travel in `MYLANG_OPTIONS`, which the runtime reads: a repeated or empty grant is a usage error, an ungranted capability under `--restrict` exits 2 before `main` runs, and `--print-result` prints `main`'s whole value on its own line at exit, in a built program too. `--max-steps N`, `--max-frames N`, `--timeout SECONDS` and `--max-memory SIZE` (each also `--flag=N`; else `limits.run` in `mylang.json`; memory else `MYLANG_MEMORY_LIMIT`, then 4 GiB; `0` removes the byte ceiling) are installed by `main` before anything else: steps and frames are charged as a test's (NEW-CORE-S4-STEPS-1, NEW-CORE-S4-FRAMES-1) and trap `R_STEP_LIMIT` and `R_FRAME_LIMIT`, the byte ceiling traps `R_MEMORY_BUDGET` and lowers `RLIMIT_AS` to it plus 256 MiB (NEW-CORE-S4-MEMORY-1), and the wall-clock limit ends the program with `R_TIMEOUT` and status 1. The program gets the caller's address space back from the compiler's own ceiling and, under a frame budget, a stack of 32 KiB per frame plus 8 MiB; a frame budget the byte ceiling cannot back is refused before building. A missing or directory entry is refused by name with status 2.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-ONE-BINARY-1] `mylang` is the one compiler binary (S5-D8): the separate `mylang-core` executable and `zig build core` are gone, and the new core's own tests drive `mylang`. A `mylang build` that names one of the core's developer-only switches — `--sabotage-facts[=widening|length]`, `--generic-vectors`, `--trap-frames=MODE` — is answered by the core's own build command, as `mylang-core build` answered it: its flag set (no `--run`), its source-path display and every build failure exit 1.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-RETIRED-1] The old back end's commands and flags are refused before anything runs, with status 2 and one line naming the retirement and what replaces it: `invoke`, `emit-qbe`, `emit-project-qbe`, `emit-runtime`, `dump-bytecode`, `compile-module`, `link-modules`, `native-test-graph`, `native-test-compile` and `bench-promote`; on any command, `--gc-runtime`, `--gc-stress`, `--rc-runtime`, `--legacy-sharing`, `--value-semantics`, `--library`, `--provider`, `--link-library`, `--library-path` and `--explain-ownership`; on `build` and `run`, `--backend`, `--llvm-opt`, `--lto`, `--target`, `--keep-symbols` and `--step-accounting`; on `test`, `--pure-skip`, `--diagnostics`, `--max-calls` and the `--bench*` flags (S5-D6). On `build`, `run` and `test`, `--explain-invalidation` (the old back end's stage fingerprints; `--report` says what the caches served, and on `test`, which refuses `--report`, `--json`), `--friction-log` (recorded by `mylang check` only) and `--timings=json` (the old phase profile) are refused as retired, and on `test`, `--report` and `--timings`.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-CHECK-1] `mylang check` of a project entry checks the merged program with the semantic cache, as the new core's front end does; it no longer reads or writes the old back end's per-module `compile-module` records, and its diagnostics are unchanged.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-ARENA-1] `with arena():` is rejected with `E_ARENA_REMOVED`; use ordinary statements after removing the header and dedenting its body. The targeted error and certain repair remain available alongside independent semantic errors.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-ARENA-2] Removed arena blocks have no replacement escape or confinement rules: migrating them to ordinary statements and unwrapping `name.escape(value)` preserves ordinary value semantics. Nested removals converge through `mylang fix`.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-ARENA-3] `E_ARENA_REMOVED` carries a structured fix, so `mylang fix` migrates a file to a clean check: it deletes the `with arena() [as name]:` line (keeping a trailing `# comment` on it as a comment line at the block's indentation), dedents every non-blank body line, and the indented comment lines right after the body, by the body's one level, and rewrites each `name.escape(value)` of that block to `value` (to `(value)` unless `value` is a name, literal, call, field or index). A nested block is migrated in a later round. No fix is offered when the edit is not certain: a header not alone on its line, a tab in the indentation of the header or of a body line, or a triple-quoted string in the body. Body lines indented less than one level (comments, bracket continuations) are left as they are. The error and its fix are derived from syntax even when a body fails semantic checking, so a check that reuses cached bodies reports them exactly as a fresh one does. Disjoint, non-nested with scopes may reuse the arena name; nested same-name bindings remain ambiguous and receive no repair.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-HOST-1] `host def` is refused at check time (S5-D5): the declaration fails with `E_HOST_RETIRED` at its `host` word, saying host functions and their registered adapters were retired and to declare the function `extern def` and link its C implementation with `--link FILE.c`, so `check`, `run`, `build` and `test` stop there instead of a run failing with `HostMissingRegistration`. No `mylang fix` is offered: the C side has to be written. `extern def` is unchanged.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-DOCTEST-1] `mylang doctest` runs examples through the new `run`: a unit marked `legacy-sharing` or `gc-runtime` is skipped as documenting a retired mode, and a `value-semantics` unit runs without the retired switch, since value semantics is always on.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-NEGATIVE-INDEX-1] A list or fixed-array read or write whose negative index stays negative after counting from the end traps `R_LIST_BOUNDS` with the index the program wrote, in the text (`(index -5, length 2)`) and in the `trap_detail` record, in debug and release builds, as the old core reports it; an index past the end is reported unchanged.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-STREAM-WRITE-1] `stream.write(value)` answers what it wrote as `len` counts it, in the new core as in the old: the Unicode scalars of a `str`, the bytes of a `bytes` or `bytearray`; a write whose answer is discarded computes nothing more in a release build.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-EMBED-1] A header-less file `mylang build`/`run` checks alone reads the files its `embed_bytes`/`embed_str` calls name, relative to its own directory and confined to its `mylang.json` root, as the old CLI did; the build replays only while those files are unchanged.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-LABELS-1] The LLVM block that continues after a check is named from its block's own label and a fresh counter, not from the chain of labels before it, so a block with any number of checks (a thousand-term sum) builds in debug and release.
- [MYLANG-START-1] `mylang` starts with the allocator, I/O, arena and environment Zig's start code would give `main`, except that the Debug and ReleaseSafe allocator records no stack trace per allocation. It keeps Zig's leak, double-free and write-after-free checks. The first stack trace parsed and sorted the unwind tables of the whole executable: about 17 ms of every Debug `mylang` start, two thirds of a cached `mylang run`.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-UNITS-1] The Zig unit tests build and run guest programs with the new core: `native_execute.run` writes a compilation's sources (one file, or a project's modules and the files they embed) into a content-addressed directory under the suite cache and runs `mylang run --diagnostics=json --cache-report --print-result` on it with the run's budgets, grants, arguments, working directory and environment. The build's warnings end at the cache report and are not the program's stderr; a trap's record is restored to the runtime's text, frames and machine record with the directory removed from its paths, so a test sees the compilation's own paths; a program the front end accepts but the new core refuses fails the test with the refusal printed.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-UNITS-BATCH-1] A Zig unit test can run several guest programs as one batch: `native_execute.runAll`, or a `Batch` the test fills, checks every program and writes its sources in order, then runs the programs on up to `wiring.lanes()` threads and returns the runs in job order. The width is `MYLANG_UNIT_JOBS`, else at most eight CPUs divided among the `MYLANG_UNIT_SHARDS` shards `zig build test` runs at once, so a focused `test-units` run builds a test's programs in parallel while the gate's shards stay one program at a time. A rerun of a program under another budget shares its written sources, and a front-end error stops the batch before any program runs.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-POLICY-1] An import the project's configured policy denies stops `mylang build`, `run` and `test` with the policy's report and status 3, as the old CLI's policy gate did; a `mylang.json` that does not load stops them with status 2, and other source failures keep status 1.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-TEST-1] An exported `test_*` function whose result is neither `bool` nor a `Result` of one, and that no cases file gives an expected value, is a failed case (`test result type is not bool`; a property test's says `a property test returns bool or Result[bool, E]`) rather than an unsupported one, so the run exits 1, as the old runner reported it.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-TEST-STATUS-1] `mylang test` exits 3 when its selection is empty or every selected test was skipped by its cases file, as the old runner did: an all-skipped run is not a green run. An invalid cases file (`error: test cases: ...`: malformed JSON, an unknown test name or field) or an invalid provider fixture (`error: test providers: ...`, `InvalidTestProviders`, a fixture that does not declare) is a setup failure and exits 2, not 1; a directory run with any program that exits 2 exits 2.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-FANOUT-1] Without `--jobs` or `MYLANG_JOBS`, `mylang test` runs as many tests at once as there are CPUs, capped by how many per-test byte ceilings (each plus the 256 MiB address-space headroom) `MemAvailable` holds, at least one, as the old runner sized its pool; a directory run sizes its programs and the tests each runs from the same cap. An explicit width is not capped, and an unreadable `/proc/meminfo` leaves the CPU count.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-TEST-USAGE-1] A `mylang test` usage error says what is wrong on one `mylang test:` line before the test usage, which lists every flag the runner takes: `invalid value 'V' for --FLAG` for a malformed or out-of-range value (`--jobs 0`, `--jobs=x`, `--property-runs 0`, `--seed x`, `--timeout-ms 0`, …), `unknown option '--X'`, `missing value for '--FLAG'`, `unexpected argument 'X'` for a second entry, and `--affected and --against REF go together`. The status stays 2.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-TRACE-1] A trap lists at most its innermost sixteen frames, its site's line included, in a program and in a test, as the old core did; a test trial, which counts the frames it entered, then adds `  N frames omitted`. A budget's trap (`R_STEP_LIMIT`, `R_FRAME_LIMIT`, `R_MEMORY_BUDGET`) names no site (a budget is charged anywhere, and the last I/O call's site may be stale) and lists its frames from the innermost program frame; in a test it writes the trap record like any trap, so the runner reports it as `failed NAME: CODE`, the frames without the compiler's `__test_trial_*` wrapper, the omitted count and, after a blank line, the runtime's advice, instead of `NativeTestProcessFailed`.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-DEBUG-RELEASE-1] `mylang build FILE.my -o OUT --release` (or `--mode ReleaseFast`) drops every `debug(...)` line in lowering, its arguments unevaluated, as the old core's ReleaseFast build did (DEBUG-LINE-1); a debug build, `run`, `run-project`, `build --run` and `test` keep them in every mode. The choice is part of the build's replay key.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-AFFECTED-1] `mylang test ENTRY --affected --against REF` runs only the tests, of those `--filter` selects, whose Core IR build reaches a declaration changed since the git revision `REF`, with the changes derived under the project root as AFFECTED-TEST-2 derives them: a function the build reaches is a changed function, a generic specialization of one, or a function of a module changed outside its functions and constants. A changed name of a module the program loads that no checked `def` carries (a constant, which the front end folds into its readers, or a deleted function) keeps every test, as does a test the new core cannot build; a change in a module the program does not load selects nothing. It prints `affected: N selected, M skipped against REF` and one `skipped NAME: no changed declaration is reachable` line per dropped test on stderr, and an empty selection exits 3. `--against` without `--affected`, or the reverse, is a usage error (status 2), and a `REF` git cannot resolve exits 2 saying `--against names a revision git cannot resolve`. Such a run neither replays nor records a whole run's report, while each selected test still replays its own receipt, and a directory run passes both switches to every program it runs.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-NO-CACHE-1] `--no-cache` on `build`, `build --run`, `run`, `run-project` and `test` builds through an empty private cache (`no-cache-PID` under the cache directory, or under a run's own directory), removed when the command ends: nothing is read from or published to the build replay, program, foreign-object, front-end or test-receipt caches. The per-user store of content-keyed runtime and unit objects (`MYLANG_CORE_RUNTIME_CACHE`) is still used, as the old core's `--no-cache` kept its native object cache. Its status, output and diagnostics are those of the same command with caches.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-REPORT-1] `--report` on `build`, `build --run`, `run` and `run-project` prints the new core's cache report as one JSON line on stderr, in place of the retired `work-report-v1` and `qbe-build-v1` reports (S5-D6): `{"version":"cache-report-v1","program":P,"units":N,"reused":N,"compiled":N,"verified":N,"processes":N}`, the units linked, served by the object store, compiled and verified (`--verify-cache`) and the tool processes started, with `program` `linked`, `reused` (the program store served the executable) or `replayed` (the last identical build was replayed; every count is 0). `--cache-report` prints the same as one `mylang-core: cache:` text line. Builds with stage measurements also include `stages` (`stage-report-v3`): frontend rejection categories, raw bodies materialized versus left unloaded, final optimized bodies loaded, intermediate checkpoint records decoded (`optimizer.restores.checkpoints`), complete LLVM keys constructed, LLVM bytes materialized into emission output buffers, deferred native handles, and inclusive stage times. `optimizer.restores.final` replaces the former flat `final_restores` count. `nanoseconds.frontend_phases` lists opt-in frontend phase names, nanosecond durations and inclusive markers; nested intervals are not additive. These distinguish body processing from compact metadata visits and actual native compilations.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-TIMINGS-1] `--timings` on `run`, `run-project` and `build --run` prints `timings: native_ns=N` on stderr once the program exits: the awake-clock nanoseconds from starting the program's process to its exit, the build excluded. It changes neither the status, the output nor a trap's report; given twice or on `build -o` it is a usage error.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-CONTRACT-1] A broken contract clause's trap names it as the old core did: `error[R_REQUIRES]: precondition violated: MESSAGE`, `R_ENSURES` `postcondition violated`, `R_INVARIANT` `invariant violated`; a failed `assert` keeps `native execution failed`. A broken precondition's first line also names the caller that broke it, `; broken by the caller FUNCTION at PATH:LINE:COLUMN`, the first of the frames listed below it.

- [FRONTEND-COST-1] `mylang cost ENTRY.my [FUNCTION] [--json]` reports static Core costs per reachable emitted function and their totals: kept/proved checks, in-place/tested/copied updates, potential dynamic allocation sites, retains/releases, inlining and vector plans. Totals share the `build --stats` counters. Cost and `build --stats` collect LLVM optimization remarks from identical emitted units; LLVM vectorization is unknown without positive optimization remarks; `--llvm-remarks FILE.opt.yaml` reads successful LLVM loop-vectorization remarks separately from Core vector plans. Allocation classification includes runtime scratch, counts unresolved function values and foreign calls conservatively, and is shared with the build-time allocation promise. `--debug`, `--no-facts` and `--verify-facts` select the corresponding build pipeline.

- [FRONTEND-INTERFACE-COST-1] `mylang list-interfaces ENTRY.my --cost` preserves the default declaration listing and appends the same release per-function cost report and totals as `mylang cost ENTRY.my`. The option requires checked executable bodies, fails before printing a partial listing, and rejects duplicate `--cost` or combination with the JSON `--contracts` option. Ordinary listings remain unchanged. Main help lists the cost command and the optional interface costs.

## New core run-time counters

- [NEW-CORE-COUNTERS-1] `--counters` on `mylang build`, `build --run` and `run` makes a counting build: the program prints, returns and traps exactly as the ordinary build does, and at exit — or when a trap ends it — writes one `mylang-counters-v1` JSON document to the path in `MYLANG_COUNTERS` (else `mylang-counters.json` in its working directory). The document holds `totals` (calls, block allocations and frees with bytes, list growths, `realloc` growths and moves, list, dictionary, buffer and text copies with bytes, runtime retains and releases, dictionary, buffer and text growths, out-of-line text appends, byte pushes and byte appends, peak and final live block bytes), per-kind site sums (`kinds`), one row per emitted function that did anything (`functions`, the runtime's events charged to the emitted function running when they happened) and one row per executed site (`sites`: emitting function, source function, path, line, column, kind, the Core operation that emitted it, count, bytes, extra). Site kinds: inline `retain` (bytes: the shared block's data bytes) and `release` of one block, `release_unique`, `unique_test` (extra: tests that found the block unique), `list_copy`, `dict_copy`, `buffer_copy` and `text_copy` (bytes credited by the runtime), `record_copy` (a `memcpy`/`memmove` of a record, union or array over four words; the detail names the copying operation, `list_set.old` for the old element read to release it), `list_push` (extra: the bytes the growths it caused moved), and `element_load`/`element_store` of a whole aggregate of four words or less.
- [NEW-CORE-COUNTERS-2] A counting build compiles the counting runtime: the ordinary runtime source, unchanged in every ordinary build, with `runtime/core_count.h` inserted after its includes, a hook inserted at each anchored place (allocation, free, list growth and copy, dictionary copy and growth, buffer copy, growth and push, text copy, growth and append, the runtime's retains and releases, exit and trap exit), and `runtime/core_count.c` appended. An anchor that is missing or occurs twice fails the counting build with an error instead of leaving a path uncounted.
- [NEW-CORE-COUNTERS-3] Without `--counters` the emitted LLVM text, the runtime object and the executable are byte-identical to a build of the compiler without counting support. With it, every emitted function has a record that its prologue makes current and every return restores, every counted site is a record in section `myl_count_sites` kept by `@llvm.used`, list allocations and frees go through the runtime so they are counted, and the build links the counting runtime.
- [NEW-CORE-COUNTERS-4] `--counters` is accepted once per command line (a repeat is a usage error) and is part of the build's replay key, so a counting build never replays an ordinary build's executable or the reverse.
- [NEW-CORE-COUNTERS-5] `mylang cost ENTRY.my --counters FILE.json` prints the static cost report unchanged and then a counters section from one counting run's `mylang-counters-v1` document: per reported function its calls, inline retains and releases, runtime count updates, uniqueness tests and how many found the block shared, copies and bytes copied (records, lists, dictionaries, buffers, text), bytes moved by growth and managed allocations with bytes, then their total; with `--json` the same rows follow the static members as `counters`. A function the run never entered has a zero row. A missing or unreadable file, or a document that is not `mylang-counters-v1`, exits 2; `--counters` given twice or without a file is a usage error.
- [NEW-CORE-COUNTERS-6] A counting build also counts every conditional trap the emitted code executes, one site per check: `bounds_check` (list, text and byte index checks, a pop's empty-list and index checks, substring bounds), `overflow_check` (integer arithmetic, negation and `MIN // -1`), `division_check` (a zero divisor), `shift_check` (a shift amount) and `other_check` (casts, float conversions, fact verification and every other conditional trap); `count` is the check's executions, and `kinds` sums them. A check the core's passes removed is never counted, a check LLVM removes later still is, and an unconditional trap is no check. The program's output, status and traps are those of the ordinary build, and without `--counters` the emitted text is unchanged.
- [NEW-CORE-COUNTERS-7] `mylang cost ENTRY.my --counters FILE.json` adds, per reported function and in the total, the checks it executed by kind (`checks: bounds N, overflow N, division N, shift N, other N` at the end of each text line, a `checks` object with `bounds`, `overflow`, `division`, `shift` and `other` beside each JSON row's `run`).

## New core copy elimination: lists

- [NEW-CORE-CE-LISTS-1] A store into a field of a list element (`xs[i].f = v`, `xs[i].a.b = v`), which the builder lowers as a read of the whole element, a record update and a write of the whole element, becomes one `list_set_field` when nothing else uses the element read or the intermediate records and only instructions that cannot trap, write or have an effect lie between the read and the write: the list gets its copy-on-write form like any list update (in place when unique, after a count test when it may be shared, on a copy when it is still used afterwards), the value is stored at the field's offset inside the element, and the field it replaces is released after the store; the rest of the element is never read or copied. The store traps where the element read would have, with the same index, length and source position. An element read nothing uses whose bounds check the next access of the same element repeats, with only such instructions between, is dropped, the access taking its position; any other unused element read emits only its bounds check. A value read from the element before the store keeps the old element.
- [NEW-CORE-CE-LISTS-2] A whole-element store (`xs[i] = v`) of a managed element reads the old element's managed parts where they are — a record, array or union over four words part by part (a union's parts for every managed case, each released only under the case its tag names), anything of at most four words as a value — stores the new value, and then releases those parts, instead of copying the whole old element out first. A field store (NEW-CORE-CE-LISTS-1) releases the field it replaces the same way.
- [NEW-CORE-CE-LISTS-3] Ownership merges a read of a record's field into an identical read in another block when the earlier read runs on every path to the later one, every path from the earlier read reaches the later one or a trap, and nothing that could keep the earlier read from taking the field can run after it: no other read of the field nor a use of what that read reached, and no use of the record but reads of its fields, the field's write-back, and joins that receive the record only on edges taken before the earlier read (a record passed to a call between the two reads keeps them separate). An index whose negative branch splits `it.rt.stack[i] = v` or `rt.marker.marks[i] = False` into a read that reaches the list and a read that writes it back then lets the write-back take the record's field and the list in it, so the store updates the list in place when it is unique (after a count test) instead of copying the whole list on every store.
- [NEW-CORE-CE-LISTS-4] A borrowed part still in use when the counted value it was read from is consumed (a `mut` call on the record holding a list) is promoted to its own count; a list or dictionary that is still needed there only through the parts read out of it is not promoted itself, they are, unless a borrowed text view hangs below it. Counting a record, union or array over four words (a retain, a release) counts each part over four words where it is, field by field, instead of copying it out first. An element payload read from `rt.objects[ref]` before `allocate(mut rt, …)` and used after it holds its own count, so `allocate` finds `rt.objects` unique and appends or stores in place instead of copying the whole object table.
- [NEW-CORE-CE-LISTS-5] A slice used only as the source of a counted loop (`for b in xs[i:j]`) is never built when the builder's endpoint clamps prove both endpoints within `[0, len(xs)]` and nothing between the slice and the loop, or in the loop, updates, stores, returns, joins or passes `xs` to a call: the loop counts from the clamped start while below the clamped end and reads `xs` at that index, so it reads exactly the elements the slice held, in bounds, and `break`, `continue` and `return` leave it as before. A slice that is still built (one kept past the loop, measured, or whose source the loop can change) is allocated with room for exactly `max(end - start, 0)` elements, so filling it never grows it.
- [NEW-CORE-CE-LISTS-6] `buffer.append_range(data, start, end)` and `append_utf8(text)` compile to the range test's comparisons (`0 <= start <= end <= len(data)`, the method's result) and a `buffer_append` update: ownership gives the buffer its copy-on-write form like any other update, and the emitted code copies the octets in place and raises the length when the range is non-empty, valid and fits the buffer's room, calling the runtime's checked append only to grow the buffer or to append nothing. The appended octets, the result, the growth policy and a trap while growing (reported at the append) are those of the runtime call.
- [NEW-CORE-CE2-EFFECTS-1] `effects.analyze` summarizes every function's effect on each parameter as two sets of field paths (record field chains from the parameter; the empty path is the whole value): the paths the function may read, whose values its result, its other parameters' final values, its traps or its output may depend on, and, for a `mut` parameter, the paths its final value may differ in from the value passed. Summaries are transitive over direct calls and a least fixed point over recursion and mutual recursion; indirect, `extern` and runtime calls read and write everything they are given. A function whose declared result is a `bool` beside `mut` parameters also gets the writes of the returns that may answer `True` and of those that may answer `False`, and a function that returns a call's whole result answers as that call does.
- [NEW-CORE-CE2-EFFECTS-2] Memory homes interfere per field: a `record_set` that shares the home of the record it updates writes only its field, and an in-out call's final value that shares its argument's home is written only where the callee's effect summary says the parameter is written, so a value whose remaining reads touch only other bytes of that home (a view of a disjoint field) no longer forces a copy. `frame.code.consts[next_op(mut frame)]` with `next_op` writing only `frame.ip` passes the frame in its own memory; a callee that writes the field a live view holds, a view held across an update of its own field, loops, recursion, counted fields and union payloads keep value semantics, with no block live at exit.
- [NEW-CORE-CE2-EFFECTS-3] Before ownership, a whole-element store `list_set(xs, k, v)` whose element nothing can read before a later store of the same element overwrites it is removed: its list is followed forward through the records it is put back into, phis (the element's index crossing with the record that holds it) and calls whose effect summary neither reads it nor, for a normalized negative index, may change the list's length, until a store of the same element ends it on every path. An element read, a return, a call that may read the list, an indirect, `extern` or runtime call and anything else that lets the list escape keep the store; a trap does not, since it ends the process (test trials are forked). When only some successors of the store's block observe it, the store and its write-backs move to those successors, reading the record field again there so ownership takes it over, and the others go without. The removed store's bounds check stays at its place as an element read nothing uses, so an index past the list traps where it did. A dispatch loop that stores its frame at the end of a step and again after the next step's first read keeps the end-of-step store only on its return path.
- [NEW-CORE-CE2-EFFECTS-4] A whole-element store `list_set(xs, k, v)` of a record `v` that was read from element `k` of the same list, or last stored there, stores only the fields that may have changed since, one `list_set_field` each (none: the bounds check alone), with no whole-element copy and no count updates for the parts that did not change. The proof walks `v` back through record updates (a nested update counts as its inner field) and `mut` calls (their effect summaries) to that read or store, and the element back through the list's stores, records and calls that do not write it; at a phi it takes each predecessor in turn with the branch conditions that lead to that edge, so a call answering `True` contributes only the writes of its returns that may answer `True`. An element index is its integer before normalization from the end, from a field of a record or a value, plus a constant; a normalized index names the same element only while the list's length cannot have changed. Lox's dispatch loop stores only `frame.ip` per instruction; a list that grows or is replaced, an error path that resets the frame count, and an indirect read keep the whole store where the proof fails.
- [NEW-CORE-CE2-EFFECTS-5] A write-back `record_set(r, f, v)` whose field `f` was read from an earlier record that updates of other fields turned into `r` (`wb = it.rt; it.returned = pop(mut wb); it.rt = wb`) takes the field over from that read as a read of `r` itself would: the read owns the field and the write-back drops nothing, when the read runs ahead of the write-back on every path, every path from it reaches the write-back or a trap, and nothing but reads of other fields and those updates uses the records in between while the read holds the field. A copy of the whole record or a call that reads it in between keeps the ordinary counted form. The field is then updated where it lies, with no retain of it and no release of the old one.
- [NEW-CORE-CE2-EFFECTS-6] A record or union construction whose one use is a whole-element store in a later block (past the index's negative-index branch or the list's uniqueness test, `frames[k] = Frame(...)`) is built straight into the element, as one in the store's own block is: its operands are read at the store, and their homes are kept intact until then. The element's old managed parts are released after the store, and a shared list is copied first, as before.
- [NEW-CORE-CE2-RESULTS-1] A value of more than four words that a `ret` stores inside the returned value — a field or `Ok`/`Err` payload of a construction returned through `sret`, the declared result next to `mut` values, or the value a view of it lies in — is built at that place in the caller's destination `%ret` (`emit_homes.zig`), so `return Ok(token)`, `return Ok(make_token(...))` and `r = next_in(src, mut cur); ...; return r` store nothing: the place must fit inside `%ret` (bytes of in-out final fields, which the caller reads where it passed them, may be used), share no byte with another value in `%ret` that is live when it is written, is read by its defining instruction or meets it on a phi's edge, and no store of any `ret` may overwrite it before that `ret` reads it.
- [NEW-CORE-CE2-RESULTS-2] A `ret` whose stores into `%ret` would overwrite a value that lives in `%ret` (built there for another return) before reading it — `return Pair(x=w.y, y=w.x)` where `w` is returned whole on another path — builds its value in a temporary and copies it to `%ret`, so the swapped halves are the old ones.
- [NEW-CORE-CE2-RESULTS-3] Results built in `%ret` behave exactly as copies would: early returns of other shapes, `Err` paths of a `?`-style match and of `?` itself (`first = next(mut tok)?` then `return Ok(first)`), a callee's payload returned under another error type, snapshots of a `mut` cursor, swaps and duplicated halves next to a `mut` record, values rebuilt while an earlier one is read, results carried around loops, and a trap between building a result in `%ret` and returning it print the same at debug, release and `--verify-facts`, with no block live at exit.
- [NEW-CORE-CE2-RESULTS-4] A list element of more than four words read with `list_get` or `list_get_field` is lent where it lies — its value is the address of its slot, with no copy — when every use of it follows it in its block and is a by-value argument of an internal call (a read-only pointer the callee never writes), a load of a part held in registers or a count increment, the calls it is passed to take no `mut` parameter that holds a block (so none can reach the list), and nothing between the read and its last use can write, free or move a list (only inert instructions, count increments and such calls). `is_free(objs[i])` passes the element itself; `grow(mut h, h.items[0])` and a read whose slot is overwritten before the call still copy.
- [NEW-CORE-CE2-RESULTS-5] Sharing a by-value argument's memory with the callee's `sret` destination (`Planner.shareable`: an internal function with no in-out parameter, nothing living in `%ret` before a `ret`, and no `ret` that reads a byte of the parameter after one of its own stores wrote it) is off (`emit_homes.steps.share_sret = false`): such a callee would be defined with neither the parameter nor `%ret` `noalias`, and the rows' `record_pass` (`s = advance(s, x)` over a twelve-field record) ran 15-19% slower than the one copy per call it saves. Every function keeps `noalias` on `%ret` and its parameters, and a loop's `s = advance(s, x)` copies the call's result once. R3's sole-pointer noalias prototype likewise regressed record_pass instructions (1.207x) and is retained only in experiment history.
- [NEW-CORE-CE2-RESULTS-6] A read `wb = r.f` that a later `r.f = ...` replaces takes the field over (the record's reference goes to the read, so a `mut` call or update of `wb` finds a unique block) even when, after the read, a call borrows `r` — `table.set(mut wb, name, string_hash(rt, name), item)` — provided that call is in the read's block and runs before every use of the read: it sees the field as it was, and if it keeps a reference to it the update copies. A read of `r.f` after the `mut` call still sees the old value, so the field is then retained as before.
- [NEW-CORE-CE2-RESULTS-7] A container update of a list element's field — `xs[i].items.append(v)`, `xs[i].items.pop()`, any update of a block-holding field `f` that the builder writes back as `list_set(xs, i, record_set(g, f, v))` after its transferred element read `g` — reads only that field, as a transferred `list_get_field` that takes the slot's owner (so the update finds the block unique when the list's element held its only reference), and stores it back with a `list_set_field` that releases nothing; the element and its other fields are never copied out or back. The read keeps the element read's bounds check and source position; a shared list is still detached first, and a field shared with another value is still copied by the update.
- [NEW-CORE-CE2-MERGE-1] Per-field interference narrows what a call writes in an argument's home to the callee's write summary only for a `mut` (owned) parameter. A by-value argument that shares the call's `sret` result home (`s = rotate(s, j)`) is overwritten whole by the result, so every value still reading that home — the argument itself, a copy kept from it — keeps its own home.

## New core runtime parity: aliasing and overflow facts (F1)

- [NEW-CORE-F1-TBAA-1] A release or debug build tags each scalar a list operation loads or stores in a list's element data (`list_get`, `list_set`, `list_get_field`, `list_set_field`, `list_push`, and the old element a store reads to release it) with a TBAA type of its LLVM storage type — `myl.data.double`, `.i64`, `.i32`, `.i16`, `.i8` (bytes and booleans) or `.ptr` — each a child of `myl.data`. A union's tag word and payload (any element, field or path that passes through a union), whole-value copies (`llvm.memcpy`, untagged) and every access that is not a list element (homes, boxes, return slots, dictionary entries, stack slots) keep the generic `myl.data`, which aliases every child. A list's element type is fixed for its block's lifetime, so the bytes at one offset of an element are only ever accessed as one scalar type or generically; answers are unchanged in every mode.
- [NEW-CORE-F1-TBAA-2] The inline free of a small block (`myl_core_free`) starts its free-list push with a compiler barrier (`asm sideeffect "", "~{memory}"`): a freed block may come back from the inline allocator as a list of another element type in the same function, and no load or store may move across the free. Frees and allocations that call the runtime are opaque calls and order every access already.
- [NEW-CORE-F1-STOREBACK-1] A whole-element store `list_set(xs, k, v)`, or a `list_set_field` of a block-pointer field reached without crossing a union, that puts back a block pointer a transferred read of the same element (same list value, index and field) took out — `c[i][j] = v` stores row `c[i]` back — follows `v` to that read through in-place updates (`list_set`, `list_set_field`, `list_pop`), copies, growth and replacements of the block and phis (a loop that carries it included). When every path is an in-place update, the slot already holds `v`'s pointer and nothing is stored; otherwise the store runs only when `v`'s pointer differs from the read's, which is when copy on write, growth or a replacement moved it. The transferred read owns the old pointer, so nothing is released either way, in every build mode.
- [NEW-CORE-F1-OVERFLOW-1] In a counted loop (`counter = phi(start, counter + 1)` while `counter < bound`), a header phi `acc = phi(init, next)` whose `next` is `acc` moved by checked or proven additions and subtractions, joins of such values inside one iteration (a conditional `count += 1`), and inner counted loops that move it (an inner loop's own such phi, entered from a value moved from `acc`) grows by a step in `[lo, hi]` per iteration, and so lies in `[init.lo + min(0, trips × lo), init.hi + max(0, trips × hi)]`, with `trips` the counted loop's trip bound (a list or byte length bounds it by `max_list_length`); an inner loop contributes its entry's step plus its own trips times its own step. A wrapping operation, a value not derived from `acc`, a loop that is not counted, or a walk of more than 64 values gives no interval. Checks the intervals rule out become `proven`; verify builds assert each interval (`R_CORE_FACT`). A fact pass that does not converge also counts every block reachable.
- [NEW-CORE-F1-OVERFLOW-2] A branch edge on `x * x <= y`, `x * x < y` or `x * x == y` (either side, a checked or proven product) bounds `x` to `[-r, r]` where it dominates, `r` the integer square root of `y`'s top (less one for `<`): `while i * i <= n` keeps `i` and `i + 1` and `j + i` (with `j <= n` from its own loop condition) in range, so their checks become `proven`.
- [NEW-CORE-F1-OVERFLOW-3] A release build's first fact pass (parameters unknown) runs callees before callers (a cycle is broken where it closes) and gives each internal function with an integer or boolean result the hull of the intervals of its reachable `ret` operands; every direct call of it then lies in that interval, in the first pass (so its argument intervals reach `NEW-CORE-FACTS-10`'s parameter intervals) and in the real one. `step(a, b) = (a + b) % 1000003` called as `total = step(total, i)` with `i >= 0` gets `a` in `[0, 1000002]`, its sum proven and its floor remainder unsigned with no fix-up; one call that may pass a negative argument keeps the fix-up. Verify builds assert each call's result interval.
- [NEW-CORE-F1-OVERFLOW-4] A `proven` (or unsigned) 64-bit division or remainder whose operands the trusted facts put in `[0, 2^32 − 1]` masks each operand to its low 32 bits (`and i64 x, 4294967295`, the same value) before its `udiv`/`urem`, so LLVM knows the high bits are zero and divides by a constant with the short reciprocal sequence; verify builds never narrow.
- [NEW-CORE-F1-OVERFLOW-5] `--sabotage-facts=overflow` builds verify mode with the overflow rules each one off on the inside — a counter's per-iteration step one smaller, `x * x <= y` bounding `x` by its square root less one, a callee's return interval without its top — and the verify assertions catch each of them (`R_CORE_FACT`) on a program that reaches the bound.
- [NEW-CORE-F2-WRAP-1] A checked list access (`list_get`, `list_get_field`, `list_set`, `list_set_field`) whose index is the builder's Python normalization of a written index `i` (`i < 0 ? len + i : i`, a phi whose operand on the `i >= 0` edge is `i`) first compares `i` with the length unsigned: an index below the length is non-negative and its own normalization, so the element's address takes `i` directly; only the others take the normalized value and its check, on a cold path that traps reporting `i` as written. The check is counted once (`bounds_check`). A later access the facts proved, whose written index and list length are those of a checked access that dominates it (`index_keys`), reuses that access's index value instead of normalizing again. Answers and traps are unchanged in every build mode.
- [NEW-CORE-F2-INDEX-1] A counted fill loop's list length fact (`length = max(n, 0)`) holds only where the loop's exit dominates the access: inside the loop the list has as many elements as iterations so far, so a read of `zs[i]` before `zs.append(i)` keeps its check (main 35df839be's release builds proved it and read past the end). Where it holds, an index whose interval lies below the least value of `n` is in range (`flags[j]` with `j <= n` and `n + 1` flags).
- [NEW-CORE-F2-INDEX-2] Value and length keys (`index_keys.zig`): a field read of a record this function built or updated is the value stored there (through `record_set`s of other fields to the `record_set` or `record_make` that stored it); a phi whose operands on executable edges are one value (or the phi itself) is that value; an index key adds a constant moved by checked or proven additions and subtractions; a list's length key sees through `list_set`, `list_set_field`, `list_copy`, `list_sort` and phis whose operands all have one key. Keys never look through a phi of different values, so they relate values of one iteration only. A dominating branch edge `x < len(other)` (or `len(other) > x`, through value-preserving conversions) proves an access to a list with `other`'s length key at an index with `x`'s key that the facts put at or above zero: a list written through a copy, the iterated list of `for x in xs`, two reads of one record's list field.
- [NEW-CORE-F2-INDEX-3] After ownership, a list access whose check a dominating access already made becomes `proven` (`index_checks.zig`): the same length key and index key, and, when the access's index may be negative as a plain index, a dominating check that shows its sign (a plain index's check does; a written index's normalized check does not, unless the facts put the index at or above zero). It covers the read and field write ownership splits a whole-element write into, the swap after Hoare partition's scans, and a pop's push after reads of the same slot. A pop, a push, a field write of the index or a different list between the two accesses changes a key, and the later access keeps its check. Verify builds assert each such check (`R_CORE_FACT`).
- [NEW-CORE-F2-INDEX-4] `--sabotage-facts=index` builds verify mode with the dominating-check rule taking a check to cover the next index up too; the verify assertion catches it (`R_CORE_FACT`) on `xs[i]` then `xs[i + 1]` with `i` the last index.
- [NEW-CORE-F2-INDEX-5] An access whose list's length key and index key both name one phi `p` (a field of it, or `p` itself), whose block dominates the access, is also covered when, on the edge from each predecessor of `p`'s block, a check made in that predecessor or a block dominating it has the keys `p`'s operand on that edge gives (`rt.frames[rt.frame_count - 1]` at the top of the Lox VM's dispatch loop: read before the loop, and at the end of each pass). Entering the block, `p` is that operand, and nothing defines it again before the access; each covering check runs before the access it covers, so the first failure would have to be an earlier check.
- [NEW-CORE-F2-FIELD-1] A release build gives each integer or boolean field of a closed record type a whole-program interval (`fields.zig`): the hull of every value the program's writes store into it — `record_make` (each field), `record_set` (a `mut` callee's included) and `list_set_field` (a field inside a list element) — each at its definition under the first fact pass's facts. Records are values, so moving one into or out of a list, dictionary, box, union, array, phi, call or return copies it whole and writes no field. Two rounds run, the second under the first's intervals; each round's hulls hold whatever the previous round knew, and a field no write reaches has none. A record value the function has no view of its own (a parameter, a call's result, an element read) starts from its type's intervals, which its field updates and joins then carry (`Aggregates.fields`); a field read (`record_get`, or a `list_get_field` whose path ends in a record field) takes the interval (met with the function's own view of the record): `table[s.index]` with every `Slot.index` written as `k % 4` (`k >= 0`) needs no normalization from the end and, on a four-element table, no check. Verify builds assert every read's interval (`R_CORE_FACT`).
- [NEW-CORE-F2-FIELD-2] A record type is closed only when every record of it is made by the program: no runtime, extern or host function takes or returns a value containing it, the entry function's parameters and the parameters of every function a function value names (it may be called from outside) do not contain it, and no operation other than the copying ones (`phi`, `copy`, calls, `record_*`, list, union, box, array and dictionary element operations, `param`, `undef`, `func_ref`) produces a value containing it. A record inside an open record is open too. An open type's fields have no interval.
- [NEW-CORE-F2-FIELD-3] `--sabotage-facts=field` builds verify mode with each field interval's top dropped; the verify assertion catches it (`R_CORE_FACT`) where a read sees the largest value written.
- [NEW-CORE-F2-VERSION-1] Bounds versioning (`version.zig`), after the trip-count guards: a counted loop (`i` from `S` while `i < N`, step one, `S` and `N` fixed for the loop, and `N` not computed inside an enclosing loop: a bound that is data, such as fannkuch's flip count, is often a few trips that the guard's cost outweighs) whose body makes no call, holds no other loop and has at most 256 instructions (NEW-CORE-FSX-VERSION-1's rule), whose header carries only integers, floats and booleans and which leaves only those to the code after it (a copy that carries a list or record out gives ownership joins to reason through: a function of such copies took minutes to compile), and checks list accesses at `I + i`, `i - I` or `I - i` (checked or proven arithmetic, after a folded normalization from the end too) with `I` fixed for the loop (defined outside it, or an unchecked or proven sum, difference or product of such values, recomputed before the loop) and the list derived, through element writes, copies and sorts that keep its length, from the list the loop is entered with, is cloned behind one guard before it. The guard measures that entry list (the loop holds it anyway; an older value of the same length would live longer and cost copies on write) and tests, for every such access, that its least index over the trips `S..N-1` is at least 0 and its greatest below the length; a value the facts do not put within `[-2^60, 2^60]` is tested to lie there too, so the guard's unchecked sums are exact whenever they decide. The clone runs with those checks proven and their normalizations from the end folded away; the original, unchanged, runs when the guard fails, so a loop that would trap traps at the same trip with the same report, after the same output. The facts run again after.
- [NEW-CORE-F2-VERSION-2] `--sabotage-facts=version` builds verify mode with each bounds guard admitting one index past the end; the verify assertion of the clone's proven check catches it (`R_CORE_FACT`) on a loop that reaches that index.
- [NEW-CORE-FSX-VERSION-1] A release build versions a counted reduction loop under a trip-count guard (`version.versionReductions`) only when the loop's body makes no `call` or `call_indirect` and contains no other loop. The clone saves one overflow check per trip, which pays only where LLVM can then vectorize or unroll a small body; around a call or an inner loop it saves nothing measurable, and the duplicated body changes LLVM's code choices (a sum of binary-search results lost its conditional moves and ran 3.7 times slower). Such a loop keeps its exact check.
- [NEW-CORE-U1-GROW-1] A large block (one outside the small-block classes) of a byte buffer grows with `realloc`, as a large list already did: the C library remaps a large block instead of copying it, so growth neither copies the octets in use nor faults fresh pages in; a block `realloc` leaves misaligned moves to a fresh 32-byte-aligned block. A byte buffer keeps its doubling policy (at least 8, doubled until the request fits) and its observable length and octets; a small buffer still grows into a fresh block. A counting build counts each such growth under `reallocs` (and `realloc_moves` when the block moved), not `buffer_grows`.
- [NEW-CORE-U1-HOIST-1] An in-place update (`list_push`, `list_set`, a buffer append, a text append, a dictionary store, …) of a value that may be shared tests its count each time it runs (form 2), unless the value reaches it through a loop header's phi and the loop cannot share it: then each edge entering the loop gets one `is_unique` test of the entering value, which is copied there when shared (and the shared original released), and every update of the value inside the loop runs in place untested (form 1; verify builds assert the count). The entering value must be owned and its edge into the loop its last use, and the loop must give the value no second owner that is still held at the update: no copy or alias kept across it, no append or store of it into another value, no call that may keep it. The outermost loop that qualifies is chosen. A loop that never reaches its update still copies a value shared at its entry; the answer is the same.
- [NEW-CORE-U1-STATE-1] A release build reads a scalar field (an integer, float, `bool` or capability) of a record from the value last put there instead of the record's memory (`forward.zig`, after ownership): through `record_set`s of other fields, from the value a `record_set` of that field or a `record_make` stored, and through a phi of records as a new phi of that field's values. A record that is none of those (a parameter, a call's result, an element read) has its field read once, right where it is defined. So a loop that reads and writes `frame.ip` or `rt.stack_top` carries it in a register; the record's memory still receives every store, so calls that are handed the record, copies of it, returns and traps see every field as the program left it. Each new value gets the interval the facts know for its record's field.
- [NEW-CORE-U1-STATE-2] The emitter keeps the length and capacity of a list or byte buffer that a loop appends to in two entry-block slots, which LLVM promotes to registers (`emit_state.zig`): a *web* is the phis and in-place updates (`list_push` other than counted fills and stack pushes, `buffer_append`) that make one value from the one before, kept when it has a phi in a loop, every update updates a member, and inside the loops its phis run in nothing but those updates and phis uses a member. A value entering the web on an edge that jumps to the phi has its header loaded into the slots there; an update appends at the slots' length (a push that must grow stores the length back, calls `myl_core_list_grow` and loads the grown capacity; a buffer append's slow path stores the length back before `myl_core_buffer_append_checked` and loads the result's header after); every other use of a member (a read, a call, a release, a return, a phi outside the web) and every verify-build fact check of one is preceded by a store of the slots' length into its header.
- [NEW-CORE-U1-SINK-1] An element store that `NEW-CORE-CE2-EFFECTS-3` sinks into the successors that observe it is weighed again where each copy lands, up to 48 blocks down, so a store ahead of an if-elif ladder (a VM's `rt.frames[rt.frame_count - 1] = frame` after each opcode read) ends in the arms that read the list, return it, or pass it to a call that may read it, and the other arms go without. A store whose two successors both observe it still moves into both when one of them branches, within three levels, to an arm that does not. Every bounds check stays where the store was; the copies, which that check covers for the same list and index, check nothing again.

- [JUICE-NATIVE-1] `mylang juice` implements doctor, analyze, record, compare, validate and calibrate inside the installed compiler without Python or a source checkout. Capture validates declared workload contracts, immutable raw evidence, complete instruction counts, correctness, separate semantic counters and bounded process groups; reports distinguish instruction work, uncertainty and held-out runtime estimates. Platform-specific capture services remain separate from JSON reporting.
- [JUICE-CONTENT-HASH-1] Native Juice computes standard SHA-256 over every actual source, tool and running executable byte using a bounded streaming implementation compiled independently in ReleaseFast, including inside a Debug compiler. Digests remain independent of file size, timestamps, embedded source identity and previous invocations; same-size writes with restored mtime and atomic replacements are reread.

- [JUICE-RUNTIME-1] Juice’s opt-in perf runtime profile collects grouped, unscaled user instructions, cycles and reference cycles on one pinned CPU. Every event must be positive, present exactly once and report 100% running; otherwise capture remains incomplete. Comparisons keep instruction work separate from the reference-cycle runtime proxy, abstain above 3% observed reference-cycle spread (or paired-ratio spread for at least five explicitly matched, complete capture pairs) and never label a prediction as validated. `validate-runtime` checks the fixed 5% relative-error target using at least five independent paired elapsed timings tied to the same comparison, workload and context; uncertain timings or estimates cannot pass. Validation applies only to the measured case.

## Readable frontend

- [FRONTEND-NUMBERS-1] `parse_int(text)` and `parse_float(text)` parse a string using the pinned numeric grammar and return `int?` and `float?`. `int(number)` and `float(number)` convert numbers. Applying a numeric conversion to text reports `E_NUMERIC_PARSE` with an exact callee-name repair; parsing a numeric argument is a type error. Declared functions and bindings can shadow these prelude names.

- [FRONTEND-MIGRATION-1] Frontend migrations publish structured diagnostic edits from parsed spans. Growable arrays use `T[]`, optional layers use `T?`, fixed arrays use `FixedArray[T, N]`, and the unit type is `Unit`. Parameters put `mut` and individual `named` markers before their names. Comments, strings, unrelated names, canonical source whitespace, and optional nesting survive migration.
- [FRONTEND-FORMAT-1] Formatting keeps contextual expression words (`try`, `then`, `yield`, `fail`) separated from their operands and preserves the spacing between optional result types and failure clauses; formatting remains parse-only.
- [FRONTEND-FUNC-1] Function declarations and callable types use `func`; structured migration edits replace legacy `def` tokens without rewriting strings or comments. Project entry discovery and guest test discovery recognize `func` declarations, including public declarations.
- [FRONTEND-BINDINGS-1] Local declarations use `const` or `mut`; assignment updates an existing writable binding. An undeclared assignment is diagnosed with a declaration repair.
- [FRONTEND-BINDINGS-2] Locals have lexical block scope and may not shadow active locals or parameters. Independent sibling blocks may reuse names.
- [FRONTEND-MUT-1] Const ordinary data and ordinary parameters are transitively readonly, including nested fields, collection receivers and mut arguments. Loop and pattern binders are readonly. Capability permissions remain independent of binding mutability.
- [FRONTEND-MUT-2] Explicit mut parameters and arguments update caller storage for ordinary scalars, records and collections, including whole-value rebinding and nested field or element places. Readonly copies preserve value independence.
- [FRONTEND-MUT-3] Statically overlapping mut arguments are rejected. Dynamic indices and keys are captured once and checked for overlap before the callee runs, with disjoint targets allowed.
- [FRONTEND-TYPES-1] Postfix `T[]` is a growable array and `T?` an optional layer; suffix order and nested optional layers are preserved. `Unit` has value `()`. Fixed arrays use the explicit `FixedArray[T, N]` type and constructor. Present values inject into an expected optional without erasing existing layers.
- [FRONTEND-FAILURES-1] A function declared `-> T fails E` returns a checked Result value: ordinary returns construct success and `fail` constructs failure. Statement-boundary `try` unwraps success or propagates a compatible declared failure.
- [FRONTEND-MATCH-1] Enum construction and matching name payload fields. Nested patterns are checked exhaustively. A match expression yields one common result type on every continuing arm; return still leaves the enclosing function.
- [FRONTEND-NAMED-CALLBACK-1] Callable contracts retain named parameter labels and exact mutation positions, with positional binder names excluded from identity. Calls through a callable bind labelled operands to their declared parameters while evaluating arguments once in written order.

- [FRONTEND-RECORD-FIELDS-1] Record constructors name every supplied field. Known positional record or enum operands and patterns receive atomic label-insertion fixes that preserve written evaluation order, including imported types. Defaults retain their declared field identity.
- [FRONTEND-ARENA-REMOVED-1] Removed arena syntax reports a hard error with its certain source edits, including alongside other semantic errors; stores, returns, nested scopes and escapes migrate to accepted ordinary statements without changing their values.
- [FRONTEND-BINDING-MIGRATION-2] Implicit-binding declaration migration resolves same-named reads by lexical binding identity and initialization order; independent sibling blocks repair and converge, while reads outside all defining scopes or before initialization have no speculative repair.
- [FRONTEND-VARIANT-MIGRATION-1] Legacy variant payload migration preserves case newlines and comments and renders nested payload types canonically without overlapping edits.
- [FRONTEND-NESTED-COMPREHENSION-MIGRATION-1] Nested legacy array comprehensions migrate from inner to outer across fix iterations, preserving expressions and converging without overlapping edits.
- [FRONTEND-BYTES-PARENS-1] Byte literals preserve managed bytes identity through parentheses and inferred or contextual conditional arms, including unequal byte lengths; explicit fixed-array lengths and incompatible arm types still reject.
- [FRONTEND-NUMERIC-MIGRATION-2] A text-conversion repair may replace int or float with parse_int or parse_float only when the replacement resolves to the builtin parser. User functions, types, parameters, local bindings and import aliases with that spelling withhold that bare-name edit, including in generic bodies. An ordinary body then takes the method form instead (FRONTEND-NUMERIC-MIGRATION-3); a generic body, whose checker does not accept the str method, carries no fix and an actionable diagnostic.
